Suleimanov-Talanov Self-Focusing and the Hierarchy of the Focusing Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation
R. J. Buckingham, R. M. Jenkins, P. D. Miller

Abstract
. We study the self-focusing of wave packets from the point of view of the semiclassical focusing nonlinear Schrödinger equation. A type of finite-time collapse/blowup of the solution of the associated dispersionless limit was investigated by Talanov in the 1960’s, and recently Suleimanov identified a special solution of the dispersive problem that formally regularizes the blowup and is related to the hierarchy of the Painlevé-III equation. In this paper we approximate the Talanov solutions in the full dispersive equation using a semiclassical soliton ensemble, a sequence of exact reflectionless solutions for a corresponding sequence of values of the semiclassical parameter ϵ \epsilon tending to zero, approximating the Talanov initial data more and more accurately in the limit ϵ → 0 \epsilon\to 0 . In this setting, we rigorously establish the validity of the dispersive saturation of the Talanov blowup obtained by Suleimanov. We extend the result to the full hierarchy of higher focusing nonlinear Schrödinger equations, exhibiting new generalizations of the Talanov initial data that produce such dispersively regularized extreme focusing in both mixed and pure flows. We also argue that generic perturbations of the Talanov initial data lead to a different singularity of the dispersionless limit, namely a gradient catastrophe for which the dispersive regularization is instead based on the tritronquée solution of the Painlevé-I equation and the Peregrine breather solution which appears near points in space time corresponding to the poles of the former transcendental function as shown by Bertola and Tovbis.

1.
Introduction

The focusing nonlinear Schrödinger (NLS) equation

(1.1)

\mathrm{i}\epsilon\psi_{t_{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{2}\psi_{xx}+|\psi|^{2}\psi=0

is a standard model for time-dependent complex-valued fields in one-dimensional physical
systems exhibiting both dispersion and cubic nonlinearity. It is this
competition between dispersion and nonlinearity in the NLS equation that leads
mathematically to a host of phenomena including solitons, wave breaking and
dispersive shock waves, and rogue waves. Classically, one way to attempt to
tease out the separate effects of dispersion and nonlinearity is to first
consider the
dispersionless focusing NLS system
.
In particular, if we rewrite the focusing NLS equation (
1.1
) with

t:=t_{2}
in Madelung coordinates by introducing

\psi=\rho^{1/2}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}S/\epsilon}
for a density
\rho\geq 0
and phase

S\in\mathbb{R}
, then with the momentum defined by
\mu:=\rho S_{x}
the
equation (
1.1
) becomes exactly the coupled system

(1.2)

\rho_{t}+\mu_{x}=0,\quad\mu_{t}+\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{\rho}-\frac{\rho^{2}}{2}\right)_{x}=\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4}\left(\rho\left(\frac{\rho_{x}}{\rho}\right)_{x}\right)_{x}.

Thinking of
\epsilon
as a measure of the strength of dispersive effects,
setting
\epsilon=0
leads to the dispersionless focusing NLS system

(1.3)

\rho_{t}+\mu_{x}=0,\quad\mu_{t}+\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{\rho}-\frac{\rho^{2}}{2}\right)_{x}=0.

The reason behind the “focusing” nomenclature is easily seen by considering
the
Akhmanov-Sukhorukov-Khokhlov solution

[
1
]

of (
1.3
) with the natural initial conditions

(1.4)

\rho(x,0)=A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}\mathrm{sech}^{2}(x),\quad\mu(x,0)=0.

Here
A_{\mathrm{max}}>0
a fixed parameter. The solution is determined
implicitly by the equations

(1.5)

\mu=-2t\rho^{2}\tanh\left(x-\frac{\mu}{\rho}t\right),\quad\rho=(A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}+t^{2}\rho^{2})\mathrm{sech}^{2}\left(x-\frac{\mu}{\rho}t\right).

The equations can be solved explicitly for
x=0
, giving

(1.6)

\rho(0,t)=\frac{1-\sqrt{1-4A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}t^{2}}}{2t^{2}},\quad|t|<\frac{1}{2A_{\mathrm{max}}}.

The solution exhibits a
finite-amplitude gradient catastrophe at
(x,t)=(0,\pm 1/(2A_{\mathrm{max}}))

with value
\rho=2A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}
and cannot be continued in
any smooth way outside the indicated interval. See
Figure
2
. On the other hand, the solution to the full
NLS equation (
1.1
) with corresponding initial condition

\psi(x,0)=A_{\mathrm{max}}\mathrm{sech}(x)
is known as the

Satsuma-Yajima solution

[
26
]
, which exists for all time.
To compare to the dispersionless focusing NLS system, it makes sense to
consider the so-called
zero-dispersion
or
semiclassical limit

\epsilon\downarrow 0
. For the sequence

\{\epsilon=\frac{A_{\mathrm{max}}}{N},\ N=1,2,3,...\}
, it has been proven

[
16
]
that the Satsuma-Yajima solution indeed converges to the
Akhmanov-Sukhorukov-Khokhlov solution in the semiclassical limit for

|t|<1/(2A_{\mathrm{max}})
. However, starting at
t=1/(2A_{\mathrm{max}})
, for
small
\epsilon
the Satsuma-Yajima solution displays a marked phase transition beyond
a certain well-defined caustic curve. Across this curve the solution’s
amplitude suddenly switches from behavior that is (asymptotically)
independent of
\epsilon
to rapid oscillations of wavelength and frequency proportional
to
\epsilon

[
16
]
.

In this work we are especially interested in the solution near the first
point of wave breaking. The dispersive regularization under the full
focusing NLS dynamics of the type of singularity appearing in the
Akhmanov-Sukhorukov-Khokhlov solution (known in catastrophe theory as
an
elliptic umbilic catastrophe
) was studied by Bertola and Tovbis

[
3
]
. (For generalizations to other similar problems, see

[
14
]
and
[
19
]
). They found that in a neighborhood of
the elliptic umbilic catastrophe point
the dispersive solution behaves like a multiscale structure consisting of a
mesoscale (spacetime scales proportional to
\epsilon^{4/5}
) background profile described by the tritronquée solution of the Painlevé-I equation except near certain points corresponding to its poles that form a
curvilinear spacetime lattice; near each lattice point one has instead an
approximation on the microscale (spacetime scales proportional to
\epsilon
) by a copy of the Peregrine breather (rogue
wave) solution
[
24
]
. It is generally understood from numerical
and analytical studies that this Bertola-Tovbis regularization is, in some
sense, the “generic” breaking behavior for the focusing NLS equation.
However, it is not the only type of breaking behavior, and it is exactly such
non-generic breaking behavior that interests us here. Returning to the
dispersionless focusing NLS system (
1.3
), we turn our attention to the

Talanov solutions

[
28
]
, which were actually
discovered slightly before the Akhmanov-Sukhorukov-Khokhlov solution. Let

E\in\mathbb{R}
and
F>0
be fixed constants and choose
w(t)
to be a
solution of

(1.7)

\frac{1}{2}w^{\prime}(t)^{2}-\frac{2F}{w(t)}=E.

Then the Talanov solution is

(1.8)

\begin{split}&\rho(x,t)=Fw(t)^{-3}(w(t)^{2}-x^{2})\chi_{[-w(t),w(t)]}(x),\\
&\mu(x,t)=Fw(t)^{-4}w^{\prime}(t)x(w(t)^{2}-x^{2})\chi_{[-w(t),w(t)]}(x),\end{split}

where
\chi_{[a,b]}
is the standard indicator function on
[a,b]
. In
particular, the density
\rho(x,t)
has the form of a
cutoff parabolic profile corresponding to a semicircular amplitude profile of
(half) width
w(t)>0
and maximum value
Fw(t)^{-1}
. See Section
2.2.1

for more details. The behavior of the solution depends on
E
as follows.

•

If
E=0
, then
w(t)=(9F)^{\frac{1}{3}}(t^{\circ}-t)^{\frac{2}{3}}
for an
arbitrary integration constant
t^{\circ}
. In particular, if
F>0
then
as
t\to-\infty
the width grows without bound and the amplitude
decays to zero monotonically. On the other hand, the solution only exists
for
t<t^{\circ}
and collapses to zero width (
w(t)\downarrow 0
) and infinite
amplitude (
Fw(t)^{-1}\uparrow\infty
) as
t\uparrow t^{\circ}
.

•

If
E>0
then it is not possible to solve for
w(t)
explicitly.
However, the qualitative behavior is similar to the
E=0
case.

•

If
E<0
then the width no longer changes monotonically. In this case,
there is a unique time at which
w^{\prime}(t)=0
and
w(t)
is maximized; we choose this time to be
t=0

by choice of the integration constant. The solution now only exists on the
time interval
-t^{\circ}<t<t^{\circ}
, where

2t^{\circ}=\sqrt{2}\pi F(-E)^{-\frac{3}{2}}
. The solution
collapses to zero width and infinite amplitude as
t\downarrow-t^{\circ}
or

t\uparrow t^{\circ}
.

The case
E=0
was recently considered by Suleimanov
[
27
]
,
where he proposed that the dispersive terms in the focusing NLS equation
serve to arrest the collapse in a specific fashion related to solutions of
the third Painlevé equation and its hierarchy. For more details see Section
2.4.2
.

In this work we consider the dispersive regularization of a class of
functions we call
semicircular Klaus-Shaw potentials
(see
Section
2.3.1
) that include the Talanov solutions with
E<0
as
a special case. To take advantage of the integrable structure of the
focusing NLS equation, we use the
semiclassical soliton ensemble

approach
[
16
]
. Rather than studying a semicircular
Klaus-Shaw potential directly, we first approximate it by a sequence of
reflectionless (pure soliton) solutions that converges when
t=0
to the
desired initial condition as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
(see
Theorem
2.1
). Then, through the
formulation of a Riemann-Hilbert problem and the use of the Deift-Zhou
nonlinear steepest-descent method, we prove rigorously that the local behavior
in the semiclassical limit at the focusing point is that proposed by
Suleimanov (see Theorem
2.77
). The following table
summarizes the relationship of our results on the focusing NLS equation
to the existing literature.

Motivating Solution of the

Dispersionless NLS System

Solution of the Full NLS

Equation

Regularization

Akhmanov-Sukhorukov-Khokhlov
[
1
]

Satsuma-Yajima
[
26
]

Bertola-Tovbis
[
3
]

Talanov (
E<0
)
[
28
]

Semiclassical soliton ensemble approx. of semicircular Klaus-Shaw potentials (this work)

Suleimanov

(this work)

In addition, we extend our results to other equations in the focusing NLS
hierarchy. See Theorems
2.77
,
2.3
, and

2.4
below. We also discuss the non-genericity
of Suleimanov-Talanov focusing in Section
2.5
, where we show
that such behavior can be easily perturbed into the type of dispersive
regularization studied by Bertola and Tovbis.

1
.

Another recent study of the Talanov solution and its perturbations is the paper
[
13
]
, in which the authors
review the Talanov theory and then consider the fully dispersive NLS equation numerically with a version of the Talanov
initial data that is artificially smoothed at the corner points where the field meets the vacuum.

2
.

An interesting open question is the behavior of Talanov-type initial data for
pulses with chirp (a linear phase gradient). We will investigate the image
in the scattering transform domain of such functions in future work.

3
.

The semicircular Klaus-Shaw initial conditions we study here are
also important in a
study of the
three-wave resonant interaction equations
in the
semiclassical limit. This is a coupled system of three equations with a

3\times 3
Lax pair. It happens that if the three fields are initially
disjointly supported then the
x
-equation in the Lax pair reduces at each
x
-value to a

2\times 2
Zakharov-Shabat eigenvalue problem (tensored with a scalar).
This is the well-studied
x
-equation in the Lax pair for the NLS hierarchy.
Therefore, if the three-wave resonant interaction equations are posed with
disjointly supported initial data of the form (
2.21
) in each channel, then the
scattering data can be determined by (i) performing a local analysis on each
Zakharov-Shabat operator to determine the
exceptional points
, and
(ii) a global analysis to determine the associated
connection
coefficients

[
12
]
. It is then possible to show using existing theory
[
16
]
that the semiclassical soliton
ensemble converges to the original initial data as
\epsilon\downarrow 0

at
t=0
, provided that the amplitude of each packet vanishes to sufficiently high order at the support endpoints. One aim of this paper is to address the technical challenges in the forward-scattering
and inverse-scattering steps when the initial data vanishes at the support endpoints like a
square root.
The results of our work in this direction are described in Theorem
2.1
and Corollary
2.61
below.
In future work, we will apply Theorem
2.1
to the
three-wave semiclassical soliton ensembles defined in
[
12
]

to prove convergence at
t=0
for ensembles corresponding to certain
disjointly supported initial packets.

2.
Preliminary material and results

2.1.
The NLS hierarchy

The
m^{\mathrm{th}}
flow in the focusing NLS hierarchy—which we will generically refer to as the
\mathrm{NLS}_{m}
equation—can be written in compact form as
\epsilon\psi_{t_{m}}=N_{m}[\psi,\psi^{*}]
where
N_{m}
is a polynomial in its arguments and their scaled
x
-derivatives (
\epsilon\partial_{x}
), normalized so that the coefficient of
(\epsilon\partial_{x})^{m}\psi
(the highest derivative) is
(\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{i})^{m-1}
. The
\mathrm{NLS}_{3}
equation is better known as the complex modified Korteweg-de Vries (mKdV) equation

(2.1)

\epsilon\psi_{t_{3}}+\frac{3}{2}\epsilon|\psi|^{2}\psi_{x}+\frac{1}{4}\epsilon^{3}\psi_{xxx}=0,

while
\mathrm{NLS}_{4}
takes the form

(2.2)

\mathrm{i}\epsilon\psi_{t_{4}}-\frac{1}{8}\epsilon^{4}\psi_{xxxx}-\epsilon^{2}|\psi|^{2}\psi_{xx}-\frac{1}{4}\epsilon^{2}\psi^{2}\psi^{*}_{xx}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{2}\psi\psi_{x}\psi^{*}_{x}-\frac{3}{4}\epsilon^{2}\psi^{*}\psi_{x}^{2}-\frac{3}{4}|\psi|^{4}\psi=0.

With index omitted NLS will always refer to (
1.1
), the
\mathrm{NLS}_{2}
equation.

It is well known that all of the equations in the focusing NLS hierarchy can be simultaneously solved, that is, there is a well-defined function
\psi(x,t_{2},t_{3},\dots,t_{M})
with suitable given initial condition
\psi(x,0,0,\dots,0)=\psi_{0}(x)
such that
\epsilon\psi_{t_{m}}=N_{m}[\psi,\psi^{*}]
holds for each
m=2,3,\dots,M
. By restricting the times to be proportional by given constants to a single independent variable
t
, i.e.
t_{m}=a_{m}t
, we see that as a function of
(x,t)
,
\psi
satisfies a mixture of the flows:
\epsilon\psi_{t}=a_{2}N_{2}[\psi,\psi^{*}]+a_{3}N_{3}[\psi,\psi^{*}]+\cdots+a_{M}N_{M}[\psi,\psi^{*}]
. Some of these mixtures have their own names in the literature. For instance, the combination
\epsilon\psi_{t}=a_{2}N_{2}[\psi,\psi^{*}]+a_{3}N_{3}[\psi,\psi^{*}]
is frequently called the Hirota equation and is written in the form

(2.3)

\mathrm{i}\epsilon\psi_{t}+a_{2}\left[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{2}\psi_{xx}+|\psi|^{2}\psi\right]+a_{3}\left[\mathrm{i}\frac{3}{2}\epsilon|\psi|^{2}\psi_{x}+\mathrm{i}\frac{1}{4}\epsilon^{3}\psi_{xxx}\right]=0.

Similarly, the mixture
\epsilon\psi_{t}=a_{2}N_{2}[\psi,\psi^{*}]+a_{4}N_{4}[\psi,\psi^{*}]
yields the Lakshmanan-Porsezian-Daniel (LPD) equation

(2.4)

\mathrm{i}\epsilon\psi_{t}+a_{2}\left[\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{2}\psi_{xx}+|\psi|^{2}\psi\right]\\
+a_{4}\left[-\frac{1}{8}\epsilon^{4}\psi_{xxxx}-\epsilon^{2}|\psi|^{2}\psi_{xx}-\frac{1}{4}\epsilon^{2}\psi^{2}\psi^{*}_{xx}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{2}\psi\psi_{x}\psi^{*}_{x}-\frac{3}{4}\epsilon^{2}\psi^{*}\psi_{x}^{2}-\frac{3}{4}|\psi|^{4}\psi\right]=0.

Like (
2.1
) and (
2.2
), these equations can be viewed as models for ultrashort pulses propagating in optical fibers.

2.2.
Singularities of solutions of dispersionless focusing NLS

2.2.1.
The Talanov solutions

Following Talanov
[
28
]
(see also
[
13
, Section II]
for a recent review), we seek a solution of the dispersionless system (
1.3
) for which
the density (squared amplitude)
\rho(x,t)
has the form of a cutoff parabolic profile corresponding to a semicircular amplitude profile of width
w(t)>0

and maximum value
f(t)w(t)^{2}>0
:

(2.5)

\rho(x,t)=f(t)(w(t)^{2}-x^{2})\chi(x,t),\quad\chi(x,t):=\chi_{[-w(t),w(t)]}(x).

Since the conservation law on
\rho
in the system (
1.3
) implies that the integral over
x
of
\rho
is conserved, we compute:

(2.6)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\rho(x,t)\,\mathrm{d}x=f(t)\int_{-w(t)}^{w(t)}(w(t)^{2}-x^{2})\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{4}{3}f(t)w(t)^{3}

so for conservation we require that
f(t)=Fw(t)^{-3}
for some constant
F>0
. Thus we have

(2.7)

\rho(x,t)=Fw(t)^{-3}(w(t)^{2}-x^{2})\chi(x,t).

From this formula, we see that

(2.8)

\rho_{t}(x,t)=-Fw(t)^{-4}w^{\prime}(t)(w(t)^{2}-3x^{2})\chi(x,t),\quad|x|\neq w(t)

(note that
\rho_{t}
has jump discontinuities at
|x|=w(t)
).
Then, using again
\rho_{t}+\mu_{x}=0
we can find
\mu(x,t)
by integration in
x
of
-\rho_{t}
:

(2.9)

\mu(x,t)=Fw(t)^{-4}w^{\prime}(t)x(w(t)^{2}-x^{2})\chi(x,t).

Note that this is the unique antiderivative of
-\rho_{t}
that decays to zero as
x\to\pm\infty
for all
t
.
On the support interval
-w(t)<x<w(t)
it makes sense to calculate the phase derivative

(2.10)

S_{x}(x,t)=\frac{\mu(x,t)}{\rho(x,t)}=\frac{w^{\prime}(t)}{w(t)}x,\quad-w(t)<x<w(t),

which shows that at time instants where
w^{\prime}(t)\neq 0
, the phase profile is quadratic as a function of
x
(called a
phase chirp
in the physical literature).

Now we turn to the equation governing the momentum
\mu
in (
1.3
) to see how it determines
w(t)
. After substituting for
\rho
and
\mu
from (
2.7
) and (
2.9
) respectively, this equation has terms proportional to both
x
and
x^{3}
and no other dependence on
x
within the support of
\chi(x,t)
. An apparent coincidence that ultimately lies behind the fact that the ansatz (
2.5
) is consistent with the dispersionless NLS system is that equating the coefficients of
x
and of
x^{3}
separately amounts to exactly the same equation on
w(t)
, namely

(2.11)

w^{\prime\prime}(t)+\frac{2F}{w(t)^{2}}=0.

This autonomous second-order nonlinear differential equation for
w(t)
can be multiplied by
w^{\prime}(t)
and integrated once to yield

(2.12)

\frac{1}{2}w^{\prime}(t)^{2}-\frac{2F}{w(t)}=E

where
E
is another integration constant.

The case
E=0
corresponds to the self-similar collapse considered by Suleimanov
[
27
]
. Assuming
w^{\prime}(t)<0
(corresponding to a collapse instead of an expansion), we get for
E=0
:

(2.13)

w(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}w^{\prime}(t)=-2\sqrt{F}\implies\frac{2}{3}w(t)^{\frac{3}{2}}=2\sqrt{F}(t^{\circ}-t)\implies w(t)=(9F)^{\frac{1}{3}}(t^{\circ}-t)^{\frac{2}{3}}

where
t^{\circ}\in\mathbb{R}
is a third integration constant with the interpretation of the focusing time, and the solution is defined only for
t<t^{\circ}
.
The solution collapses to zero width (
w(t)\downarrow 0
) and infinite
amplitude (
f(t)w(t)^{2}=Fw(t)^{-1}\uparrow\infty
) as
t\uparrow t^{\circ}
.
However, as
t\to-\infty
the width grows without bound and the amplitude
decays to zero monotonically.
If instead we assume that
w^{\prime}(t)>0
, we get the same solution with
t^{\circ}-t
replaced by
t-t^{\circ}
and the solution exists for
t>t^{\circ}
evolving from a collapsed state to a spreading and decaying state as
t\to+\infty
. Either way, the monotonicity of
w(t)
implies that when
E=0
, the phase derivative
x\mapsto S_{x}(x,t)
given by (
2.10
) is nonconstant for every time
t<t^{\circ}
.

If
E\neq 0
, it is no longer possible to solve (
2.12
) explicitly for
w(t)
, although one can explicitly find the inverse function by integration. To analyze this more general case, it is convenient to rescale the variables. Let
t=\sqrt{2}F|E|^{-\frac{3}{2}}T
and
w=2F|E|^{-1}W
. Then (
2.12
) implies an equivalent equation on
W(T)
provided that
E\neq 0
:

(2.14)

W^{\prime}(T)^{2}-\frac{1}{W(T)}=\mathrm{sgn}(E)=\pm 1.

Assuming that
W(T)>0
, in the case
E>0
we easily see that
W^{\prime}(T)^{2}\geq 1
so
W(T)
is strictly monotone and, just as for the
E=0
case, the map
x\mapsto S_{x}(x,t)
is nonconstant for every
t
.

Now assume that
E<0
. In this case, it is obvious from a phase portrait (see the left-hand panel of Figure
1
) that
W^{\prime}(T)
changes sign at exactly one point, which we may take without loss of generality to be
T=0
.

Figure 1.
Left: the phase portrait for (
2.14
) in the part of the
(W,W^{\prime})
plane with
W>0
. Gray:
E>0
. Black:
E<0
. Compare with
[
28
, Fig. 3(b)]
. Right: the inverse function
\pm T(W)
in the case
E<0
.

The orbit of (
2.14
) for
E<0
is traversed in the downward direction in finite time
\Delta T
given by (integrating
|\mathrm{d}T/\mathrm{d}W|
over half of the orbit and doubling the result):

(2.15)

\Delta T=2\int_{0}^{1}\left(\frac{1}{W}-1\right)^{-1/2}\,\mathrm{d}W=\pi.

The corresponding duration of the solution in the
t
-variable is

(2.16)

\Delta t=\sqrt{2}F(-E)^{-\frac{3}{2}}\Delta T=\frac{\sqrt{2}\pi F}{(-E)^{\frac{3}{2}}},\quad E<0,\quad F>0.

The function
W(T)
is well-defined for
E<0
from (
2.14
) with the initial condition
W(0)=1
, and
W(T)\downarrow 0
as
|T|\uparrow\tfrac{1}{2}\pi
. We can give an explicit formula for its inverse function for
T>0
as a function of
W\in(0,1)
:

(2.17)

T=\int_{W}^{1}\left(\frac{1}{y}-1\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}y=\sqrt{(1-W)W}+\frac{\pi}{4}+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Arctan}\left(\frac{1-2W}{2\sqrt{(1-W)W}}\right)

which is plotted in the right-hand panel of Figure
1
.
When
t=0
, we have
T=0
and
W=1
. Therefore the support of
\chi(x,0)
and hence also the initial support of
\psi(x,t)
is
|x|\leq w=2F(-E)^{-1}
. The maximum amplitude
A_{\mathrm{max}}:=\max_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\psi(x,0)|
is
A_{\mathrm{max}}=\sqrt{Fw^{-1}}=\sqrt{-E/2}
. Therefore, the value of
E<0
is determined directly from
A_{\mathrm{max}}
by

(2.18)

E:=-2A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}<0

and then the value of
F>0
is determined directly from
E
and the distance between the support endpoints by

(2.19)

F:=A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}w(0).

Hence the duration of the solution becomes

(2.20)

\Delta t=\frac{\pi w(0)}{2A_{\mathrm{max}}}.

The Talanov solutions were rediscovered in the setting of shallow water equations by Ovsjannikov
[
23
]
, where it was shown that a similar approach also applies for the defocusing version of the dispersionless NLS equation. The latter is obtained from (
1.3
) by replacing
-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{2}
with
\frac{1}{2}\rho^{2}
in the second equation, making the system equivalent to a shallow water model.

Figure 2.
Left: the amplitude
\rho^{1/2}
for the
E<0
Talanov solution (
1.8
) of (
1.3
) with initial width
w(0)=2
and amplitude
A_{\mathrm{max}}=1
, exhibiting the infinite-amplitude collapse and blowup in finite time (at
(x,t)=(0,\pi/2)
). Right: the amplitude
\rho^{1/2}
for the Akhmanov-Sukhorukov-Khokhlov solution (
1.5
) of the same system with
A_{\mathrm{max}}=1
, exhibiting instead a finite-amplitude gradient catastrophe at
(x,t)=(0,1/2)
with finite value
\rho^{1/2}=\sqrt{2}
.

2.3.
Semiclassical soliton ensembles for initial data generalizing Talanov profiles

We consider initial data
\psi_{0}(x)
of the form

(2.21)

\psi_{0}(x)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}A(x)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\kappa x/\epsilon},

where
\theta
and
\kappa
are real constants. By gauge invariance
\psi\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\theta}\psi
, Galilean invariance,
and mixing the flows,
we can and will assume without loss of generality that
\theta=\kappa=0
.

2.3.1.
Semiclassical direct scattering for semicircular Klaus-Shaw potentials

We further assume that
A:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a real-valued function independent of
\epsilon>0
that we call a
semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential
. Such potentials are intended to generalize the initial data for Talanov-type solutions of the dispersionless focusing NLS system (
1.3
) in the
E<0
case. They are defined as follows.

1
Semicircular Klaus-Shaw potentials.

Let
X_{-}<X_{+}
and
c>0
be real constants. A function
A:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is called a
semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential
if

•

A
has compact support
\mathrm{spt}(A)=[X_{-},X_{+}]

on which it can be written in the form
A(x)=u(x)\sqrt{(X_{+}-x)(x-X_{-})}
, where
u(x)\geq c
on
[X_{-},X_{+}]
and
u
has an analytic continuation to a complex neighborhood of
[X_{-},X_{+}]\subset\mathbb{C}
; and

•

A
has the Klaus-Shaw property:
A:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is of class
L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and
A
has a unique maximizer
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
.

•

The generic condition
A^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})<0
holds at the maximizer.

We denote the maximum value of
A(x)
by
A_{\mathrm{max}}:=A(x_{0})>0
.

Of course if
u(x)\equiv c\geq 0
, then the graph of
A(x)
is a scaled semicircle that can be written in the form

(2.22)

A(x)=\frac{2A_{\mathrm{max}}\chi_{[X_{-},X_{+}]}(x)}{X_{+}-X_{-}}\sqrt{(X_{+}-x)(x-X_{-})},\quad x_{0}=\frac{1}{2}(X_{+}+X_{-}),

hence the name. After an irrelevant translation by
x_{0}
to recenter
A(x)
at the origin, this also matches the initial condition of a Talanov-type solution of the dispersionless NLS system with parameters
E=-2A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}<0
and
F=\frac{1}{2}(X_{+}-X_{-})A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}>0
.

By inverse-spectral theory
[
29
]
, the solution of any equation in the focusing NLS hierarchy with initial data
\psi_{0}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
is based on the spectral analysis of the non-selfadjoint Zakharov-Shabat problem:

(2.23)

\epsilon\frac{\mathrm{d}{\bf w}}{\mathrm{d}x}=\begin{pmatrix}-\mathrm{i}\lambda&\psi_{0}(x)\\
-\psi_{0}(x)^{*}&\mathrm{i}\lambda\end{pmatrix}{\bf w},\quad{\bf w}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}.

If
\psi_{0}(x)=A(x)
with
A:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
being a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential, then according to
[
17
]
, the number of eigenvalues is
finite, and all eigenvalues are simple and lie on the imaginary axis. We
choose

(2.24)

\epsilon=\epsilon_{N}:=\frac{1}{N\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}A(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

for some nonnegative integer
N
. This ensures there are exactly

N
strictly positive (and simple) imaginary eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}=\mathrm{i}s_{n}
,

0<s_{N-1}<\cdots<s_{1}<s_{0}
. When
\lambda=\lambda_{n}
, the unique solutions
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}_{n}^{\pm}(x)
of (
2.23
) given by
\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}^{-}_{n}(x)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}x/\epsilon}(1,0)^{\top}
for
x\leq X_{-}
and
\mathbf{w}^{+}_{n}(x)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}x/\epsilon}(0,1)^{\top}
for
x\geq X_{+}
are necessarily proportional; there exists a nonzero connection coefficient
\tau_{n}\neq 0
such that
\mathbf{w}_{n}^{-}(x)=\tau_{n}\mathbf{w}_{n}^{+}(x)
. Together with the reflection coefficient that may be defined for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
(its precise definition is not relevant in this paper), this comprises the scattering data associated with
\psi_{0}
.

Considering the limit
N\to\infty
equivalent to
\epsilon\to 0
, we apply the WKB method to (
2.23
) in order to approximate the scattering data for
\psi_{0}(x)=A(x)
a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential. One finds that the reflection coefficient tends to zero as
\epsilon\to 0
and obtains
approximate eigenvalues and connection coefficients defined as follows.

•

Let
\varrho(s)
be defined by

(2.25)

\begin{split}\varrho(s):=&\frac{s}{\pi}\int_{x_{-}(s)}^{x_{+}(s)}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}}\\
=&-\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\int_{x_{-}(s)}^{x_{+}(s)}\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}},\end{split}

where
x_{-}(s)<x_{+}(s)
are the two roots
x
of
A(x)^{2}-s^{2}
, in other words they are the two branches of the inverse function to
s=A(x)
defined for
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
.
Then, the
approximate eigenvalues
in the upper half-plane are
\lambda=\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}
,
n=0,\dots,N-1
, where
\widetilde{s}_{0},\dots,\widetilde{s}_{N-1}
are determined uniquely by the Bohr-Sommerfeld quantization rule

(2.26)

\Phi(\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n})=(n+\tfrac{1}{2})\epsilon\pi=\frac{2n+1}{2N}\int_{\mathbb{R}}A(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad n=0,\dots,N-1

with the
phase integral

\Phi
being defined by

(2.27)

\Phi(\mathrm{i}s):=\pi\int_{s}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}}\varrho(s^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}=\int_{x_{-}(s)}^{x_{+}(s)}\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}.

Note that
s\mapsto\Phi(\mathrm{i}s)
is real-valued and strictly decreasing on its interval
(0,A_{\mathrm{max}})
of definition, and
\Phi(\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}})=0
, implying that the definition (
2.26
) is sensible.

•

The connection coefficient
\tau_{n}
associated with the eigenvalue best approximated by
\lambda=\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}
is itself approximated by

(2.28)

\tau_{n}\approx\widetilde{\tau}_{n}:=(-1)^{n+1}\mathrm{e}^{\Xi(\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n})/\epsilon}

where the
tail integral

\Xi
is defined by

(2.29)

\Xi(\mathrm{i}s):=(x_{+}(s)+x_{-}(s))s+\int_{-\infty}^{x_{-}(s)}\left(\sqrt{s^{2}-A(x)^{2}}-s\right)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
-\int_{x_{+}(s)}^{+\infty}\left(\sqrt{s^{2}-A(x)^{2}}-s\right)\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}.

This is also real-valued on its interval of definition. Observe that, using the quantization rule (
2.26
), the approximate connection coefficients defined in (
2.28
) can equivalently be expressed as

(2.30)

\widetilde{\tau}_{n}=\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(2K+1)\Phi(\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n})/\epsilon}\mathrm{e}^{\Xi(\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n})/\epsilon},\quad K\in\mathbb{Z},

for any arbitrary integer
K
. The freedom to select
K\in\mathbb{Z}
is essential to our subsequent analysis.

The functions
\Phi(\lambda)
and
\Xi(\lambda)
have the following properties.

1
.

Let
A
be a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential. Then the phase integral
\Phi(\lambda)
defined by (
2.27
)
is positive for
s=-\mathrm{i}\lambda\in(0,A_{\mathrm{max}})
with
\Phi(\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}})=0
. Also,
\Phi(\lambda)
is an even analytic function at
\lambda=0
, and in particular the following series is convergent:

(2.31)

\Phi(\lambda)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Phi_{k}\lambda^{2k},

with real coefficients
\Phi_{k}
, and the strict inequalities
\Phi_{0}>0
and
\Phi_{1}>0
both hold.

Proof.

That
\Phi(\mathrm{i}s)>0
for
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
and that
\Phi(\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}})=0
are direct consequences of the definition (
2.27
).
If
A
is a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential, then for
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
the phase integral (
2.27
) can be written as a contour integral:

(2.32)

\Phi(\mathrm{i}s)=\frac{1}{2}\oint_{L}R(x;s^{2})\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}},

where
R(x;s^{2})^{2}=A(x)^{2}-s^{2}=u(x)^{2}(X_{+}-x)(x-X_{-})-s^{2}
and
R(x;s^{2})
is analytic in a deleted neighborhood of
[x_{-}(s),x_{+}(s)]
with positive (resp., negative) boundary values on the bottom (resp., top) edge of the cut
[x_{-}(s),x_{+}(s)]
, and where
L
is a positively-oriented loop surrounding the cut. Since
u
is analytic in a complex neighborhood of the support interval
[X_{-},X_{+}]
containing the cut
[x_{-}(s),x_{+}(s)]
, we can expand the loop
L
to enclose the larger interval
[X_{-},X_{+}]
without changing the value of the integral. Then as
L
is independent of
s
, even in a neighborhood of
s=0
, it is clear that the integral is an analytic function of
s^{2}
near
s=0
. Indeed, writing the integrand in the form
R(x;s^{2})=R(x;0)(1-s^{2}/R(x;0)^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
, where the square root factor converges uniformly to
1
as
s\to 0
for
x\in L
, we may expand the integrand in a series of even powers of
s
and integrate term-by-term:

(2.33)

\Phi(\mathrm{i}s)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}s^{2k}(-1)^{k}\binom{1/2}{k}\oint_{L}R(x;0)^{1-2k}\,\mathrm{d}x

for
|s|
sufficiently small. Using
\lambda=\mathrm{i}s
then proves the claimed series expansion. The reality of the coefficients
\Phi_{k}
comes from Schwarz symmetry of the integrand and integration contour. If
k=0,1
, the integrand is integrable at
x=X_{\pm}
and by the generalized Cauchy integral theorem we obtain

(2.34)

\begin{split}\Phi_{0}&=\binom{1/2}{0}\int_{X_{-}}^{X_{+}}u(x)\sqrt{(X_{+}-x)(x-X_{-})}\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{X_{-}}^{X_{+}}A(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,\\
\Phi_{1}&=\binom{1/2}{1}\int_{X_{-}}^{X_{+}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{u(x)\sqrt{(X_{+}-x)(x-X_{-})}},\end{split}

both of which are obviously positive.
∎

2
.

Let
A
be a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential. Then the tail integral
\Xi(\lambda)
defined by (
2.29
) is an odd analytic function at
\lambda=0
, and in particular the following series is convergent:

(2.35)

\Xi(\lambda)=\mathrm{i}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\Xi_{k}\lambda^{2k-1},

with real coefficients
\Xi_{k}
, and
\Xi_{1}=-(X_{+}+X_{-})
.

Proof.

Since
A
has compact support in
[X_{-},X_{+}]
, for
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
we can rewrite (
2.29
) in the form

\displaystyle\Xi(\mathrm{i}s)

\displaystyle=(x_{+}(s)+x_{-}(s))s+\int_{X_{-}}^{x_{-}(s)}\left(\sqrt{s^{2}-A(x)^{2}}-s\right)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{x_{+}(s)}^{X_{+}}\left(\sqrt{s^{2}-A(x)^{2}}-s\right)\,\mathrm{d}x

(2.36)

\displaystyle=(X_{+}+X_{-})s+\int_{X_{-}}^{x_{-}(s)}\sqrt{s^{2}-A(x)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{x_{+}(s)}^{X_{+}}\sqrt{s^{2}-A(x)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Each integral on the right-hand side makes a similar contribution of order
\mathcal{O}\!\left(s^{3}\right)
, so we just consider the first integral in detail. Making the change of variables
x=X_{-}+s^{2}w
gives

(2.37)

\int_{X_{-}}^{x_{-}(s)}\sqrt{s^{2}-A(x)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x=s^{3}\int_{0}^{(x_{-}(s)-X_{-})/s^{2}}\sqrt{1-u(X_{-}+s^{2}w)^{2}(X_{+}-X_{-}-s^{2}w)w}\,\mathrm{d}w.

The upper limit of integration is the positive value
w_{-}(s)
of
w
that makes the integrand vanish and that satisfies
w_{-}(s)=1/[u(X_{-})^{2}(X_{+}-X_{-})]+\mathcal{O}\!\left(s^{2}\right)
. We introduce a function
R(w;s^{2})
with a branch cut extending from
w=w_{-}(s)
to the left through
w=0
and satisfying
R(w;s^{2})^{2}=1-u(X_{-}+s^{2}w)^{2}(X_{+}-X_{-}-s^{2}w)w
with
R(w;s^{2})>0
(resp.,
R(w;s^{2})<0
) on the bottom edge (resp., top edge) of the cut, and then let
L
denote a teardrop-shaped contour from
w=0
on the bottom edge to
w=0
on the top edge and encircling the branch cut between
w=0
and the branch point
w=w_{-}(s)
once in the counterclockwise sense. We take
L
to be independent of
s
for
s>0
sufficiently small. Then we have

(2.38)

\int_{X_{-}}^{x_{-}(s)}\sqrt{s^{2}-A(x)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2}s^{3}\int_{L}R(w;s^{2})\,\mathrm{d}w.

Now,
R(w;s^{2})
has a Taylor expansion about
s=0
in even powers of
s
, and this series is uniformly convergent for
x\in L
, so we may integrate term-by-term. Thus,

(2.39)

\int_{X_{-}}^{x_{-}(s)}\sqrt{s^{2}-A(x)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2}s^{3}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{s^{2n}}{n!}\int_{L}\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}R}{\mathrm{d}\sigma^{n}}(w;\sigma)\right|_{\sigma=0}\,\mathrm{d}w.

It is easy to check that each Taylor coefficient is an integral that evaluates to a real number by Schwarz symmetry. Since the second integral in (
2.36
) has a similar expansion, using
\lambda=\mathrm{i}s
completes the proof.
∎

Finally, we note that a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential
A
can be recovered from the corresponding phase integral
\Phi
and tail integral
\Xi
.

3
.

Let
A
be a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential, and let
\Phi(\lambda)
and
\Xi(\lambda)
be defined by (
2.27
) and (
2.29
), respectively. Then, for
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
, the inverse functions
x_{-}(s)<x_{+}(s)
of
x\mapsto A(x)
satisfy

(2.40)

x_{\pm}(s)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}m}\Xi(\mathrm{i}m)\,\mathrm{d}m}{\sqrt{s^{2}-m^{2}}}\mp\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{s}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}}\frac{\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}m}\Phi(\mathrm{i}m)\,\mathrm{d}m}{\sqrt{m^{2}-s^{2}}}.

Proof.

Directly from the definition of
\Phi
in (
2.27
),
we can compute

(2.41)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}m}\Phi(\mathrm{i}m)=-m\int_{x_{-}(m)}^{x_{+}(m)}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-m^{2}}}.

Therefore, integrating and changing the integration variable from
m
to
v=m^{2}
,

(2.42)

\int_{s}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}}\frac{\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}m}\Phi(\mathrm{i}m)\,\mathrm{d}m}{\sqrt{m^{2}-s^{2}}}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{s^{2}}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}\int_{x_{-}(\sqrt{v})}^{x_{+}(\sqrt{v})}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-v}}\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{\sqrt{v-s^{2}}}.

Exchanging the order of integration,

(2.43)

\int_{s}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}}\frac{\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}m}\Phi(\mathrm{i}m)\,\mathrm{d}m}{\sqrt{m^{2}-s^{2}}}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{x_{-}(s)}^{x_{+}(s)}\int_{s^{2}}^{A(x)^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-v}\sqrt{v-s^{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}x.

By an affine transformation taking the integration endpoints
v=s^{2}
and
v=A(x)^{2}
to
\pm 1
, one sees that the inner integral over
v
is actually independent of
s^{2}
and
A(x)^{2}
, and evaluates to
\pi
.
Therefore by evaluating the outer integral we obtain

(2.44)

\int_{s}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}}\frac{\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}m}\Phi(\mathrm{i}m)\,\mathrm{d}m}{\sqrt{m^{2}-s^{2}}}=-\frac{\pi}{2}(x_{+}(s)-x_{-}(s)).

Similarly, since for
A(x)
a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential we can express
\Xi
using (
2.36
),
we take a derivative to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}m}\Xi(\mathrm{i}m)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}m}\left[(X_{+}+X_{-})m+\int_{X_{-}}^{x_{-}(m)}\sqrt{m^{2}-A(x)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{x_{+}(m)}^{X_{+}}\sqrt{m^{2}-A(x)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\right]

(2.45)

\displaystyle=X_{+}+X_{-}+m\int_{X_{-}}^{x_{-}(m)}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\sqrt{m^{2}-A(x)^{2}}}-m\int_{x_{+}(m)}^{X_{+}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\sqrt{m^{2}-A(x)^{2}}}.

So, integrating and changing the integration variable from
m
to
v=m^{2}
,

(2.46)

\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}m}\Xi(\mathrm{i}m)\,\mathrm{d}m}{\sqrt{s^{2}-m^{2}}}=(X_{+}+X_{-})\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\mathrm{d}m}{\sqrt{s^{2}-m^{2}}}\\
{}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{s^{2}}\int_{X_{-}}^{x_{-}(\sqrt{v})}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\sqrt{v-A(x)^{2}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{\sqrt{s^{2}-v}}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{s^{2}}\int_{x_{+}(\sqrt{v})}^{X_{+}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\sqrt{v-A(x)^{2}}}\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{\sqrt{s^{2}-v}}.

The integral on the first line evaluates to
\pi/2
independent of
m
, and on the second line we exchange the order of integration in each integral to obtain

(2.47)

\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}m}\Xi(\mathrm{i}m)\,\mathrm{d}m}{\sqrt{s^{2}-m^{2}}}=\frac{\pi}{2}(X_{+}+X_{-})\\
+\frac{1}{2}\int_{X_{-}}^{x_{-}(s)}\int_{A(x)^{2}}^{s^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{\sqrt{v-A(x)^{2}}\sqrt{s^{2}-v}}\,\mathrm{d}x-\frac{1}{2}\int_{x_{+}(s)}^{X_{+}}\int_{A(x)^{2}}^{s^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}v}{\sqrt{v-A(x)^{2}}\sqrt{s^{2}-v}}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Again the inner integral evaluates to
\pi
in each case, so we obtain simply

(2.48)

\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}m}\Xi(\mathrm{i}m)\,\mathrm{d}m}{\sqrt{s^{2}-m^{2}}}=\frac{\pi}{2}(x_{+}(s)+x_{-}(s)).

Combining (
2.44
) and (
2.48
) yields (
2.40
).
∎

1
.

A semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential
A
is even about
x=\frac{1}{2}(X_{+}+X_{-})
if and only if
\Xi(\lambda)=-\mathrm{i}(X_{+}+X_{-})\lambda
.

Proof.

If
A
is a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential for which
A(\frac{1}{2}(X_{+}+X_{-})+y)
is an even function of
y
, then
x_{-}(s)-\frac{1}{2}(X_{+}+X_{-})=-(x_{+}(s)-\frac{1}{2}(X_{+}+X_{-}))
holds identically for
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
, and it then follows from (
2.36
) that
\Xi(\mathrm{i}s)=(X_{+}+X_{-})s
. On the other hand, if
\Xi(\mathrm{i}s)=(X_{+}+X_{-})s
, then it follows from (
2.40
) that
x_{-}(s)-\frac{1}{2}(X_{+}+X_{-})=-(x_{+}(s)-\frac{1}{2}(X_{+}+X_{-}))
, which implies that
A
is even about
x=\frac{1}{2}(X_{+}+X_{-})
.
∎

1
.

Proposition
2.40
and Corollary
1
are also valid for more general Klaus-Shaw potentials. However Propositions
1
and
2
require the more restrictive properties of semicircular Klaus-Shaw potentials.

2.3.2.
Semiclassical soliton ensembles for semicircular Klaus-Shaw potentials

We now discard the original initial data
\psi_{0}(x)=A(x)
and replace it with the initial
data corresponding to the pure-soliton solution with discrete spectral data defined in
(
2.25
)–(
2.29
). This is the semiclassical
soliton ensemble associated to the initial condition
A(x)
. Here is the precise definition.

2
Semiclassical soliton ensemble.

Let
A
be a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential and let
N>0
be an integer. The
semiclassical soliton ensemble
associated with the initial condition
\psi_{0}(x)=A(x)
and the index
N
is the exact solution
\widetilde{\psi}(x,t_{2},t_{3},\dots,t_{M})
of the first
M-1
flows in the focusing NLS hierarchy for parameter
\epsilon=\epsilon_{N}
given by (
2.24
) with initial condition
\widetilde{\psi}(x,0,0,\dots,0)
that is reflectionless (reflection coefficient vanishing identically) and has eigenvalues
\lambda=\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}
defined by (
2.26
)–(
2.27
) with corresponding connection coefficients
\widetilde{\tau}_{n}
defined by (
2.28
)–(
2.29
). Given real constants
a_{2},a_{3},\dots,a_{M}
, the semiclassical soliton ensemble for the mixture of the flows corresponding to these constants is the function
\widetilde{\psi}(x,t):=\widetilde{\psi}(x,a_{2}t,a_{3}t,\dots,a_{M}t)
; it is an exact solution of the prescribed mixture of the flows.

We introduce the compact notation
\mathbf{t}=(t_{2},t_{3},\dots,t_{M})\in\mathbb{R}^{M-1}
to denote the vector of time coordinates, and write
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{t})=\widetilde{\psi}(x,t_{2},t_{3},\dots,t_{M})
.
Because
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{t})
is a reflectionless solution of the focusing NLS hierarchy, it can be characterized in terms of the solution of a Riemann-Hilbert problem with purely discrete data. To formulate this problem, first define the
set of poles in
\mathbb{C}_{+}
by

(2.49)

P:=\{\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}\}_{n=0}^{N-1}.

1
Semiclassical soliton ensemble for the focusing NLS hierarchy.

Given
\epsilon>0
and values of the independent variables
(x,t_{2},t_{3},\dots,t_{M})\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
, seek a
2\times 2
matrix function
\widetilde{\bf M}(\lambda)=\widetilde{\bf M}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
with the following properties:

Meromorphicity:

\widetilde{\bf M}(\lambda)
is
analytic for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(P\cup P^{*})
, with simple poles
in
P\cup P^{*}
.

Residues:

We have the residue conditions

(2.50)

\begin{split}\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{\lambda=\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}}\widetilde{\bf M}(\lambda)=\lim_{\lambda\to\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}}\widetilde{\bf M}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
c_{n}(x,\mathbf{t})&0\end{pmatrix},\quad n=0,\dots,N-1,\end{split}

and

(2.51)

\begin{split}\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{\lambda=-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}}\widetilde{\bf M}(\lambda)=\lim_{\lambda\to-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}}\widetilde{\bf M}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}0&-c_{n}(x,\mathbf{t})^{*}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad n=0,\dots,N-1,\end{split}

where

(2.52)

c_{n}(x,\mathbf{t}):=c_{n}^{0}\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}Q(\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n};x,\mathbf{t})/\epsilon},

with

(2.53)

c_{n}^{0}:=\widetilde{\tau}_{n}\frac{\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}\left(\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}+\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{j}\right)}{\prod_{j=0,j\neq n}^{N-1}\left(\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{j}\right)},\quad Q(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t}):=\lambda x+\sum_{m=2}^{M}\lambda^{m}t_{m}.

Normalization:

\widetilde{\bf M}(\lambda)\to\mathbb{I}
as
\lambda\to\infty
.

It can be shown that for each
\epsilon>0
, this problem has a unique solution that is defined and real analytic on the parameter space
(x,t_{2},t_{3},\dots,t_{M})\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
. Indeed, one can see this by following the standard method of removing the poles in favor of jumps across small circles surrounding each of them (see for instance
[
10
]
), and doing so in a way that preserves Schwarz symmetry of the conditions as one uses to prove Proposition
4
below. Then one applies Zhou’s vanishing lemma
[
30
]
.

An equivalent Riemann-Hilbert problem useful in some situations arises from the transformation

(2.54)

\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t}):=\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}\left(\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{j}}{\lambda+\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{j}}\right)^{\sigma_{3}}.

Clearly, this modified matrix is also meromorphic with simple poles only in
P\cup P^{*}
, and it tends to the identity as
\lambda\to\infty
.
The main effect of (
2.54
) is to move the simple poles from the first column to the second and vice-versa. Therefore, (
2.50
)–(
2.51
) imply that

\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
satisfies the conditions of the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

2
Renormalized soliton ensemble for the focusing NLS hierarchy.

Given
\epsilon>0
and values of the independent variables
(x,t_{2},t_{3},\dots,t_{M})\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
, seek a
2\times 2
matrix function
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda)=\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
with the following properties:

Meromorphicity:

\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda)
is
analytic for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(P\cup P^{*})
, with simple poles
in
P\cup P^{*}
.

Residues:

We have the residue conditions

(2.55)

\begin{split}\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{\lambda=\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}}\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda)=\lim_{\lambda\to\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}}\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}0&c^{\updownarrow}_{n}(x,\mathbf{t})\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad n=0,\dots,N-1,\end{split}

and

(2.56)

\begin{split}\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{\lambda=-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}}\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda)=\lim_{\lambda\to-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}}\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda)\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
-c^{\updownarrow}_{n}(x,\mathbf{t})^{*}&0\end{pmatrix},\quad n=0,\dots,N-1,\end{split}

where

(2.57)

c^{\updownarrow}_{n}(x,\mathbf{t}):=\frac{1}{c_{n}(x,\mathbf{t})}\frac{\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}(\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}+\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{j})^{2}}{\prod_{j=0,j\neq n}^{N-1}(\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{j})^{2}},\quad n=0,\dots,N-1.

Normalization:

\mathbf{M}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda)\to\mathbb{I}
as
\lambda\to\infty
.

Once the unique solution of either Riemann-Hilbert Problem
1
or
2
is known, the semiclassical soliton ensemble corresponding to the semicircular Klaus-Shaw initial data
\psi_{0}(x)=A(x)
is the exact solution to
the focusing NLS hierarchy given by

(2.58)

\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{t}):=2\mathrm{i}\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda\widetilde{M}_{12}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})=2\mathrm{i}\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda\widetilde{M}^{\updownarrow}_{12}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t}).

Indeed, it is a consequence of (
2.54
) that the two formulæ in (
2.58
) are consistent. Then the fact that
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{t})
solves the first
M-1
equations in the focusing NLS hierarchy follows by a standard dressing calculation that proceeds as follows. First, one checks that the substitution
\mathbf{L}(\lambda)=\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}Q(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\sigma_{3}}
yields residue conditions for
\mathbf{L}(\lambda)
that do not depend on the coordinates
(x,t_{2},t_{3},\dots,t_{M})
and hence
\mathbf{X}(\lambda):=\epsilon\mathbf{L}_{x}(\lambda)\mathbf{L}(\lambda)^{-1}
and
\mathbf{T}^{(m)}(\lambda):=\epsilon\mathbf{L}_{t_{m}}(\lambda)\mathbf{L}(\lambda)^{-1}
are polynomials in
\lambda
of degree
1
and
m=2,\dots,M
respectively. Expressing the coefficients of these polynomials in terms of the expansion of
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda)
as
\lambda\to\infty
then shows that
\mathbf{L}(\lambda)
is a fundamental simultaneous solution matrix of the Lax equations
\epsilon\mathbf{L}_{x}=\mathbf{XL}
and
\epsilon\mathbf{L}_{t_{m}}=\mathbf{T}^{(m)}\mathbf{L}
for
m=2,3,\dots,M
. The compatibility condition
\epsilon\mathbf{X}_{t_{m}}-\epsilon\mathbf{T}^{(m)}_{x}+[\mathbf{X},\mathbf{T}^{(m)}]=\mathbf{0}
is then equivalent to the equation
\epsilon\widetilde{\psi}_{t_{m}}=N_{m}[\widetilde{\psi},\widetilde{\psi}^{*}]
and hence taking
m=2,3,\dots,M
shows that
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{t})
is a simultaneous solution of the first
M-1
flows of the focusing NLS hierarchy, where
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{t})
is given by (
2.58
).

4
.

For each
\epsilon>0
and
(x,t_{2},t_{3},\dots,t_{M})\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
, the solution
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
of Riemann-Hilbert Problem
1
, satisfies the Schwarz symmetric property

(2.59)

\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})=\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda^{*};x,\mathbf{t})^{-\dagger},\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(P\cup P^{*}).

The renormalized function
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
defined by (
2.54
) inherits the same symmetry.

Proof.

If
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda)=\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
solves Riemann-Hilbert Problem
1
, first notice that
\det(\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda))
is entire, bounded and goes to 1 for
\lambda\to\infty
. A Liouville argument then implies that
\det(\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda))\equiv 1
.
A straightforward calculation shows that the function
\mathbf{N}(\lambda):=\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda^{*})^{-\dagger}
also solves Riemann-Hilbert Problem
1
.
The ratio
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda)\mathbf{N}(\lambda)^{-1}
is then an entire, bounded function which approaches
\mathbb{I}
as
\lambda\to\infty
.
The result then follows from Liouville’s theorem.
∎

5
.

Suppose that
c_{n}(x,\mathbf{t})\in\mathbb{R}
for
n=0,\dots,N-1
. Then
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{t})\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
. Likewise if
c_{n}(x,\mathbf{t})\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
for
n=0,\dots,N-1
, then
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{t})\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

In the former (respectively, latter) case,
\mathbf{N}(\lambda):=\sigma_{1}\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(-\lambda)\sigma_{1}
(respectively,
\mathbf{N}(\lambda):=\sigma_{2}\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(-\lambda)\sigma_{2}
) solves Riemann-Hilbert Problem
1
whenever
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda)
does. Arguing as in the proof of Proposition
4
, uniqueness implies that
\mathbf{N}(\lambda)=\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda)
. By Proposition
4
, we also have
\mathbf{N}(\lambda)=\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda)=\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda^{*})^{-\dagger}=\sigma_{2}\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda^{*})^{*\top}\sigma_{2}
. Equating the Laurent expansions in descending powers of
\lambda
as
\lambda\to\infty
and using (
2.58
) completes the proof.
∎

Since the connection coefficients
\widetilde{\tau}_{n}
defined in (
2.28
) are all real numbers, it follows that
c_{n}^{0}\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
for all
n=0,\dots,N-1
, and the same holds for
c_{n}(x,\mathbf{t})
provided that
t_{2}=t_{4}=\dots=0
(i.e., all even-indexed time coordinates vanish). Therefore by Proposition
5
, this condition implies that
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{t})
is real. In particular,
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{0})
is real for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

2.4.
Results

2.4.1.
Semiclassical soliton ensembles versus semicircular Klaus-Shaw potentials at
t=0

Our first result establishes the accuracy of approximating Cauchy data of semicircular Klaus-Shaw type (
2.21
) by its semiclassical soliton ensemble approximation. Recall that the semiclassical soliton ensemble
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{t})
depends on
N\in\mathbb{N}
(or
\epsilon=\epsilon_{N}
via (
2.24
)), but the semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential
\psi_{0}(x)=A(x)
that generates it is fixed.

Theorem 2.1

(Initial accuracy of semiclassical soliton ensembles)
.

Let Cauchy data
\psi_{0}(x)=A(x)
be given, where
A
is a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential supported on
[X_{-},X_{+}]
(see Definition
1
), and for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
let
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{t})
be the corresponding semiclassical soliton ensemble (see Definition
2
). Then

(2.60)

\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{0})=\psi_{0}(x)+\begin{cases}\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)&x\in(X_{-},x_{0})\cup(x_{0},X_{+})\\
\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{2}\right)&x\in[X_{-},X_{+}]^{c}\\
\end{cases},\qquad\epsilon=\epsilon_{N}\downarrow 0.

These estimates are uniform for
x
in compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{x_{0},X_{-},X_{+}\}
.

The proof will be given in Sections
4
and
5
below. We can apply this result to obtain convergence in the mean-square sense.

2
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2.1
, with
\epsilon=\epsilon_{N}
,

(2.61)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\|\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0})-\psi_{0}(\diamond)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=0.

Proof.

Since
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{0})
is a reflectionless potential, its
L^{2}
-norm is expressed in terms of the discrete eigenvalues
\lambda=\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}
,
n=0,\dots,N-1
by a standard trace formula:

(2.62)

\|\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{t})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}=\|\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}=4\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\epsilon\widetilde{s}_{n}.

Now the sum is a Riemann sum approximation of an integral; indeed, combining
(
2.24
) with (
2.26
) shows that

(2.63)

\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\epsilon\widetilde{s}_{j}=\frac{\|A\|_{1}}{\mathrm{i}\pi N}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\Phi^{-1}\left((j+\tfrac{1}{2})\frac{\|A\|_{1}}{N}\right)=\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\pi}\int_{0}^{\|A\|_{1}}\Phi^{-1}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi+\mathcal{O}\!\left(N^{-2}\right)

where
\|A\|_{1}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}A(x)\mathrm{d}x
and the error estimate is standard for the midpoint rule.
The integral can be evaluated by integration by parts as

(2.64)

\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\pi}\int_{0}^{\|A\|_{1}}\Phi^{-1}(\xi)\mathrm{d}\xi=\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\pi}\xi\Phi^{-1}(\xi)\Big|_{0}^{\|A\|_{1}}-\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\pi}\int_{\Phi^{-1}(0)}^{\Phi^{-1}(\|A\|_{1})}\Phi(\lambda)\mathrm{d}\lambda=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{A_{\text{max}}}\Phi(\mathrm{i}s)\mathrm{d}s,

where we have used that fact, following from (
2.26
), that
\Phi^{-1}(0)=\mathrm{i}A_{\text{max}}
and
\Phi^{-1}(\|A\|_{1})=0
.
Note that upon using (
2.27
), exchanging the integration order and using the substitution
s=A(x)t
in the (new) inner integral, one gets

(2.65)

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}}\Phi(\mathrm{i}s)\,\mathrm{d}s=\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}}A(x)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{4}\|\psi_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

Then we see that

(2.66)

\begin{split}\|\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0})-\psi_{0}(\diamond)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}&=\|\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\|\psi_{0}(\diamond)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}-2\mathrm{Re}\left(\langle\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0}),\psi_{0}(\diamond)\rangle\right)\\
&=2\|\psi_{0}(\diamond)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(N^{-2})-2\mathrm{Re}\left(\langle\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0}),\psi_{0}(\diamond)\rangle\right)\\
&=2\mathrm{Re}\left(\langle\psi_{0}(\diamond)-\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0}),\psi_{0}(\diamond)\rangle\right)+\mathcal{O}(N^{-2}).\end{split}

Clearly for any given
\delta>0
there exists
N_{0}
sufficiently large that
N>N_{0}
implies

(2.67)

\|\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0})-\psi_{0}(\diamond)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq 2\left|\langle\psi_{0}(\diamond)-\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0}),\psi_{0}(\diamond)\rangle\right|+\frac{1}{3}\delta.

Suppose
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{X_{-},x_{0},X_{+}\})
. Then

(2.68)

\begin{split}\left|\langle\psi_{0}(\diamond)-\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0}),\psi_{0}(\diamond)\rangle\right|&\leq\left|\langle\psi_{0}(\diamond)-\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0}),f(\diamond)\rangle\right|+\left|\langle\psi_{0}(\diamond)-\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0}),\psi_{0}(\diamond)-f(\diamond)\rangle\right|\\
&\leq\|\psi_{0}(\diamond)-\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathrm{spt}(f))}\|f(\diamond)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\\
&\qquad{}+\|\psi_{0}(\diamond)-\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|\psi_{0}(\diamond)-f(\diamond)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\\
&\leq\|\psi_{0}(\diamond)-\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathrm{spt}(f))}\|f(\diamond)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\\
&\qquad{}+\left(\|\psi_{0}(\diamond)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\|\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\right)\|\psi_{0}(\diamond)-f(\diamond)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.\end{split}

Since
\|\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\to\|\psi_{0}(\diamond)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}
as
N\to\infty
, there is some
N_{1}
so large that
N>N_{1}
guarantees
\|\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leq 2\|\psi_{0}(\diamond)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}
.
By a density argument, for each given
\delta>0
there is a
f=f_{\delta}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{X_{-},x_{0},X_{+}\})
such that

(2.69)

6\|\psi_{0}(\diamond)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|\psi_{0}(\diamond)-f_{\delta}(\diamond)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\leq\frac{1}{3}\delta.

Thus, if
N>\max\{N_{0},N_{1}\}
,

(2.70)

\|\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0})-\psi_{0}(\diamond)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq 2\|\psi_{0}(\diamond)-\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathrm{spt}(f_{\delta}))}\|f_{\delta}(\diamond)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}+\frac{2}{3}\delta.

Now by Theorem
2.1
there exists
N_{2}>0
such that
N>\max\{N_{0},N_{1},N_{2}\}
implies that

(2.71)

\|\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0})-\psi_{0}(\diamond)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq\delta

because the support of
f_{\delta}
is a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{X_{-},x_{0},X_{+}\}
.
∎

\begin{overpic}[NLS_IC_plots]

\end{overpic}

\end{overpic}
