\end{overpic}

Figure 3.

a) Plots of the numerically computed semiclassical soliton ensemble approximations
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{0})
(shown in colors) of the Klaus-Shaw initial data
\psi_{0}(x)=A(x)
(shown in black) given by (
2.100
) (with
A_{\mathrm{max}}=1
,
X_{\pm}=\pm\tfrac{1}{2}
, and
\xi=\frac{2}{3}
) for
\epsilon=\tfrac{1}{80},\tfrac{1}{120},\tfrac{1}{160},\tfrac{1}{240},\tfrac{1}{320},\frac{1}{480},\tfrac{1}{640},\tfrac{1}{960},\tfrac{1}{1280}
;
b) Pointwise plot of the errors
A(x)-\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{0})
for each value of
\epsilon
in part a);
c) Points (red squares/green triangles) show the sup-norm error
\|(A-\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0}))\chi_{\mathrm{in/out}}\|_{\infty}
over compact subsets of the interior/exterior of the support (
[-0.45,0.45]
and
\{0.55\leq|x|\leq 0.65\}
respectively) for each value of
\epsilon
in part a). The lines show the least squares fit of a power law to each data set. The computed powers
0.4933
and
2.0671
are in good agreement with the result of Theorem
2.1
.

2
.

Both
\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{t})
and the solution
\psi(\diamond,\mathbf{t})
of the focusing NLS hierarchy for fixed initial data
\psi(\diamond,\mathbf{0})=\psi_{0}(\diamond)
satisfy the same
\epsilon
-dependent system of partial differential equations, and both solutions are evidently close when
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{0}
and
\epsilon>0
is small. However, despite convergence of the initial data asserted in Theorem
2.1
and Corollary
2.61
, we cannot guarantee that
\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{t})
and
\psi(\diamond,\mathbf{t})
remain close for nonzero
\mathbf{t}
in the limit
\epsilon\downarrow 0
, because there are no known stability results for the Cauchy problem of the focusing NLS hierarchy that are uniform in
\epsilon
. Indeed, the maximum exponential growth rate for the well-known modulational instability is inversely proportional to
\epsilon
. Nonetheless, we will demonstrate below that some predictions about the dynamics for initial data
\psi_{0}(\diamond)
carry over also to the semiclassical soliton ensemble initial data
\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0})
.

2.4.2.
Extreme focusing in the NLS hierarchy

To formulate the next results, we first introduce a certain function
\Psi(X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})
by means of an auxiliary Riemann-Hilbert problem.

3
Rogue wave of infinite order for the NLS hierarchy.

Fix an integer
M\geq 2
. Given
(X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
, seek a
2\times 2
matrix function
\mathbf{R}(\Lambda)=\mathbf{R}(\Lambda;X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})
with the following properties:

Analyticity:

\mathbf{R}(\Lambda)
is an analytic function of
\Lambda
in the domains
|\Lambda|<1
and
|\Lambda|>1
.

Jump condition:

\mathbf{R}(\Lambda)
takes continuous boundary values on the unit circle from the interior (denoted
\mathbf{R}_{-}(\Lambda)
) and exterior (denoted
\mathbf{R}_{+}(\Lambda)
), and these are related by

(2.72)

\mathbf{R}_{+}(\Lambda)=\mathbf{R}_{-}(\Lambda)\exp\left(-\mathrm{i}\left(\Lambda X+\sum_{m=2}^{M}\Lambda^{m}T_{m}+2\Lambda^{-1}\right)\sigma_{3}\right)\\
{}\cdot\mathbf{Q}^{-1}\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\left(\Lambda X+\sum_{m=2}^{M}\Lambda^{m}T_{m}+2\Lambda^{-1}\right)\sigma_{3}\right),\quad|\Lambda|=1,\quad\mathbf{Q}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}.

Normalization:

\mathbf{R}(\Lambda)\to\mathbb{I}
as
\Lambda\to\infty
.

This problem has a unique solution for each choice of
(X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
, as follows from Zhou’s vanishing lemma
[
30
]
, and the function
\Psi(X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})
defined by

(2.73)

\Psi(X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M}):=2\mathrm{i}\lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}\Lambda R_{12}(\Lambda;X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})

is a complex-valued function whose real and imaginary parts are real-analytic
function of the
M
arguments. In the case
M=2
, this function first
appeared in the paper of Suleimanov
[
27
]
, where it was formally
proposed as a dispersive regularization of the blowup/collapse predicted by
Talanov’s analysis
[
28
]
of the dispersionless focusing NLS system
reviewed above in Section
2.2
. Later, in

[
6
]
, the same function appeared as a near-field/high-order limit
of fundamental rogue-wave solutions of the focusing NLS equation with nonzero
boundary conditions, where it was called the
rogue wave of infinite
order
. This function has also been shown to arise in the study of
boundary layers for the sharp-line Maxwell-Bloch system in characteristic
coordinates
[
18
]
, multiple-pole solutions of the focusing NLS equation

[
5
]
,
and more general rogue-wave solutions of the focusing NLS equation arising from
iterated Bäcklund transformations
[
7
,
9
]
.
In particular
\Psi(\diamond,0,\dots,0)
is a real-valued function that is not
in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
but is square integrable with

\|\Psi(\diamond,0,\dots,0)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\sqrt{8}
, and

\Psi(0,0,\dots,0)=4
. In
[
6
]
it was proved that this function
for
M=2
is an exact solution of the focusing NLS equation in the form

\mathrm{i}\Psi_{T_{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\Psi_{XX}+|\Psi|^{2}\Psi=0
, and that it also
solves equations in the Painlevé-III hierarchy of Sakka
[
25
]
as a
function of
X
for each fixed
T_{2}
. See
[
8
]
for further
information about the
M=2
case. Similar arguments based on the dressing
method show that for arbitrary
M=2,3,4,\dots
, the function

\Psi(X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})
is a simultaneous solution of the first
M-1

flows, suitably rescaled by setting
\epsilon=1
and replacing

(x,t_{2},t_{3},\dots,t_{M})
with
(X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})
, of the focusing NLS
hierarchy. See
[
4
]
for further details about

\Psi(X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})
and its generalizations.

A key point is that the solution
\Psi(X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})
describes the semiclassical asymptotic behavior of solutions of the focusing NLS hierarchy whenever
\Phi(\lambda)
and
\Xi(\lambda)
are polynomials:

Theorem 2.2

(Suleimanov-Talanov focusing of the hierarchy and dispersive regularization)
.

Suppose
\psi_{0}(x)=A(x)
is a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential for which
\Phi(\lambda)
and
\Xi(\lambda)
are polynomials of exact degree
2\mathcal{P}
and
2\mathcal{Q}-1
respectively:

(2.74)

\Phi(\lambda)=\sum_{p=0}^{\mathcal{P}}\Phi_{p}\lambda^{2p},\quad\Xi(\lambda)=\mathrm{i}\sum_{q=1}^{\mathcal{Q}}\Xi_{q}\lambda^{2q-1},

and let
M:=\max\{2\mathcal{P},2\mathcal{Q}-1\}
. Fixing an arbitrary integer
K
, define a point in
\mathbb{R}^{M}
by

(2.75)

(x^{\circ},t_{2}^{\circ},t_{3}^{\circ},\dots,t_{M}^{\circ}):=\begin{cases}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\Xi_{1},(2K+1)\Phi_{1},\Xi_{2},(2K+1)\Phi_{2},\dots,(2K+1)\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}\right),&M=2\mathcal{P},\\
-\frac{1}{2}\left(\Xi_{1},(2K+1)\Phi_{1},\Xi_{2},(2K+1)\Phi_{2},\dots,\Xi_{\mathcal{Q}}\right),&M=2\mathcal{Q}-1,\end{cases}

and denote the corresponding times by
\mathbf{t}^{\circ}:=(t_{2}^{\circ},t_{3}^{\circ},\dots,t_{M}^{\circ})
.
Then the semiclassical soliton ensemble
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{t})
associated with
\psi_{0}(x)=A(x)
satisfies

(2.76)

\widetilde{\psi}\left(x^{\circ}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\nu}X,\mathbf{t}^{\circ}+\left(\frac{\epsilon^{3}}{\nu^{2}}T_{2},\frac{\epsilon^{4}}{\nu^{3}}T_{3},\dots,\frac{\epsilon^{M+1}}{\nu^{M}}T_{M}\right)\right)\\
{}=\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K+N}\frac{\nu}{\epsilon}\Psi(X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})+\mathcal{O}\!\left(1\right)

as
\epsilon=\epsilon_{N}\downarrow 0
, uniformly for
(X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
bounded, where

(2.77)

\nu:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}}\Phi(\mathrm{i}s)\mathrm{d}s.

This result shows that
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{t})
exhibits focusing events of large amplitude proportional to
\epsilon^{-1}
in the neighborhood of each focal point
(x^{\circ},\mathbf{t}^{\circ})\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
, the family of which is parameterized by an arbitrary integer
K
. In a neighborhood of size proportional to
\epsilon^{2}
in
x
and proportional to
\epsilon^{m+1}
in
t_{m}
of each focal point, the wave field takes on a universal form involving the function
\Psi(X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})
. We refer to this kind of focusing as
Suleimanov-Talanov focusing
.

2.4.3.
Extreme focusing for mixed flows

According to Proposition
2
, the
x
-coordinate of each focal point is the same and is given explicitly in terms of the support endpoints as
x^{\circ}=\frac{1}{2}(X_{+}+X_{-})
. On the other hand, the time coordinates of the focal points
\mathbf{t}^{\circ}
vary with the index
K\in\mathbb{Z}
, lying equally-spaced along a straight line in the multi-time parameter space
\mathbb{R}^{M-1}
for
\mathbf{t}
. Considering now a particular mixture of the flows in the focusing NLS hierarchy defined by relating the coordinates
t_{2},t_{3},\dots,t_{M}
to a single real time variable
t
by
t_{m}=a_{m}t
for some fixed real constants
a_{2},a_{3},\dots,a_{M}
, it becomes clear that the focal points correspond to rare events that do not occur at all for most mixtures. If they do occur, the fact that the rescaled local time coordinates
T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M}
should be bounded while the unscaled time variables
t_{2},t_{3},\dots,t_{M}
should be in fixed proportion means that the limiting function
\Psi
should be evaluated at
T_{2}=T_{3}=\cdots=T_{M-1}=0
. However, it is also clear that the type of phenomena that can occur depends on whether the line containing the focal points passes through the origin
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{0}
. This happens exactly when
\Xi(\lambda)
is a linear monomial, or equivalently by Corollary
1
, when
\psi_{0}(x)=A(x)
is even about the midpoint
x^{\circ}
of its support interval
[X_{-},X_{+}]
. See Figure
4
.

odd times
even times
odd times
even times

Figure 4.
Left: the case that
\Xi(\lambda)
is a linear monomial. Red points indicate the focus times
\mathbf{t}^{\circ}
for different integers
K
, and there is a mixture of even flows (blue line) that experiences each focus. Right: the case that
\Xi(\lambda)
has a cubic or higher-order term, where there is a different mixture of flows for each
K\in\mathbb{Z}
that focuses just once.

Our result for mixtures of flows in the focusing NLS hierarchy is as follows:

Theorem 2.3

(Suleimanov-Talanov focusing of mixed flows and dispersive regularization)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2.77
,

(1)

If
\Xi(\lambda)
is a linear monomial, i.e.,
\psi_{0}(x)=A(x)
is even about
x^{\circ}
, then
all mixed flows of the focusing NLS hierarchy that undergo Suleimanov-Talanov focusing correspond to coordinates (a combination of the even flows only in the hierarchy)

(2.78)

(a_{2},a_{3},\dots,a_{M})=-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\left(\Phi_{1},0,\Phi_{2},0,\dots,\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}\right),\quad M=2\mathcal{P},

for a fixed real
\alpha\neq 0
, and the flow with commensurate coordinates
t_{m}=a_{m}t
exhibits infinitely many Suleimanov-Talanov focusing events periodically in time
t
with period
2/|\alpha|
. Specifically,
if we write
\widetilde{\psi}(x,t):=\widetilde{\psi}(x,(a_{2},a_{3},\dots,a_{M})t)
and set
t^{\circ}:=(2K+1)/\alpha
for an arbitrary integer
K
, then with
\nu
defined by (
2.77
),

(2.79)

\widetilde{\psi}\left(x^{\circ}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\nu}X,t^{\circ}+\frac{\epsilon^{M+1}}{a_{M}\nu^{M}}T_{M}\right)=\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K+N}\frac{\nu}{\epsilon}\Psi(X,0,0,\dots,0,T_{M})+\mathcal{O}\!\left(1\right)

as
\epsilon\to 0
, uniformly for
(X,T_{M})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
bounded.

(2)

Otherwise, for each integer
K
, mixed flows of the focusing NLS hierarchy corresponding to coordinates
t_{m}=a_{m}t
with

(2.80)

(a_{2},a_{3},\dots,a_{M})=\\
-\frac{1}{2}\alpha\begin{cases}\left((2K+1)\Phi_{1},\Xi_{2},(2K+1)\Phi_{2},\Xi_{3},\dots,(2K+1)\Phi_{\mathcal{P}}\right),&M=2\mathcal{P}\\
\left((2K+1)\Phi_{1},\Xi_{2},(2K+1)\Phi_{2},\Xi_{3},\dots,\Xi_{2\mathcal{Q}-1}\right),&M=2\mathcal{Q}-1\end{cases}

exhibit exactly one Suleimanov-Talanov focusing event near time
t=t^{\circ}:=1/\alpha
, where
\widetilde{\psi}(x,t):=\widetilde{\psi}(x,(a_{2},a_{3},\dots,a_{M})t)
is characterized by (
2.79
) in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
with
(X,T_{M})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
bounded.

2.4.4.
Application to the focusing NLS equation

In particular, this theorem allows us to prove a rigorous version of the result conjectured in the paper of Suleimanov
[
27
]
. To apply Theorem
2.3
in the case of initial data
\psi_{0}(x)=A(x)
given by (
2.22
) as is consistent with a Talanov-type solution for
E<0
of the dispersionless focusing NLS system (
1.3
), first note that from the definition (
2.27
) of
\Phi(\lambda)
, by a residue calculation at
x=\infty
(see
[
12
, Eqn. (4.27)]
),

(2.81)

\begin{split}\Phi(\mathrm{i}s)&=\int_{x_{-}(s)}^{x_{+}(s)}\sqrt{\frac{4A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}(x-X_{-})(X_{+}-x)}{(X_{+}-X_{-})^{2}}-s^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\frac{\pi(X_{+}-X_{-})}{4A_{\mathrm{max}}}(A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}-s^{2}),\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}\end{split}

and an easier calculation starting from (
2.29
) gives
1
1

1

This corrects
[
12
, Eqn. (4.29)]
, which includes an extraneous factor of
\frac{1}{2}
.

(2.82)

\Xi(\mathrm{i}s)=(X_{+}+X_{-})s,\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}.

Replacing
s
with
s=-\mathrm{i}\lambda
we see that
\Phi(\lambda)
and
\Xi(\lambda)
are the following polynomials in
\lambda

(2.83)

\Phi(\lambda)=\frac{\pi(X_{+}-X_{-})}{4A_{\mathrm{max}}}(A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}+\lambda^{2})\quad\text{and}\quad\Xi(\lambda)=-\mathrm{i}(X_{+}+X_{-})\lambda.

According to Propositions
1
and
2
, these are the simplest possible for a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential (having the minimal number of terms in their Taylor series at
\lambda=0
). They match the form (
2.74
) with
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{Q}=1
, and hence
M=\max\{2\mathcal{P},2\mathcal{Q}-1\}=2
. Our rigorous version of Suleimanov’s result
[
27
]
is then as follows:

3
Suleimanov-Talanov focusing in the NLS equation.

Let
\widetilde{\psi}(x,t_{2})
denote the semiclassical soliton ensemble with
M=2
, for the Talanov-type initial condition
\psi_{0}(x)=A(x)
given by (
2.22
). Then
\widetilde{\psi}(x,t_{2})
is an exact solution of the focusing NLS equation (
1.1
) with initial condition close to
\psi_{0}(x)
as described by Theorem
2.1
. Furthermore,
\widetilde{\psi}(x,t_{2})
exhibits Suleimanov-Talanov focusing near
x=x^{\circ}:=\frac{1}{2}(X_{+}+X_{-})
periodically in time
t_{2}
in the sense that for each integer
K
, defining a focus time by

(2.84)

t_{K}^{\circ}:=-\frac{\pi(X_{+}-X_{-})}{8A_{\mathrm{max}}}(2K+1),

we have

(2.85)

\widetilde{\psi}\left(x^{\circ}+\frac{12\epsilon^{2}}{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}(X_{+}-X_{-})}X,t_{K}^{\circ}+\frac{144\epsilon^{3}}{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{4}(X_{+}-X_{-})^{2}}T_{2}\right)\\
=(-1)^{K+N}\mathrm{i}\frac{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}(X_{+}-X_{-})}{12\epsilon}\Psi(X,T_{2})+\mathcal{O}\!\left(1\right)

as
\epsilon\to 0
through the integer sequence
\epsilon=\epsilon_{N}:=\frac{1}{4}A_{\mathrm{max}}(X_{+}-X_{-})N^{-1}
,
N=1,2,3,\dots
, where the error term is uniform for bounded
(X,T_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Proof.

Apply Theorem
2.3
in the case
M=2
with
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{Q}=1
, in which case the first scenario holds. We take
a_{2}=1
to ensure that
\widetilde{\psi}
solves the focusing NLS equation in the form (
1.1
).
∎

3
.

This result differs from the claim in
[
27
]
in two ways. Firstly, it concerns initial data
\psi_{0}(\diamond)=A(\diamond)
corresponding to an integration constant
E<0
instead of
E=0
for the dispersionless system (
1.3
). Second, it is not a statement about the initial-value problem for (
1.1
) with Cauchy data
\psi_{0}(\diamond)=A(\diamond)
but rather with modified Cauchy data
\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0})
. While
\psi_{0}(\diamond)
and
\widetilde{\psi}(\diamond,\mathbf{0})
are close according to Theorem
2.1
and Corollary
2.61
, these two initial conditions are not equal. Therefore, in light of the strong instabilities pointed out in Remark
2
, it is remarkable that the dispersionless theory makes such an accurate prediction.

The focusing events nearest to
t_{2}=0
correspond to
K=-1,0
, where the solution grows to size proportional to
\epsilon^{-1}
near the points
(x,t_{2})=(\frac{1}{2}(X_{+}+X_{-}),\pm\pi(X_{+}-X_{-})/(8A_{\mathrm{max}}))
. The time coordinates here are precisely
\pm\frac{1}{2}\Delta t
as defined in (
2.16
) using also (
2.18
)–(
2.19
) with the initial width being
w(0)=\frac{1}{2}(X_{+}-X_{-})
. Thus the solution becomes large exactly near the points predicted by the dispersionless Talanov theory
[
28
]
for
E<0
as discussed in Section
2.2
and near each of these two points the blowing up and collapsing solution is dispersively regularized according to the prediction of Suleimanov
[
27
]
. Moreover, the NLS solution survives beyond the time interval
(-\frac{1}{2}\Delta t,\frac{1}{2}\Delta t)
and exhibits periodic “breathing” of period
\Delta t
consisting of alternate periods of dispersive spreading and refocusing. See Figure
5
.

Figure 5.
Top row:
Density plots of
|\widetilde{\psi}(x,t_{2})|
for different values of
\epsilon
, where
\widetilde{\psi}
is the semiclassical soliton ensemble solution of NLS generated from the simplest semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential
A(x)=\sqrt{1-(x/2)^{2}}\chi_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}(x)
.
Red circles indicate the location of the Suleimanov-Talanov focusing events described by Corollary
3
.
Bottom row:
Plots of
|\widetilde{\psi}(x,t_{2})|
in the rescaled local coordinates
(X,T_{2})
defined by (
2.85
) centered at the focus point
(x^{\circ},t_{-1}^{\circ})=(0,\tfrac{\pi}{8})
.

2.4.5.
Application to other mixed flows: Hirota and LPD equations

There are many other semicircular Klaus-Shaw potentials for which
\Phi(\lambda)
and
\Xi(\lambda)
are polynomials. To prove this, we introduce polynomial perturbations of (
2.83
):

(2.86)

\displaystyle\Phi(\lambda)=\Phi^{\mathrm{sc}}(\lambda)\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{P}-1}B_{k}\lambda^{2k}\right),\quad

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mathrm{sc}}(\lambda):=\Phi_{0}\left(1+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\Xi(\lambda)=\Xi^{\mathrm{sc}}(\lambda)+\mathrm{i}\sum_{q=2}^{\mathcal{Q}}\Xi_{q}\lambda^{2q-1},\quad

\displaystyle\Xi^{\mathrm{sc}}(\lambda):=\mathrm{i}\Xi_{1}\lambda,

for some real coefficients
\Phi_{0}
,
A_{\mathrm{max}}
,
B_{1},\dots,B_{\mathcal{P}-1}
,
\Xi_{1},\dots,\Xi_{\mathcal{Q}}
. The perturbation of
\Phi^{\mathrm{sc}}(\lambda)
is taken to be relative instead of additive in order to fix the maximum amplitude
A_{\mathrm{max}}
, since Proposition
1
requires that
\Phi(\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}})=0
.
If the coefficients
B_{1},\dots,B_{\mathcal{P}-1},\Xi_{1},\dots,\Xi_{\mathcal{Q}}
are sufficiently small, then these expressions will be the phase integral
\Phi
and tail integral
\Xi
of a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential
A(x)
with maximum amplitude
A_{\mathrm{max}}
as determined by
(
2.27
) and (
2.29
) respectively. To see this, we apply Proposition
2.40
.
First, we calculate

(2.87)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}m}\Xi(\mathrm{i}m)\,\mathrm{d}m}{\sqrt{s^{2}-m^{2}}}&=-\frac{\Xi_{1}}{2}+\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{q=2}^{\mathcal{Q}}(-1)^{q}(2q-1)\Xi_{q}\int_{0}^{s}\frac{m^{2q-2}\,\mathrm{d}m}{\sqrt{s^{2}-m^{2}}}\\
&=-\frac{\Xi_{1}}{2}+\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{q=2}^{\mathcal{Q}}(-1)^{q}(2q-1)\Xi_{q}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{v^{2q-2}\,\mathrm{d}v}{\sqrt{1-v^{2}}}\cdot s^{2q-2}\\
&=-\frac{\Xi_{1}}{2}+\sum_{q=2}^{\mathcal{Q}}\frac{(-1)^{q}\Xi_{q}}{2}\frac{(2q-1)!!}{(2q-2)!!}\cdot s^{2q-2}.\end{split}

Also,

(2.88)

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{s}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}}\frac{\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}m}\Phi(\mathrm{i}m)\,\mathrm{d}m}{\sqrt{m^{2}-s^{2}}}=-\frac{2\Phi_{0}}{\pi A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}-s^{2}}\\
+\frac{2\Phi_{0}}{\pi A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{P}-1}(-1)^{k}B_{k}\left(A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}k\int_{s}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}}\frac{m^{2k-1}\,\mathrm{d}m}{\sqrt{m^{2}-s^{2}}}-(k+1)\int_{s}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}}\frac{m^{2k+1}\,\mathrm{d}m}{\sqrt{m^{2}-s^{2}}}\right),

and since by the substitution
m=\sqrt{s^{2}+(A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}-s^{2})z^{2}}
,

(2.89)

\begin{split}\int_{s}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}}\frac{m^{2k-1}\,\mathrm{d}m}{\sqrt{m^{2}-s^{2}}}&=\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}-s^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}(s^{2}+(A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}-s^{2})z^{2})^{k-1}\,\mathrm{d}z,\\
\int_{s}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}}\frac{m^{2k+1}\,\mathrm{d}m}{\sqrt{m^{2}-s^{2}}}&=\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}-s^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}(s^{2}+(A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}-s^{2})z^{2})^{k}\,\mathrm{d}z,\end{split}

we obtain

(2.90)

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{s}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}}\frac{\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}m}\Phi(\mathrm{i}m)\,\mathrm{d}m}{\sqrt{m^{2}-s^{2}}}=-\frac{2\Phi_{0}}{\pi A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}-s^{2}}\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{P}-1}(-1)^{k}B_{k}P_{k}(s^{2})\right),

where
P_{k}(s^{2})
is a polynomial in
s^{2}
of degree
k
given by

(2.91)

P_{k}(s^{2}):=\int_{0}^{1}\left((k+1)(s^{2}+(A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}-s^{2})z^{2})-A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}k\right)(s^{2}+(A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}-s^{2})z^{2})^{k-1}\,\mathrm{d}z.

This yields

(2.92)

x_{\pm}(s)=-\frac{\Xi_{1}}{2}+\sum_{q=2}^{\mathcal{Q}}\frac{(-1)^{q}\Xi_{q}}{2}\frac{(2q-1)!!}{(2q-2)!!}\cdot s^{2q-2}\\
\pm\frac{2\Phi_{0}}{\pi A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}-s^{2}}\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{P}-1}(-1)^{k}B_{k}P_{k}(s^{2})\right).

These will be the two inverse functions of a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential
A(x)
with maximum amplitude
A_{\mathrm{max}}
provided that
x_{+}(s)>x_{-}(s)
and
x_{+}(s)
(resp.,
x_{-}(s)
) is decreasing (resp., increasing) on
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
. For fixed
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{P}
, this is clearly the case as long as the coefficients
B_{1},\dots,B_{\mathcal{P}-1}
and
\Xi_{2},\dots,\Xi_{\mathcal{Q}}
are sufficiently small. The maximizer
x_{0}
of the semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential
A(x)
obtained is

(2.93)

x_{0}:=x_{\pm}(A_{\mathrm{max}})=-\frac{\Xi_{1}}{2}+\sum_{q=2}^{\mathcal{Q}}\frac{(-1)^{q}\Xi_{q}}{2}\frac{(2q-1)!!}{(2q-2)!!}A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2q-2},

and the support endpoints
X_{\pm}
are given by

(2.94)

X_{\pm}:=x_{\pm}(0)=-\frac{\Xi_{1}}{2}\pm\frac{2\Phi_{0}}{\pi A_{\mathrm{max}}}\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^{\mathcal{P}-1}(-1)^{k}B_{k}P_{k}(0)\right).

Note that this gives
\Xi_{1}=-(X_{+}+X_{-})
, as is consistent with Proposition
2
.

For low-degree examples it is possible to invert the above relationships and express things in terms of the Klaus-Shaw potential directly.
Consider the case that
\mathcal{P}=1
and
\mathcal{Q}=2
.
Then the corresponding inverse functions from (
2.92
) are

(2.95)

x_{\pm}(s)=\frac{X_{+}+X_{-}}{2}+\frac{3}{4}\Xi_{2}s^{2}\pm\frac{X_{+}-X_{-}}{2A_{\mathrm{max}}}\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}-s^{2}},\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}},

where (
2.94
) has been used to express
\Phi_{0}
in terms of
X_{+}-X_{-}
and
A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}
.
Therefore also

(2.96)

x_{\pm}^{\prime}(s)=\left(\frac{3}{2}\Xi_{2}\mp\frac{X_{+}-X_{-}}{2A_{\mathrm{max}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}-s^{2}}}\right)s,\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}.

From this, we can see easily that
\mp x_{\pm}^{\prime}(s)>0
on
(0,A_{\mathrm{max}})
with linear vanishing at
s=0
as necessarily holds for the inverse functions of every semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential if and only if

(2.97)

3|\Xi_{2}|<\frac{X_{+}-X_{-}}{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}.

Enforcing this inequality on the coefficient
\Xi_{2}
by setting

(2.98)

\Xi_{2}:=\frac{X_{+}-X_{-}}{3A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}\xi,\qquad\xi\in(-1,1),

we obtain a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential with support
[X_{-},X_{+}]
that is additionally parametrized by
\xi
. We can write the potential explicitly by replacing
x_{\pm}(s)
on the left-hand side of (
2.95
) with
x
, yielding a quadratic equation for
s^{2}=A(x)^{2}
:

(2.99)

\displaystyle\frac{\xi^{2}}{4}\frac{s^{4}}{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{4}}+\left[1-\xi\frac{2x-X_{+}-X_{-}}{X_{+}-X_{-}}\right]\frac{s^{2}}{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}+\left(\frac{2x-X_{+}-X_{-}}{X_{+}-X_{-}}\right)^{2}-1=0.

Noting that the constant (in
s
) term above is negative for
x\in(X_{-},X_{+})
, the roots
s^{2}
have opposite signs for
x\in[X_{-},X_{+}]
, so selecting the positive root and taking a square root gives (
2.100
).
Applying Theorem
2.3
to the semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential (
2.100
) in the case that
M=\max\{2\mathcal{P},2\mathcal{Q}-1\}=3
(Hirota equation) yields the following corollary.

4
Suleimanov-Talanov focusing in the Hirota equation.

Consider the Hirota equation (
2.3
) with nonzero coefficients
a_{2}
and
a_{3}
. For parameters
A_{\mathrm{max}}>0
,
X_{+}>X_{-}
, and
\xi\in(-1,1)
, let
\psi_{0}(x)=A(x)
be the semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential

(2.100)

A(x)=\frac{\sqrt{2}A_{\mathrm{max}}}{|\xi|}\left[\sqrt{1-2y(x)\xi+\xi^{2}}-1+y(x)\xi\right]^{\mathrlap{{1/2}}}\chi_{[X_{-},X_{+}]}(x),\quad y(x)=\frac{2x-X_{+}-X_{-}}{X_{+}-X_{-}},

and denote by
\widetilde{\psi}(x,t)
the semiclassical soliton ensemble solution of (
2.3
) corresponding to
\psi_{0}
. Fix an integer
K\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then whenever the parameters

(a_{2},a_{3};A_{\mathrm{max}},\xi)

satisfy

(2.101)

4a_{2}\xi-3\pi(2K+1)a_{3}A_{\mathrm{max}}=0,

\widetilde{\psi}(x,t)
undergoes a single Suleimanov-Talanov focusing event near the point

(2.102)

(x^{\circ},t^{\circ}_{K})=\left(\frac{1}{2}(X_{+}+X_{-}),-\frac{\pi(X_{+}-X_{-})}{8a_{2}A_{\mathrm{max}}}(2K+1)\right)

in the sense that

(2.103)

\widetilde{\psi}\left(x^{\circ}+\frac{12\epsilon^{2}}{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}(X_{+}-X_{-})}X,t^{\circ}_{K}+\frac{\epsilon^{4}}{a_{3}}\left(\frac{12}{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}(X_{+}-X_{-})}\right)^{3}T_{3}\right)\\
=(-1)^{K+N}\mathrm{i}\frac{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}(X_{+}-X_{-})}{12\epsilon}\Psi(X,0,T_{3})+\mathcal{O}\!\left(1\right)

as
\epsilon\to 0
through the integer sequence
\epsilon=\epsilon_{N}
,
N=1,2,3,\dots
, with the error estimate being uniform for bounded
(X,T_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Proof.

Using the calculations in the paragraph preceding Corollary
4
, a Klaus-Shaw potential of the form (
2.100
) produces polynomial phase and tail integrals given by

(2.104)

\Phi(\lambda)=\frac{\pi(X_{+}-X_{-})}{4A_{\mathrm{max}}}(A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}+\lambda^{2}),\qquad\Xi(\lambda)=-\mathrm{i}(X_{+}+X_{-})\lambda+\frac{\mathrm{i}(X_{+}-X_{-})\xi}{3A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}\lambda^{3}.

We then apply Theorem
2.3
in the case
M=3
with
\mathcal{P}=1
and
\mathcal{Q}=2
, in which case the second scenario holds.
∎

As an illustration of Corollary
4
, we fixed a suitable semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential consistent with the hypotheses as well as a small value of
\epsilon
, and then constructed the corresponding semiclassical soliton ensembles for the Hirota equation (
2.3
) with a fixed coefficient
a_{2}=1
, varying only the coefficient
a_{3}
. The plots are shown in Figure
6
. Suleimanov-Talanov focusing is only observed for certain quantized values of
a_{3}
, and for those values it occurs precisely once. Although these focusing events occur for different equations and at different times, upon rescaling about the predicted focus coordinates
(x^{\circ},t_{K}^{\circ})
to the coordinates
(X,T_{3})
the plots all appear similar, pointing toward the universal nature of the limiting function
\Psi(X,0,T_{3})
.

Figure 6.

Top row:
Density plots of
|\widetilde{\psi}(x,t)|
for the semiclassical soliton ensemble solutions, generated by the Klaus-Shaw potential (
2.100
) (with
X_{\pm}=\pm\tfrac{1}{2},A_{\mathrm{max}}=1
, and
\xi=\tfrac{2}{3}
), of the Hirota equation (
2.3
) with
\epsilon=\tfrac{1}{80}
,
a_{2}=1
and varying values of
a_{3}
.
Red circles indicate the locations of Suleimanov-Talanov focusing events as described in Corollary
4
.
Unlike the even NLS flow, these occur only for quantized values of
a_{3}
and at most once in the spacetime.
Bottom row:
Density plots of
|\widetilde{\psi}(x,t)|
in the rescaled local coordinates
(X,T_{3})
defined by (
2.103
) centered at the focus point
(x^{\circ},t_{K}^{\circ})
.

As a second example, consider the case
\mathcal{P}=2
and
\mathcal{Q}=1
, so that
M=\max\{2\mathcal{P},2\mathcal{Q}-1\}=4
and we are thus in the setting of the LPD equation. From (
2.92
), the inverse functions become

(2.105)

x_{\pm}(s)=-\frac{\Xi_{1}}{2}\pm\frac{2\Phi_{0}}{\pi A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}-s^{2}}\left(1+\frac{b_{1}}{3}\left(1-\frac{4s^{2}}{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}\right)\right),\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}},

where in (
2.86
) we have written
B_{1}=b_{1}A_{\mathrm{max}}^{-2}
to simplify the resulting formulæ. The derivatives are

(2.106)

x^{\prime}_{\pm}(s)=\mp\frac{2\Phi_{0}}{\pi A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}\frac{s}{\sqrt{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}-s^{2}}}\left(1+b_{1}\left(3-4\frac{s^{2}}{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}\right)\right),\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}},

and so the monotonicity condition
\mp x_{\mp}^{\prime}(s)>0
on
(0,A_{\mathrm{max}})
guaranteeing that
A(x)
is a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential is satisfied if and only if

(2.107)

-\frac{1}{3}<b_{1}<1.

Assuming that
b_{1}
satisfies this inequality, we get a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential whose support is the interval
[X_{-},X_{+}]
where, according to (
2.94
),

(2.108)

X_{\pm}=x_{\pm}(0)=-\frac{\Xi_{1}}{2}\pm\frac{2\Phi_{0}}{3\pi A_{\mathrm{max}}}(3+b_{1}).

The relations (
2.108
) yield expressions for the polynomial coefficients
\Xi_{1}
and
\Phi_{0}
in terms of the physical parameters of the initial condition:

(2.109)

\Xi_{1}=-(X_{+}+X_{-}),\qquad\Phi_{0}=\frac{3\pi A_{\mathrm{max}}(X_{+}-X_{-})}{4(3+b_{1})}.

The potential
A(x)
is then given implicitly by replacing
x_{\pm}(s)
on the left-hand side of (
2.105
)
with
x
, yielding a sextic equation for
s=A(x)
:

(2.110)

\left[\frac{2x-X_{+}-X_{-}}{X_{+}-X_{-}}\right]^{2}=\left(1-\frac{A(x)^{2}}{A^{2}_{\mathrm{max}}}\right)\left(1-\gamma\frac{A(x)^{2}}{A^{2}_{\mathrm{max}}}\right)^{2},\quad\gamma:=\frac{4b_{1}}{3+b_{1}},

which has a unique solution such that
A(x)\in[0,A_{\mathrm{max}}]
for each
x\in[X_{-},X_{+}]
. Note that
-\frac{1}{3}<b_{1}<1
corresponds to
-\frac{1}{2}<\gamma<1
.

Using (
2.26
)-(
2.27
) with
\Phi(\lambda)=\Phi_{0}(1+\lambda^{2}/A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2})(1+b_{1}\lambda^{2}/A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2})
, the semiclassical soliton ensemble
\widetilde{\psi}
corresponding to this Klaus-Shaw potential is obtained via (
2.58
) from the solution of Riemann-Hilbert Problem
1
where the poles
\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}\in P\subset\mathbb{C}_{+}
are given by

(2.111)

\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}=\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}}\left[\frac{1+b_{1}}{2b_{1}}\left(1-\sqrt{1-\frac{4b_{1}}{(1+b_{1})^{2}}\left(1-\frac{2n+1}{2N}\right)}\right)\right]^{1/2},\quad n=0,\dots,N-1;

the residue coefficients
c_{n}^{0}
are given by (
2.53
) with
\widetilde{\tau}_{n}=(-1)^{n+1}\mathrm{e}^{(X_{+}+X_{-})\widetilde{s}_{n}/\epsilon}
according to (
2.28
); and
\epsilon=\epsilon_{N}
is given by (
2.116
) below.

Applying Theorem
2.3
to the semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential (
2.110
) yields the following corollary.

5
Suleimanov-Talanov focusing in the LPD equation.

Consider the LPD equation (
2.4
) with nonzero coefficients
a_{2}
and
a_{4}
. Given parameters
A_{\mathrm{max}}>0
,
X_{+}>X_{-}
, and
\gamma\in(-\tfrac{1}{2},1)
, let
\psi_{0}(x)=A(x)
be the semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential supported on
[X_{-},X_{+}]
implicitly defined as the unique solution of

(2.112)

\left(\frac{2x-X_{+}-X_{-}}{X_{+}-X_{-}}\right)^{2}=\left(1-\frac{A(x)^{2}}{A^{2}_{\mathrm{max}}}\right)\left(1-\gamma\frac{A(x)^{2}}{A^{2}_{\mathrm{max}}}\right)^{2},\qquad x\in[X_{-},X_{+}],

for which
0<A(x)<A_{\mathrm{max}}
for each
x\in[X_{-},X_{+}]
.
Denote by
\widetilde{\psi}(x,t)
the corresponding semiclassical soliton ensemble solution of (
2.4
). Then whenever the parameters
(a_{2},a_{4};A_{\mathrm{max}},\gamma)
satisfy

(2.113)

(4+2\gamma)a_{4}A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}-3\gamma a_{2}=0,

\widetilde{\psi}(x,t)
experiences periodic Suleimanov-Talanov focusing events near the points

(2.114)

(x^{\circ},t^{\circ}_{K})=\left(\frac{1}{2}(X_{+}+X_{-}),-(2K+1)\frac{\pi(X_{+}-X_{-})(2+\gamma)}{16a_{2}A_{\mathrm{max}}}\right),\quad K\in\mathbb{Z}

in the sense that for each
K\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(2.115)

\widetilde{\psi}\left(x^{\circ}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\nu_{\textsc{lpd}}}X,t^{\circ}_{K}+\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{a_{4}\nu_{\textsc{lpd}}^{4}}T_{4}\right)=\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K+N}\frac{\nu_{\textsc{lpd}}}{\epsilon}\Psi(X,0,0,T_{4})+\mathcal{O}\!\left(1\right)

as
\epsilon\to 0
through the integer sequence
\epsilon=\epsilon_{N}
,
N=1,2,3,\dots
, with the error estimate being uniform for bounded
(X,T_{4})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Here

(2.116)

\epsilon_{N}=\frac{(4-\gamma)(X_{+}-X_{-})A_{\mathrm{max}}}{16N},\qquad\nu_{\textsc{lpd}}=\frac{(5-2\gamma)(X_{+}-X_{-})A_{\mathrm{max}}}{60}.

Proof.

A Klaus-Shaw potential of the form (
2.112
) produces phase and tail integrals given by

(2.117)

\displaystyle\Phi(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{(4-\gamma)\pi(X_{+}-X_{-})}{16A_{\mathrm{max}}}(A_{\mathrm{\max}}^{2}+\lambda^{2})\left(1+\frac{3\gamma}{4-\gamma}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\Xi(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}(X_{+}+X_{-})\lambda.

We apply Theorem
2.3
in the case
M=4
with
\mathcal{P}=2
and
\mathcal{Q}=1
, in which case the first scenario holds.
∎

An illustration of the prediction of Corollary
5
is shown in Figure
7
, which displays the expected characteristic periodic focusing.

Figure 7.
a) Density plot of
|\widetilde{\psi}(x,t)|
for the semiclassical soliton ensemble solution of the LPD equation (
2.4
) generated by the Klaus-Shaw initial data
A(x)
given by (
2.112
) (with
X_{\pm}=\pm\tfrac{1}{2}=0
,
A_{\mathrm{max}}=1
,
\gamma=\tfrac{4}{7}
) with
a_{2}=1
and
a_{4}
given by (
2.113
) and
\epsilon=\tfrac{1}{160}
.
b) A higher resolution computation of the solution in the red rectangular region surrounding the focus point
(x^{\circ},t_{-1}^{\circ})=(0,\tfrac{3\pi}{16})
.
c) The solution in the local coordinates
(X,T_{4})
described in Corollary
5
in which the leading order behavior is described by
\Psi(X,0,0,T_{4})
.

4
.

Under a Madelung-type ansatz
\psi(x,t)=\rho^{1/2}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}S/\epsilon}
introducing real variables
\rho
and
\mu=\rho S_{x}
, one can obtain a dispersionless system with two unknowns similar to (
1.3
) for each mixed flow of the focusing NLS hierarchy.
Although Theorem
2.3
proves that solutions of various mixed flows exhibit asymptotic behavior in which the amplitude reaches large values proportional to
\epsilon^{-1}
on space and time scales that are small as
\epsilon\to 0
, we do not claim in general that these focusing events represent dispersive regularizations of Talanov-like collapse/blow-up solutions of the corresponding dispersionless systems. Indeed, the plots shown in Figure
7
suggest that the earliest catastrophe of the dispersionless LPD system is instead of elliptic-umbilic type, leading to a triangular array of peaks similar to the Bertola-Tovbis regularization for focusing NLS
[
3
]
. Moving forward in time from this point, the triangular array apparently develops into a modulated genus-two solution. As such, the function
\Psi(X,0,0,T_{4})
might be expected to be a dispersive regularization of a Talanov-like collapse/blow-up solution of the genus-two Whitham modulation system for the LPD equation. The latter is a quasilinear elliptic system with
six
unknowns (see
[
15
]
for the NLS analogue).

2.4.6.
Extreme focusing for higher pure flows

The
n^{\mathrm{th}}

pure flow
of the focusing NLS hierarchy, denoted NLS
n
, corresponds to the case in which we tie the sequence of time coordinates to a single variable
t\in\mathbb{R}
by
t_{m}=a_{m}t
where
a_{m}=\delta_{m,n}
is the Kronecker delta. Examples include the mKdV equation (
2.1
) for
n=3
and (
2.2
) for
n=4
. These equations for
n>2
do not fall into the category of mixed flows to which Theorem
2.3
applies, because according to Proposition
1
the coefficient
\Phi_{1}
cannot vanish for semiclassical soliton ensembles generated from any semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential, and as such the mixture must contain a component of the
t_{2}
flow (i.e., the NLS equation itself). However, we can still show that for any given pure flow there exists initial data that leads to Suleimanov-Talanov focusing.

Theorem 2.4

(Suleimanov-Talanov focusing of pure flows)
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2.77
, fix
K\in\mathbb{Z}
, a flow index
n=2,3,\dots,M
,
M:=\max\{\deg(\Phi),\deg(\Xi)\}
, and a real number
t^{\circ}
. Set
\mathbf{t}_{n}^{\circ}:=(t_{2}^{\circ},\dots,t_{n-1}^{\circ},t_{n}^{\circ}-t^{\circ},t_{n+1}^{\circ},\dots,t_{M}^{\circ})\in\mathbb{R}^{M-1}
with
t_{j}^{\circ}
defined by (
2.75
) for
j=2,\dots,M
. Then the function
x\mapsto\psi(x,0):=\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{t}_{n}^{\circ})
is an initial datum
for which the corresponding solution
\psi(x,t)
of the
n^{\mathrm{th}}
pure flow of the focusing NLS hierarchy as a function of
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
experiences a Suleimanov-Talanov focusing at
(x,t)=(x^{\circ},t^{\circ})
with
x^{\circ}:=-\frac{1}{2}\Xi_{1}
. More precisely,

(2.118)

\psi\left(x^{\circ}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\nu}X,t^{\circ}+\frac{\epsilon^{n+1}}{\nu^{n}}T_{n}\right)=\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K+N}\frac{\nu}{\epsilon}\Psi(X,0,\dots,0,T_{n},0,\dots,0)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(1\right)

as
\epsilon=\epsilon_{N}\downarrow 0
, uniformly for
(X,T_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
bounded, where
\nu
is given by (
2.77
).

Proof.

We use the fact that all of the flows in the focusing NLS hierarchy commute to shift the origin in the space of times
t_{2},t_{3},\dots
so that the line in the
t_{n}
direction intersects a selected focus point indexed by
K
.
∎

5
.

The characterization of initial data in Theorem
2.4
is implicit, in terms of the solution of Riemann-Hilbert Problem
1
. The shift of origin that is behind the proof would be expected to introduce oscillations of finite amplitude and wavelength proportional to
\epsilon
, so that the initial data
\psi(x,0)
would not be an approximation of any Klaus-Shaw potential, semicircular or otherwise. The presence of such oscillations is clear in examples.

To demonstrate the use of Theorem
2.4
, first consider the NLS
3
(mKdV) flow.
We generate initial data starting with a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential
A(x)
of the form (
2.100
) which has a quadratic phase integral
\Phi(\lambda)
and a cubic tail integral
\Xi(\lambda)
given by (
2.104
).
Using Theorem
2.77
we choose a focus point

(2.119)

\begin{split}(x^{\circ},t_{2}^{\circ},t_{3}^{\circ})&=(-\tfrac{1}{2}\Xi_{1},-\tfrac{1}{2}(2K+1)\Phi_{1},-\tfrac{1}{2}\Xi_{2})\\
&=\left(\frac{X_{+}+X_{-}}{2},-(2K+1)\frac{\pi(X_{+}-X_{-})}{\pi A_{\mathrm{max}}},-\frac{\xi(X_{+}-X_{-})}{6}\right)\end{split}

by fixing an integer
K\in\mathbb{Z}
.
We then construct initial data
\psi(x,0)
for NLS
3
by flowing the semiclassical soliton ensemble
\widetilde{\psi}(x,t_{2},t_{3})
defined by
A(x)
under the
t_{2}
(NLS) flow to
t_{2}^{\circ}
, i.e., we set
\psi(x,0):=\widetilde{\psi}(x,t_{2}^{\circ},0)
.

Note that for each
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
\psi(x,0):=\widetilde{\psi}(x,t_{2}^{\circ},0)
is purely imaginary. Indeed, the effect of evaluation at
t_{2}=t_{2}^{\circ}
is to replace each coefficient
c_{n}^{0}
,
n=0,\dots,N-1
, in Riemann-Hilbert Problem
1
with
c_{n}^{0}\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}t_{2}^{\circ}(\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n})^{2}/\epsilon}
. Since
\Phi(\lambda)=\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{1}\lambda^{2}
, using (
2.26
) gives
2\mathrm{i}t_{2}^{\circ}(\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n})^{2}/\epsilon=2\pi\mathrm{i}(n+\frac{1}{2})t_{2}^{\circ}/\Phi_{1}-2\mathrm{i}t_{2}^{\circ}\Phi_{0}/(\epsilon\Phi_{1})
. Also, combining (
2.24
) and (
2.34
) gives
\Phi_{0}/\epsilon=N\pi
, so
2\mathrm{i}t_{2}^{\circ}(\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n})^{2}/\epsilon=2\pi\mathrm{i}(n-N+\frac{1}{2})t_{2}^{\circ}/\Phi_{1}
. Finally, using
t_{2}^{\circ}=-\frac{1}{2}(2K+1)\Phi_{1}
gives
2\mathrm{i}t_{2}^{\circ}(\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n})^{2}/\epsilon=-2\pi\mathrm{i}(n-N+\frac{1}{2})(K+\frac{1}{2})
, and hence
\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}t_{2}^{\circ}(\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n})^{2}/\epsilon}=(-1)^{n-N+K+1}\mathrm{i}
. Since
c_{n}^{0}
is purely imaginary,
c_{n}(x,t_{2}^{\circ},0)=c_{n}^{0}\mathrm{e}^{-2\widetilde{s}_{n}x/\epsilon}\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}t_{2}^{\circ}(\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n})^{2}/\epsilon}
is real for every
n=0,\dots,N-1
. It then follows from Proposition
5
that
\psi(x,0)=\widetilde{\psi}(x,t_{2}^{\circ},0)
is purely imaginary for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Since the mKdV equation in the form (
2.1
) preserves this property, we can write the solution for positive
t=t_{3}
in the form
\psi(x,t)=\mathrm{i}u(x,t)
where
u(x,t)
is a real-valued solution of the mKdV (NLS
3
) equation.
According to Theorem
2.4
, the solution
u(x,t)
undergoes a Suleimanov-Talanov focusing near the point
(x^{\circ},t_{3}^{\circ})
.
The results of numerical implementation of this procedure are shown in the first row of Figure
8
.

Next, we illustrate Theorem
2.4
for NLS
4
, following the same procedure starting with a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential
A(x)
given by (
2.112
) with phase and tail integrals given by (
2.117
);
the corresponding focus point from Theorem (
2.76
) is

(2.120)

(x^{\circ},t_{2}^{\circ},t_{3}^{\circ},t_{4}^{\circ})=\left(\frac{X_{+}+X_{-}}{2},-(2K+1)\frac{\pi(2-\gamma)(X_{+}-X_{-})}{16A_{\mathrm{max}}},0,-(2K+1)\frac{3\pi\gamma(X_{+}-X_{-})}{32A_{\mathrm{max}}^{3}}\right).

For the NLS
4
flow, we take as our initial condition
\psi(x,0)=\widetilde{\psi}(x,t_{2}^{\circ},t_{3}^{\circ},0)
where
\widetilde{\psi}
is the semiclassical soliton ensemble corresponding to
A(x)
given by (
2.112
). This initial condition is complex-valued. The NLS
4
numerics are shown in the second row of Figure
8
.

\psi(x,t_{4})
at the focusing time

.

6
.

Unlike the Talanov solutions of the dispersionless focusing NLS system (
1.3
) which may be viewed as the collapse of a modulated plane wave (genus
0
), here we observe that in the mKdV case for special initial data one has extreme-amplitude focusing and collapse instead of a
dispersive shock wave
, or a modulated genus-
1
structure. Likewise for the NLS
4
equation we have the focusing and collapse of a modulated genus-
2
structure.

2.5.
Nongeneric character of Suleimanov-Talanov focusing

Another way to view the unusual nature of Suleimanov-Talanov focusing, going beyond the fact that the semiclassical soliton ensemble for a given semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential
A(x)
has only been proved to exhibit this kind of asymptotic behavior at a discrete set of points in the multi-time space of the focusing NLS hierarchy (Theorem
2.77
) which will only be observed in
1+1
mixed-flow equations in the hierarchy that have just the right ratios of coefficients (Theorem
2.3
), is to illustrate how deformations of a Klaus-Shaw potential can easily perturb a Talanov-type infinite-amplitude singularity into an elliptic umblic gradient catastrophe for solutions of the dispersionless approximate system (
1.3
).

Here, we construct a family of smooth Klaus-Shaw potentials
A(x;\delta)
interpolating between initial data consistent with a Talanov solution with
E<0
for
\delta=0
and and that consistent with an Akhmanov-Sukhorukov-Khokhlov solution for
\delta=1
. We thus choose for the endpoints

(2.121)

A(x;0)=\sqrt{1-x^{2}}\chi_{[-1,1]}(x)\quad\text{and}\quad A(x;1)=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{sech}(x),

both of which have the same
L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
norm, namely
\pi/2
. We will interpolate between these two endpoints by constructing a Klaus-Shaw potential
A(x;\delta)
such that for each
\delta\in[0,1]
,

(2.122)

\|A(\diamond;\delta)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}=\Phi(0;\delta)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}A(x;\delta)\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{\pi}{2}.

The family of potentials
A(x;\delta)
will be constructed by explicitly interpolating the derivatives
\varrho(s)=\varrho(s;\delta)
of the corresponding phase integrals
\Phi(\mathrm{i}s;\delta)
corresponding to
A(x;\delta)
by (
2.25
). A direct computation shows that

(2.123)

\varrho(s;0)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\int_{x_{-}(s)}^{x_{+}(s)}\sqrt{1-x^{2}-s^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\oint_{L}R(x;s)\,\mathrm{d}x,

where
R(x;s)^{2}=x^{2}+s^{2}-1
and
R(x;s)
is cut in the interval
[x_{-}(s),x_{+}(s)]
with
R(x;s)=x+O(x^{-1})
as
x\to\infty
, and
L
is a positively-oriented loop enclosing the cut. By a residue at
x=\infty
we then obtain

(2.124)

\varrho(s;0)=s,\quad 0<s<1.

The function
\varrho(s;1)
corresponding to the other endpoint case of
A(x;1)
can also be calculated directly from (
2.25
). The calculation was done as an example in
[
16
]
, with the result being that

(2.125)

\varrho(s;1)=1,\quad 0<s<\frac{1}{2}.

Thus, we introduce coefficients
a(\delta)
and
b(\delta)
to be defined for
0<\delta<1
and assume a linear combination

(2.126)

\varrho(s;\delta)=a(\delta)+b(\delta)s,\quad 0<s<1-\frac{1}{2}\delta.

We require that
a(0)=b(1)=0
and that
b(0)=a(1)=1
to match the desired endpoints, and we obtain a relation between the functions
a(\delta)
and
b(\delta)
by imposing the condition (
2.122
). Using the connection between
\varrho(s)
and
\Phi(\mathrm{i}s)
in the definition (
2.27
), we are imposing that

(2.127)

\frac{1}{2}=\int_{0}^{1-\delta/2}(a(\delta)+b(\delta)s)\,\mathrm{d}s=a(\delta)\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta\right)+\frac{1}{2}b(\delta)\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta\right)^{2}.

It is straightforward to check that this relation is consistent with the boundary conditions on
a(\delta)
and
b(\delta)
at
\delta=0,1
. We may therefore supplement it with an arbitrary second independent equation that is also consistent with the boundary conditions; we choose to simply define
b(\delta):=1-\delta
. Then (
2.127
) determines
a(\delta)
explicitly, and we have determined that

(2.128)

a(\delta):=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta\right)^{-1}-\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta\right),\quad b(\delta):=1-\delta.

Having determined
\varrho(s;\delta)
in this way, we impose the condition that
x\mapsto A(x;\delta)
is an even function by choosing
\Xi(\mathrm{i}s;\delta)=0
for
s\in(0,1-\frac{1}{2}\delta)
for all
\delta\in[0,1]
(see Corollary
1
).

Now that we have
\varrho(s;\delta)
(and hence also
\Phi(\mathrm{i}s;\delta)
by (
2.27
)),
\Xi(\mathrm{i}s;\delta)
, and the maximum amplitude
A_{\mathrm{max}}(\delta)=1-\frac{1}{2}\delta
, we apply Proposition
2.40
to obtain the inverse functions
x_{\pm}(s;\delta)
of
s=A(x;\delta)
. We obtain

(2.129)

x_{+}(s;\delta)=-x_{-}(s;\delta)=\frac{a(\delta)}{2}\ln\left(\frac{1-\frac{1}{2}\delta+\sigma}{1-\frac{1}{2}\delta-\sigma}\right)+b(\delta)\sigma,

where
\sigma=\sigma(s;\delta)
given by

(2.130)

\sigma(s;\delta):=\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta\right)^{2}-s^{2}}

is a monotone decreasing function of
s
on
(0,1-\frac{1}{2}\delta)
. Note also that

(2.131)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\sigma}x_{+}(s;\delta)=\frac{(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta)a(\delta)}{(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta)^{2}-\sigma^{2}}+b(\delta)=\frac{(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta)a(\delta)}{s^{2}}+b(\delta).

Since
(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta)a(\delta)>0
and
b(\delta)>0
for
0<\delta<1
,
x_{+}(s;\delta)
is monotone increasing in
\sigma
and hence is a monotone decreasing function of
s\in(0,A_{\mathrm{max}}(\delta))
. This proves that
A(x;\delta)
is a Klaus-Shaw potential for all
\delta\in(0,1)
.

In
[
16
, Eqn. (6.56)]
, an implicit formula is given for the solution of the dispersionless focusing NLS system (
1.3
) with even initial data
\rho(x,0)=\rho(-x,0)
having zero initial momentum
\mu(x,0)\equiv 0
, along the symmetry axis
x=0
. That formula reads

(2.132)

t=\frac{1}{\rho}\int_{A_{\mathrm{max}}}^{\sqrt{\rho}>A_{\mathrm{max}}}\frac{s\varrho(s)\,\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{\rho-s^{2}}},\quad\rho=\rho(0,t),

which uses the analytic continuation of the function
\varrho(s)
beyond the right endpoint of the interval
(0,A_{\mathrm{max}})
of its definition. Substituting the
\delta
-dependent quantities
A_{\mathrm{max}}(\delta)
and
\varrho(s;\delta)
obtained above, the relation (
2.132
) becomes

(2.133)

t=\frac{\pi}{4}b(\delta)+\frac{1}{\rho}\left(a(\delta)+\frac{b(\delta)}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta\right)\right)\sqrt{\rho-\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta\right)^{2}}\\
{}-\frac{b(\delta)}{2}\arctan\left(\frac{1-\frac{1}{2}\delta}{\sqrt{\rho-(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta)^{2}}}\right).

Hence,

(2.134)

\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{\mathrm{d}\rho}=\frac{2(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta)^{2}a(\delta)+(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta)^{3}b(\delta)-a(\delta)\rho}{2\rho^{2}\sqrt{\rho-(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta)^{2}}}.

If
\delta=0
we are at the Talanov-type potential endpoint, and
a(\delta)=0
while
b(\delta)=1
. Hence also
\mathrm{d}t/\mathrm{d}\rho=1/(2\rho^{2}\sqrt{\rho-1})
which is positive for
\rho>A_{\mathrm{max}}(0)=1
and integrable at
\rho=+\infty
, so
t
increases to a finite positive limiting value of
\pi/4
as
\rho\to+\infty
. This corresponds of course to the finite-time blowup of the Talanov solution with
E<0
:
\rho(0,t)\uparrow+\infty
as
t\uparrow\frac{1}{4}\pi
. However, if
0<\delta\leq 1
we have
a(\delta)>0
, and hence
\mathrm{d}t/\mathrm{d}\rho
has a simple root at the finite value
\rho=\rho_{\mathrm{c}}(\delta)
given by

(2.135)

\rho_{\mathrm{c}}(\delta)=\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta\right)^{2}\left[2+\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta\right)\frac{b(\delta)}{a(\delta)}\right]\geq 2\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta\right)^{2}>\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta\right)^{2},

at which point
t
takes the corresponding value
t=t_{\mathrm{c}}(\delta)
obtained by evaluating the right-hand side of (
2.133
) at
\rho=\rho_{\mathrm{c}}(\delta)
.
We have
\mathrm{d}t/\mathrm{d}\rho>0
for
(1-\frac{1}{2}\delta)^{2}<\rho<\rho_{\mathrm{c}}(\delta)
, but
t
has a simple critical point at
\rho=\rho_{\mathrm{c}}(\delta)
, a nondegenerate local maximum. Hence for the direct function
\rho=\rho(0,t)
we have a finite-amplitude gradient catastrophe point at
t=t_{\mathrm{c}}(\delta)
with finite value
\rho=\rho_{\mathrm{c}}(\delta)
. At the endpoint
\delta=1
we recover the expected result that
t_{\mathrm{c}}(1)=1
and
\rho_{\mathrm{c}}(1)=\frac{1}{2}
, consistent with the prediction of the Akhmanov-Sukhorukov-Khokhlov solution reviewed in §
1
.

This calculation shows that an arbitrarily small perturbation measured by
\delta>0
of a Talanov-type initial condition destroys the infinite-amplitude focusing, replacing it instead with an elliptic umbilic gradient catastrophe point. This result suggests a form of nongenericity of Talanov-type infinite-amplitude focusing, however it is a challenge to properly formulate this because the space of admissible perturbations of initial data should make sense for the dispersionless focusing NLS system (
1.3
), which is generally of elliptic type, except where
\rho=0
.

Taken together with the prediction of Suleimanov
[
27
]
for Talanov pulses with
E=0
and the Bertola-Tovbis result
[
3
]
for data leading to an elliptic-umbilic catastrophe, our work on Talanov pulses with
E<0
(or more properly the corresponding semiclassical soliton ensembles) adds further evidence that
the scenario that prevails in terms of the type and scale of the dispersive regularization is determined primarily by the nature of the singularity in the dispersionless solution. If it is an elliptic-umbilic catastrophe (finite amplitude gradient catastrophe), then one has Bertola-Tovbis regularization based on the Painlevé-I tritronquée solution and a field of Peregrine rogue waves at locations corresponding to the poles of the tritronquée solution. If it is an infinite-amplitude collapse and blow up, then one has instead Suleimanov-Talanov focusing based on the Painlevé-III hierarchy of Sakka. This rubric seems clearer than that proposed in the recent work
[
13
]
. Note that the quantity denoted
\alpha_{0}^{2}+4\gamma_{0}
in
[
13
]
is proportional by a positive quantity to the integration constant
E
.

3.
General semicircular Klaus-Shaw potentials

3.1.
Basic quantities in the semiclassical direct scattering theory for Klaus-Shaw potentials

For a potential
A
with the Klaus-Shaw property (see Definition
1
), we may define the following quantities:

(3.1)

L(\lambda):=-\ell^{-}(+\infty;\lambda)=-\ell^{+}(-\infty;\lambda)

for suitable
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
(see Lemma
1
below), wherein with the square root denoting the principal branch,

(3.2)

\begin{split}\ell^{-}(x;\lambda)&:=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\left[\left(-\lambda^{2}-A(y)^{2}\right)^{1/2}+\mathrm{i}\lambda\right]\,\mathrm{d}y,\\
\ell^{+}(x;\lambda)&:=\int_{x}^{+\infty}\left[\left(-\lambda^{2}-A(y)^{2}\right)^{1/2}+\mathrm{i}\lambda\right]\,\mathrm{d}y.\end{split}

First, we give an alternate formula for
L(\lambda)
.

1
.

L(\lambda)
defined by (
3.1
) is an analytic function of
\lambda
in the upper half-plane with the imaginary segment
0<-\mathrm{i}\lambda\leq A_{\mathrm{max}}
omitted, and it satisfies the asymptotic condition
L(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{-1}\right)
as
\lambda\to\infty
in the upper half-plane. Also,
L(\lambda)
is equivalently given by

(3.3)

L(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}}\left[\log(-\mathrm{i}\lambda+s)-\log(-\mathrm{i}\lambda-s)\right]\varrho(s)\mathrm{d}s,

where
\log
denotes the principal branch and
\varrho(s)
is defined by (
2.25
), and its boundary values on the branch cut satisfy the jump condition

(3.4)

\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\left[L(\mathrm{i}s+\delta)-L(\mathrm{i}s-\delta)\right]=2\mathrm{i}\Phi(\mathrm{i}s),\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}},

where
\Phi(\mathrm{i}s)
is the phase integral defined by (
2.27
).

Proof.

The claimed domain of analyticity of
L(\lambda)
and the fact that
L(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{-1}\right)
for large
\lambda
are obvious from the formula (
3.1
) and the definition (
3.2
).

Let
\hat{L}(\lambda)
denote the function defined by the right-hand side of (
3.3
).
Replacing
\varrho(s)
by the first expression in the definition (
2.25
), we first exchange the order of integration to obtain

(3.5)

\begin{split}\hat{L}(\lambda)&=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}}\int_{x_{-}(s)}^{x_{+}(s)}\left[\log(-\mathrm{i}\lambda+s)-\log(-\mathrm{i}\lambda-s)\right]\frac{s}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}I(x)\,\mathrm{d}x,\end{split}

where the inner integral over
s
is

(3.6)

I(x):=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{A(x)}\left[\log(-\mathrm{i}\lambda+s)-\log(-\mathrm{i}\lambda-s)\right]\frac{s}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}s.

We may now evaluate
I(x)
explicitly. First, observe that the integrand is an even function of
s
, so

(3.7)

I(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-A(x)}^{A(x)}\left[\log(-\mathrm{i}\lambda+s)-\log(-\mathrm{i}\lambda-s)\right]\frac{s\,\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}}.

Now let
S(s)
denote the (odd) function analytic in the domain
\mathbb{C}\setminus[-A(x),A(x)]
that satisfies the equation
S(s)^{2}=A(x)^{2}-s^{2}
and the normalization (choice of branch)
S(s)=\mathrm{i}s+\mathcal{O}\!\left(s^{-1}\right)
as
s\to\infty
in the complex plane. Then, by the generalized Cauchy integral theorem,

(3.8)

I(x)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\oint_{C}\left[\log(-\mathrm{i}\lambda+s)-\log(-\mathrm{i}\lambda-s)\right]\frac{s\,\mathrm{d}s}{S(s)},

where
C
is a positively-oriented loop surrounding the branch cut of
S
for which the non-real horizontal branch cuts of the logarithms are in the unbounded exterior. Now using
s/S(s)=-S^{\prime}(s)
, we integrate by parts to obtain

(3.9)

I(x)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\oint_{C}\left[\frac{1}{s-\mathrm{i}\lambda}-\frac{1}{s+\mathrm{i}\lambda}\right]S(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

Expanding the contour
C
toward
s=\infty
we may now evaluate
I(x)
by residues at
s=\pm\mathrm{i}\lambda
and
s=\infty
:

(3.10)

I(x)=-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{i}S(\mathrm{i}\lambda)+\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{i}S(-\mathrm{i}\lambda)-\mathrm{i}\lambda=-\mathrm{i}S(\mathrm{i}\lambda)-\mathrm{i}\lambda.

Comparing with (
3.1
)–(
3.2
), to show
\hat{L}(\lambda)=L(\lambda)
and thus complete the proof of the formula (
3.3
), it remains to identify
\mathrm{i}S(\mathrm{i}\lambda)
with the principal branch square root
(-\lambda^{2}-A(x)^{2})^{1/2}
as is valid for all
\lambda
in the upper half-plane.

Finally, to confirm the jump condition (
3.4
), observe from (
3.3
) that for
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
,

(3.11)

\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\left[L(\mathrm{i}s+\delta)-L(\mathrm{i}s-\delta)\right]=\int_{s}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}}2\pi\mathrm{i}\varrho(s^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}s^{\prime},

which equals
2\mathrm{i}\Phi(\mathrm{i}s)
according to (
2.27
).
∎

Now recalling the numbers
\{\widetilde{s}_{n}\}_{n=0}^{N-1}
determined from (
2.26
) and letting

(3.12)

\widetilde{a}(\lambda):=\prod_{n=0}^{N-1}\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}}{\lambda+\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}},

we introduce a function
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
given by

(3.13)

Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda):=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-L(\lambda)/\epsilon}}{\widetilde{a}(\lambda)}

defining a function analytic for all complex
\lambda
with the vertical segment connecting
-\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}}
with
\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}}
omitted.
Looking at the formula (
3.3
), and taking into account the definition (
3.12
) of
\widetilde{a}(\lambda)
as a Blaschke product, one gets the idea that
\epsilon\log(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda))
looks like the error in an approximation of the integral
L(\lambda)
by a Riemann sum.
By this reasoning, for some Klaus-Shaw potentials
A(x)
,
\log(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda))
has been shown to be as small as
\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)
for suitable
\lambda
. In the case of semicircular Klaus-Shaw potentials
A(x)
considered in this paper, we will show below (cf. Proposition
6
) that for such
\lambda
,
\log(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda))=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)
(and compute the leading term).

Recalling the domain of analyticity of
L(\lambda)
, whose boundary values on the cut
0<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A_{\mathrm{max}}
are related according to (
3.4
) by
L_{+}(\lambda)-L_{-}(\lambda)=2\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda)
where the subscript
\pm
indicates the limit from
\pm\re\{\lambda\}>0
, we see that the function
\overline{L}(\lambda)
defined by

(3.14)

\overline{L}(\lambda):=L(\lambda)\mp\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda),\quad 0<\imag\{\lambda\}<A_{\mathrm{max}},\quad\lambda\in\Omega,\quad\text{and}\quad\pm\re\{\lambda\}>0,

where
\Omega
is the domain of analyticity of the phase integral
\Phi(\lambda)
,
is analytic where defined and moreover is continuous across the cut, therefore
actually defining an analytic function on the part of
\Omega
with
0<\imag\{\lambda\}<A_{\mathrm{max}}
.
For
0<\mathrm{i}\lambda<A_{\mathrm{max}}
, this function coincides with the average of the boundary values taken by
L(\lambda)

(cf. (
4.2
) below),
which explains our use of the “bar” notation.
Then, a function closely related to
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
is

(3.15)

T_{\epsilon}(\lambda):=\frac{2\cos(\Phi(\lambda)/\epsilon)\mathrm{e}^{-\overline{L}(\lambda)/\epsilon}}{\widetilde{a}(\lambda)},\quad 0<\imag\{\lambda\}<A_{\mathrm{max}}\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda\in\Omega,

which, according to (
2.26
) has removable singularities at the zeros of
\widetilde{a}(\lambda)
along the positive imaginary axis. The relation between
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
and
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
is given by the exact identities

(3.16)

T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)(1+\mathrm{e}^{\pm 2\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda)/\epsilon}),\quad 0<\imag\{\lambda\}<A_{\mathrm{max}},\quad\lambda\in\Omega,\quad\text{and}\quad\pm\re\{\lambda\}>0.

Assuming that
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=1+o(1)
for
\lambda
bounded away from the branch cut of
Y_{\epsilon}
, a Cauchy-Riemann argument shows that
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=1+o(1)
holds for
\lambda
to the left or right of the segment
0<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A_{\mathrm{max}}
. It was first noticed in
[
21
]
, and further explained in
[
2
]
and
[
20
]
, that under some conditions a similar estimate of
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
holds uniformly on compact subsets of its domain of definition, including points
\lambda
with
0<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A_{\mathrm{max}}
. We will prove the version of this result applicable to semicircular Klaus-Shaw potentials
A(x)
(see Definition
1
) below.

3.2.
Semicircular Klaus-Shaw potentials. Asymptotic properties of
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
and
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)

In this section we record several properties of the functions
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
and
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
that are needed in the steepest-descent analysis that follows. To streamline the presentation, propositions with longer more technical proofs are deferred to Appendix
A
.

Recall Proposition
1
.
Now for
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
, consider

(3.17)

r:=\frac{1}{\pi}(\Phi(0)-\Phi(\mathrm{i}s))\quad\text{with inverse}\quad s=s(r).

The inverse function here is well-defined because
\varrho(s)>0
, and we have the following result.

2
.

Let
A
be a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential. Then
s(r)
is analytic on the open interval
0<r<\Phi(0)/\pi
, and furthermore

(3.18)

s(r)=\sqrt{r}v(r),\quad 0<r<\frac{\Phi(0)}{\pi},

where
v(r)
is analytic at
r=0
:

(3.19)

v(r)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}v_{k}r^{k},\quad|r|<\sigma,\quad v_{0}>0.

Also,
s
is also analytic at
r=\Phi(0)/\pi
and

s^{\prime}(\Phi(0)/\pi)>0
.

7
.

In the case that
A(x)=\psi_{0}(x)
is a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential for which
u(x)\equiv\text{const.}
, it is straightforward to use (
2.81
) to obtain

(3.20)

r=\frac{X_{+}-X_{-}}{4A_{\mathrm{max}}}s^{2}\implies s=\sqrt{r}v(r),\quad v(r)\equiv v_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{4A_{\mathrm{max}}}{X_{+}-X_{-}}}>0.

In particular,
v_{k}=0
for all
k>0
in (
3.19
).

Proof.

Analyticity of
s(r)
on the open interval
0<r<\Phi(0)/\pi
follows from the implicit function theorem because
\Phi(\mathrm{i}s)
is analytic for
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
and
\varrho(s)>0
holds for each point in this open interval.
For a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential
A
, it follows from (
3.17
) and Proposition
1
that

r
is an even analytic function of
s
at
s=0
, with
r=0
for
s=0
:

(3.21)

r=-\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\Phi_{k}(-1)^{k}s^{2k},\quad|s|<\delta

for some radius of convergence
\delta>0
. Since
\Phi_{2}>0
, it follows from the implicit function theorem that

(3.22)

s(r)^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\phi_{k}r^{k},\quad|r|<\sigma

for some radius of convergence
\sigma>0
, and where
\phi_{1}>0
. Taking a positive square root then yields (
3.18
) with (
3.19
).

To study
s(r)
for
r
near
\Phi(0)/\pi
, we
write
\varrho(s)
as a contour integral:

(3.23)

\varrho(s)=\frac{s}{2\pi}\oint_{L}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{R(x;s^{2})}

and note that under the stated condition on
A^{\prime\prime}
, as
s\uparrow A_{\mathrm{max}}
exactly two roots of
R(x;s^{2})
coalesce at
x=x_{0}
. This implies that
L
can be chosen so that
R(x;A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2})^{-1}
is meromorphic within
L
having a single pole at
x=x_{0}
with residue
-\mathrm{i}/\sqrt{-A_{\mathrm{max}}A^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})}
. Thus
r^{\prime}(A_{\mathrm{max}})=\varrho(A_{\mathrm{max}})>0
, from which it follows that
s^{\prime}(\Phi(0)/\pi)>0
holds for the inverse function.
∎

3.2.1.
Analysis of
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
in the limit
\epsilon\downarrow 0

The first result concerns the asymptotic behavior of the function
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
for
\lambda
suitably bounded away from the branch cut of
Y_{\epsilon}
. For some small width parameter
\delta>0
, let
\Lambda
denote the thin “parabolic” lens centered on the imaginary segment
0<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A_{\mathrm{max}}
consisting of the points

(3.24)

\Lambda:=\left\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\,:\,|\re\{\lambda\}|\leq\delta\imag\{\lambda\}(A_{\mathrm{max}}-\imag\{\lambda\}),\ 0\leq\imag\{\lambda\}\leq A_{\mathrm{max}}\right\}.

6
Exterior asymptotic behavior of
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
.

Suppose that
A
is a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential.
For arbitrary
\sigma>0
, let
D_{\sigma}
denote the domain defined by
D_{\sigma}:=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}\setminus\Lambda:|\lambda|>\sigma,\;|\lambda-\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}}|>\sigma\}
. Then

(3.25)

Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=1-\frac{2\mathrm{i}v_{0}}{\lambda}\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2})\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right),\quad\epsilon\downarrow 0,

where
\zeta(\cdot)
denotes the Riemann zeta function and
v_{0}>0
denotes the constant defined in Lemma
2
, holds uniformly for
\lambda\in D_{\sigma}
.

Proof.

The proof
can be found in Appendix
A
.
∎

Note that the explicit term proportional to
\epsilon^{1/2}
is not present in a result proven for a function similar to
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
in
[
2
, Proposition 4.3]
. This discrepancy can be directly traced to the fact that the function
\rho(s)
here vanishes linearly at
s=0
while
its analogue in
[
2
]
is bounded away from zero at the origin, as the proof will show.

The domain
D_{\sigma}
obviously excludes small values of
\lambda
. The next result describes the different way that
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
behaves for
|\lambda|
small. Significantly, an approximation accurate in the sense of small relative error can be obtained without excluding
\lambda
from the lens
\Lambda
, i.e., the following result allows
\lambda
to lie among the singularities of
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
.

7
Asymptotic behavior of
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
for
\lambda\approx 0
.

Suppose that
A
is a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential.
Then

(3.26)

Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=\mathcal{Y}_{0}\left(\frac{\varphi_{0}(\lambda)}{\epsilon^{1/2}}\right)\left(1+\mathcal{E}_{0}(\lambda)\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right),\quad\epsilon\downarrow 0,\quad\varphi_{0}(\lambda):=\mathrm{i}\pi^{-1/2}(\Phi(0)-\Phi(\lambda))^{1/2}

holds uniformly for
\imag\{\lambda\}>0
and
|\lambda|
sufficiently small,
where the mapping
\lambda\mapsto\varphi_{0}(\lambda)
is conformal near
\lambda=0
with
\varphi_{0}(0)=0
and
\varphi_{0}^{\prime}(0)=1/v(0)>0
, and where

(3.27)

\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z):=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\pi Z^{2}\mathrm{sgn}(\re\{Z\})}\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\displaystyle\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}-\mathrm{i}Z}{\displaystyle\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}+\mathrm{i}Z}\mathrm{e}^{4\mathrm{i}Z(\sqrt{n+1}-\sqrt{n})},\quad\text{and}

(3.28)

\mathcal{E}_{0}(\lambda):=-2\mathrm{i}\frac{\varphi_{0}(\lambda)}{v(-\varphi_{0}(\lambda)^{2})}\frac{v(-\varphi_{0}(\lambda)^{2})-v(0)}{-\varphi_{0}(\lambda)^{2}}\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2}).

Also, the error terms in (
3.26
) proportional to
\epsilon^{1/2}
and
\epsilon
both vanish identically in the limit
\lambda\to 0
. In fact,
\mathcal{E}_{0}(\lambda)
is analytic at
\lambda=0
and
\mathcal{E}_{0}(\lambda)=O(\lambda)
.

Proof.

The proof
can be found in Appendix
A
. ∎

8
.

Note that for the semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential with constant
u(x)
, given by
A(x)=\psi(x,0)
defined by (
2.22
), Remark
7
gives
v(r)\equiv v(0)
, so
\mathcal{E}_{0}(\lambda)
vanishes identically in this case.

The model function
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)
defined by (
3.27
) is meromorphic in
Z
in the right and left
Z
-planes but has a jump discontinuity across the imaginary
Z
-axis, which corresponds to the imaginary
\lambda
-axis near
\lambda=0
. The form of this model function is quite different from that which can be obtained (also with a smaller relative error) under the assumption that
\rho(s)
does not vanish at
s=0

[
2
, Proposition 4.3]
. While we are unable to express
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)
in closed form, we can easily see that

\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\pi Z^{2}\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{Re}\{Z\})}
is meromorphic with simple zeros at
Z=-\mathrm{i}\sqrt{n+\frac{1}{2}}
and simple poles at
Z=\mathrm{i}\sqrt{n+\frac{1}{2}}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, a phenomenon that locally captures the features of the factor
\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}
appearing in the definition (
3.13
) of
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
. Although
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)
appears to be difficult to analyze directly from its definition in terms of an infinite product, we can easily prove the following.

8
Behavior of
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)
for small and large
Z
.

As
Z\to 0
,
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)=1-2\mathrm{i}(\sqrt{2}-1)\zeta(\frac{1}{2})Z+\mathcal{O}\!\left(Z^{2}\right)
where the error term has a jump discontinuity across the imaginary axis in the
Z
-plane.
Also, for each small
\delta>0
,
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)=1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(Z^{-1}\right)
as
Z\to\infty
uniformly for
0\leq\arg(Z)\leq\pi/2-\delta
and for
\pi/2+\delta\leq\arg(Z)\leq\pi
.

Proof.

The proof
can be found in Appendix
A
.
∎

Finally, we have the following result, describing the behavior of
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
for
\lambda
near
\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}}
in the limit
\epsilon\downarrow 0
. As with Proposition
7
,
the relative error terms are controlled even if
\lambda
is near the imaginary axis, i.e., among the poles of
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
.

9
Asymptotic behavior of
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
for
\lambda\approx\mathrm{i}A_{\max}
.

Suppose that
A
is a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential.
Then
the asymptotic formula

(3.29)

Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=\mathcal{Y}_{1}\left(\frac{\varphi_{1}(\lambda)}{\epsilon}\right)\left(1+\mathcal{E}_{1}(\lambda)\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\left(\epsilon\right)\right),\quad\epsilon\downarrow 0,\quad\varphi_{1}(\lambda):=-\frac{\Phi(\lambda)}{\pi}

holds uniformly for
|\lambda-\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}}|
sufficiently small, where the mapping
\lambda\mapsto\varphi_{1}(\lambda)
is conformal near
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}}
with
\varphi_{1}(\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}})=0
and
\varphi_{1}^{\prime}(\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}})
negative imaginary, and where

(3.30)

\mathcal{Y}_{1}(W):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}W^{-W}\Gamma(W+\tfrac{1}{2})\mathrm{e}^{W}

and

(3.31)

\mathcal{E}_{1}(\lambda):=-\frac{2\mathrm{i}v_{0}}{\lambda}\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2}).

Proof.

The proof
can be found in Appendix
A
.
∎

9
.

The leading term of the relative error is exactly the same as in Proposition
6
.

The model function defined by (
3.30
) is very similar to that obtained in a similar situation in
[
2
, Proposition 4.3]
, but the error term is larger due to the effect of
\varrho(s)
vanishing linearly at
s=0
. Note that in (
3.30
),
W^{-W}
is the principal branch, and hence
\mathcal{Y}_{1}(W)
has a branch cut across the negative real
W
-axis, which corresponds to the imaginary
\lambda
-axis below the point
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}}
.

3.2.2.
Analysis of
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
in the limit
\epsilon\downarrow 0

We first give the analogue for
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
of Proposition
6
. The function
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
is analytic for
\lambda\in\Lambda
(unlike
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
, which has poles and a branch cut along the center line of the lens
\Lambda
) and the following result shows that its asymptotic behavior in this domain is simplest for
\lambda
suitably bounded away from the points
\lambda=0
and
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}}
.

10
Basic asymptotic behavior of
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
for
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

Suppose that
A
is a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential.
Let
\sigma>0
be arbitrary and define
\Lambda_{\sigma}:=\{\lambda\in\Lambda:|\lambda|>\sigma,\;|\lambda-\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}}|>\sigma\}
.
Then

(3.32)

T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=1-\frac{2\mathrm{i}v_{0}}{\lambda}\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2})\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right),\quad\epsilon\downarrow 0

holds uniformly for
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\sigma}
.

Proof.

The proof
can be found in Appendix
A
.
∎

Next, we can easily obtain analogues of Propositions
7
and
3.31
with the help of the exact relation (
3.16
) between
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
and
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
.

11
Asymptotic behavior of
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
for
\lambda\approx 0
.

Suppose that
A
is a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential.
Then

(3.33)

T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=\mathcal{T}_{0}\left(\frac{\varphi_{0}(\lambda)}{\epsilon^{1/2}}\right)\left(1+\mathcal{E}_{0}(\lambda)\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right),\quad\epsilon\downarrow 0,\quad\varphi_{0}(\lambda):=\mathrm{i}\pi^{-1/2}(\Phi(0)-\Phi(\lambda))^{1/2}

holds uniformly for
\imag\{\lambda\}>0
and
|\lambda|
sufficiently small, where the mapping
\lambda\mapsto\varphi_{0}(\lambda)
is conformal near
\lambda=0
with
\varphi_{0}(0)=0
and
\varphi_{0}^{\prime}(0)=1/v(0)>0
,
\mathcal{E}_{0}(\lambda)
is defined by (
3.28
), and where

(3.34)

\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z):=2\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(1-\frac{\mathrm{i}Z}{\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}}\right)^{2}\left(1-\frac{Z^{2}}{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{4\mathrm{i}Z(\sqrt{n+1}-\sqrt{n})}.

Also, the error terms in (
3.33
) proportional to
\epsilon^{1/2}
and
\epsilon
both vanish identically in the limit
\lambda\to 0
.

Proof.

The proof
can be found in Appendix
A
.
∎

The behavior of
\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)
for small and large
Z
is as follows.

12
Behavior of
\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)
for small and large
Z
.

\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)
is analytic at
Z=0
with Taylor expansion
\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)=2-4\mathrm{i}(\sqrt{2}-1)\zeta(\tfrac{1}{2})Z+\mathcal{O}\!\left(Z^{2}\right)
as
Z\to 0
.
Also, for each small
\delta>0
,
\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)=1-2\mathrm{i}(1-\sqrt{2})\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2})Z^{-1}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(Z^{-2}\right)
as
Z\to\infty
uniformly for
|\arg(-\mathrm{i}Z)|\leq\delta
.

Proof.

The proof
can be found in Appendix
A
.
∎

13
Asymptotic behavior of
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
for
\lambda\approx\mathrm{i}A_{\max}
.

Suppose that
A
is a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential.
Then
the asymptotic formula

(3.35)

T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=\mathcal{T}_{1}\left(\frac{\varphi_{1}(\lambda)}{\epsilon}\right)\left(1+\mathcal{E}_{1}(\lambda)+\mathcal{O}\left(\epsilon\right)\right),\quad\epsilon\downarrow 0,\quad\varphi_{1}(\lambda):=-\frac{\Phi(\lambda)}{\pi}

holds uniformly for
|\lambda-\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}}|
sufficiently small, where the mapping
\lambda\mapsto\varphi_{1}(\lambda)
is conformal near
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}}
with
\varphi_{1}(\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}})=0
and
\varphi_{1}^{\prime}(\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}})
negative imaginary, and where

(3.36)

\mathcal{T}_{1}(W):=\frac{\sqrt{2\pi}\mathrm{e}^{W}(-W)^{-W}}{\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{2}-W)},

and where
\mathcal{E}_{1}(\lambda)
is defined in (
3.31
).

Proof.

The proof
can be found in Appendix
A
.
∎

4.
Proof of Theorem
2.1
for
x\in(X_{-},X_{+})\setminus\{x_{0}\}

In this section we apply the Deift-Zhou steepest-descent method to prove Theorem
2.1
for
x\in(X_{-},X_{+})\setminus\{x_{0}\}
, that is for
x
inside the support of the initial data
A(x)
and away from its unique maximizer
x=x_{0}
.
The analysis is different for
x
in one or the other of the open intervals

(4.1)

J^{-}:=(X_{-},x_{0}),\qquad J^{+}:=(x_{0},X_{+}).

For technical reasons we do not consider the case when
x=x_{0}
, though our numerics (see Figure
3
) suggest nothing interesting happens near the maximizer.
In Section
4.1
we construct the
g
-functions
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
which we will use for the steepest-descent analysis for
x\in J^{\pm}
. We then describe the remaining analysis for
x\in J^{+}
in Sections
4.2
-
4.4
. The modifications necessary to extend the proof to
x\in J^{-}
are then described briefly in Section
4.5
. Finally, in Section
4.6
we combine the results to complete the proof of Theorem
2.1
for
x\in(X_{-},X_{+})\setminus\{x_{0}\}
.

4.1.
Two
g
-functions for inverse scattering

Let
A
be a potential with the Klaus-Shaw property (see Definition
1
).
Note that for
L(\lambda)
defined by (
3.1
), we may consider a point
\lambda=\mathrm{i}s
,
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
and compute the average of the boundary values taken on the branch cut at this point:

(4.2)

\overline{L}(\mathrm{i}s):=\frac{1}{2}\left(L_{+}(\mathrm{i}s)+L_{-}(\mathrm{i}s)\right)\\
=(x_{+}(s)-x_{-}(s))s-\int_{-\infty}^{x_{-}(s)}\left[\sqrt{s^{2}-A(x)^{2}}-s\right]\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{x_{+}(s)}^{+\infty}\left[\sqrt{s^{2}-A(x)^{2}}-s\right]\,\mathrm{d}x.

Comparing with the definition (
2.29
) of
\Xi(\mathrm{i}s)
, we see that

(4.3)

\Xi(\mathrm{i}s)\pm\overline{L}(\mathrm{i}s)=2x_{\pm}(s)s-2\int_{x_{\pm}(s)}^{\pm\infty}\left[\sqrt{s^{2}-A(x)^{2}}-s\right]\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}.

3
.

Let
A:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
be a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential with support
[X_{-},X_{+}]
(see Definition
1
). Then

(4.4)

\Xi(\mathrm{i}s)\pm\overline{L}(\mathrm{i}s)=2X_{\pm}s-2\int_{x_{\pm}(s)}^{X_{\pm}}\sqrt{s^{2}-A(x)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}.

Furthermore,
\Xi(\mathrm{i}s)\pm\overline{L}(\mathrm{i}s)
is analytic on
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
and extends to an odd analytic function of
s
in a neighborhood of
s=0
satisfying
\Xi(\mathrm{i}s)\pm\overline{L}(\mathrm{i}s)=2X_{\pm}s+\mathcal{O}\!\left(s^{3}\right)
. Also for
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
,
\mp(\Xi(\mathrm{i}s)\pm\overline{L}(\mathrm{i}s))\geq\mp 2X_{\pm}s
.

Proof.

Analyticity on
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
follows from analyticity of
A(x)
within its support, which also implies the analyticity of the turning point functions
x_{\pm}(s)
on
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
. Equation
4.4

follows since
A(x)
has compact support
[X_{-},X_{+}]
.
Since for
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
it holds that
x_{\pm}(s)\in(X_{-},X_{+})
, it follows that the integral may be dropped to obtain the inequality
\mp(\Xi(\mathrm{i}s)\pm\overline{L}(\mathrm{i}s))>2X_{\pm}s
.
Now observe that due to the Klaus-Shaw condition,
A(x)^{2}
is monotone on the interval of integration, and hence the inverse function
x(y)
satisfying
y=A(x)^{2}
is well-defined. Therefore, making the substitution
x=x(y)
and rescaling by
y=s^{2}z
gives

(4.5)

\begin{split}\Xi(\mathrm{i}s)\pm\overline{L}(\mathrm{i}s)&=2X_{\pm}s+2\int_{0}^{s^{2}}\sqrt{s^{2}-y}\,x^{\prime}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y\\
&=2X_{\pm}s+2s^{3}\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{1-z}\,x^{\prime}(s^{2}z)\,\mathrm{d}z,\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}.\end{split}

Taking into account the representation
A(x)^{2}=u(x)^{2}(X_{+}-x)(x-X_{-})
on its support, with
u(x)
being a positive analytic function on a complex neighborhood of
[X_{-},X_{+}]
, it is obvious that
y=A(x)^{2}
can be considered to be a univalent function on a neighborhood of each of the support endpoints
X_{\pm}
. Therefore,
x^{\prime}(y)
is an analytic function of
y
near
y=0
and hence the right-hand side of (
4.5
) is an odd analytic function of
s
near
s=0
.
∎

Let

(4.6)

R(\lambda;x)=\sqrt{\lambda^{2}+A(x)^{2}}

be the function analytic for
\lambda
in the complex plane with a vertical cut between
\pm\mathrm{i}A(x)
omitted that satisfies
R(\lambda;x)=\lambda+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{-1}\right)
as
\lambda\to\infty
. Consider the function
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
defined in the same domain as
R(\lambda;x)
by the formula

\displaystyle g^{\pm}(\lambda;x):=

\displaystyle\,\frac{R(\lambda;x)}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\left[\int_{0}^{A(x)}\frac{2xs-\Xi(\mathrm{i}s)\mp\overline{L}(\mathrm{i}s)}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}(s+\mathrm{i}\lambda)}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{A(x)}\frac{2xs-\Xi(\mathrm{i}s)\mp\overline{L}(\mathrm{i}s)}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}(s-\mathrm{i}\lambda)}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]

(4.7)

\displaystyle{}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{R(\lambda;x)}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{0}^{A(x)}\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}(s^{2}+\lambda^{2})},

where

(4.8)

\phi^{\pm}(s;x):=2s\cdot(2sx-\Xi(\mathrm{i}s)\mp\overline{L}(\mathrm{i}s)),\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}.

According to the identities (
4.3
),
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
is a kind of integral transform of the turning point function
x_{\pm}(\cdot)
. If
A(\cdot)
is a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential (cf. Definition
1
) then by Lemma
3
,
\phi^{\pm}(s;x)
may be uniquely extended to a complex neighborhood of
[-A(x),A(x)]
as an even analytic function of
s
(which we also denote by
\phi^{\pm}(s;x)
), in which case we can write
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
in the form

(4.9)

\begin{split}g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)&=\frac{R(\lambda;x)}{4\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{-A(x)}^{A(x)}\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}(s^{2}+\lambda^{2})}\\
&=\frac{R(\lambda;x)}{8\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{L}\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{R(\mathrm{i}s;x)(s^{2}+\lambda^{2})},\quad\text{$\mathrm{i}\lambda$ and $-\mathrm{i}\lambda$ exterior to $L$},\end{split}

where
L
is a simple closed curve in the domain of analyticity of
\phi^{\pm}(s;x)
that encircles the interval
[-A(x),A(x)]

once in the positive sense. If we want to allow
\mathrm{i}\lambda
and
-\mathrm{i}\lambda
to approach the interval
[-A(x),A(x)]
, we can pay the price of two residues
and obtain (using oddness of
R(\diamond;x)
and evenness of
\phi^{\pm}(\cdot;x)
)

(4.10)

g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)=\frac{\phi^{\pm}(-\mathrm{i}\lambda;x)}{4\mathrm{i}\lambda}+\frac{R(\lambda;x)}{8\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{L}\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{R(\mathrm{i}s;x)(s^{2}+\lambda^{2})},\quad\text{$\mathrm{i}\lambda$ and $-\mathrm{i}\lambda$ interior to $L$}.

14
.

Let
A(\cdot)
be a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential with support
[X_{-},X_{+}]
and maximizer
x_{0}
. The function
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
, for
x\in[X_{-},X_{+}]
, has the following properties.

G1:

g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
is analytic and uniformly bounded in its domain of definition.

G2:

g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
