is an odd function of
\lambda
.

G3:

g^{\pm}(\lambda^{*};x)=-g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)^{*}
, and in particular
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
is imaginary for real
\lambda\neq 0
.

G4:

The sum of boundary values taken by
g^{\pm}
on its branch cut satisfies

(4.11)

g_{+}^{\pm}(\mathrm{i}s;x)+g_{-}^{\pm}(\mathrm{i}s;x)=-2sx+\Xi(\mathrm{i}s)\pm\overline{L}(\mathrm{i}s),\quad 0<s<A(x).

G5:

g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{-1}\right)
as
\lambda\to\infty
.

G6:

If
\pm(x-x_{0})>0
, then there exist analytic functions
\lambda\mapsto G^{\pm}_{1}(\lambda;x)
and
\lambda\mapsto G^{\pm}_{2}(\lambda;x)
defined in a neighborhood
D(x)
of
\mathrm{i}A(x)
such that

(4.12)

g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)=G_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda;x)+(-\mathrm{i}\lambda-A(x))^{3/2}G_{2}^{\pm}(\lambda;x)

holds for
\lambda\in D(x)\setminus B(x)
, where
B(x)
denotes the branch cut of
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
.

G7:

g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)\to 0
as
x\downarrow X_{-}
or
x\uparrow X_{+}
.

G8:

The partial derivative of
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
with respect to
x
is given explicitly by
g^{\pm}_{x}(\lambda;x)=\mathrm{i}(\lambda-R(\lambda;x))
.

G9:

We have the identities

(4.13)

g^{+}(\lambda;x)=-\mathrm{i}\int_{x}^{X_{+}}(\lambda-R(\lambda;y))\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad g^{-}(\lambda;x)=\mathrm{i}\int_{X_{-}}^{x}(\lambda-R(\lambda;y))\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad x\in[X_{-},X_{+}].

Recalling the function
L(\lambda)
defined by (
3.1
)–(
3.2
), we also have the identity

(4.14)

L(\lambda)-g^{+}(\lambda;x)=-g^{-}(\lambda;x),\quad x\in[X_{-},X_{+}].

Proof.

The proof
can be found in Appendix
B
.
∎

The formula (
4.10
) motivates the introduction of a function
h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
related to
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
as follows:

(4.15)

\begin{split}h^{\pm}(\lambda;x):=&\pm\left(g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)-\frac{1}{2}(\Xi(\lambda)\pm\overline{L}(\lambda)+2\mathrm{i}\lambda x)\right)\\
{}=&\pm\frac{R(\lambda;x)}{8\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{L}\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{R(\mathrm{i}s;x)(s^{2}+\lambda^{2})},\quad\text{$\mathrm{i}\lambda$ and $-\mathrm{i}\lambda$ interior to $L$}.\end{split}

15
.

Let
A(\cdot)
be a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential with support
[X_{-},X_{+}]
and maximizer
x_{0}
. For
x\in J^{\pm}_{c}
an arbitrary compact subset of
J^{\pm}
, the corresponding function
h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
has the following properties:

H1:

There is a conformal mapping
\lambda\mapsto W(\lambda)
defined in a neighborhood
D(x)
of
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A(x)
such that
4h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)^{2}=W(\lambda)^{3}
for
\lambda\in D(x)
and
W(\lambda)>0
for
\lambda\in D(x)
with
A(x)<-\mathrm{i}\lambda
.

H2:

Given
\delta>0
sufficiently small there exists a positive constant
\eta=\eta(J_{c}^{\pm},\delta)
such that

h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)>\eta
for
A(x)+\delta<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A_{\mathrm{max}}
and

\re\{h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)\}<-\eta
for
\delta<|\re\{\lambda\}|<2\delta
and
\delta<\imag\{\lambda\}<A(x)-\delta
.

H3:

Given
\delta>0
sufficiently small there exists a positive constant
\eta=\eta(J^{\pm}_{c},\delta)
such that
\re\{h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)-\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda)\}>\eta
holds on the parabolic arc

\re\{\lambda\}=\delta\imag\{\lambda\}(A_{\mathrm{max}}-\imag\{\lambda\})
with
\delta<\imag\{\lambda\}<A_{\mathrm{max}}
.
Similarly,
\re\{h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)+\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda)\}>\eta
holds on the parabolic arc

\re\{\lambda\}=-\delta\imag\{\lambda\}(A_{\mathrm{max}}-\imag\{\lambda\})
with
\delta<\imag\{\lambda\}<A_{\mathrm{max}}
.

H4:

The boundary values
h^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda;x)
and
h^{\pm}_{-}(\lambda;x)
taken by
h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
on the branch cut
-A(x)\leq-\mathrm{i}\lambda\leq A(x)
from the right and left half-planes respectively are both analytic at
\lambda=0
with convergent power series consisting of even powers of
\lambda
. Also,
h^{\pm}_{-}(\lambda;x)=-h^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda;x)
, and
h^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathrm{i}\alpha^{\pm}(x)+\mathrm{i}\beta^{\pm}(x)\lambda^{2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{4}\right)
as
\lambda\to 0
where
\alpha^{\pm}(x)
and
\beta^{\pm}(x)
are real, and where
\beta^{\pm}(x)\geq c
, for
c>0
a constant depending on
J_{c}^{\pm}
.

H5:

Recalling that
\Phi(\lambda)
is an even analytic function of
\lambda
near
\lambda=0
, the even analytic function
h^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda;x)-\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda)
satisfies
h^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda;x)-\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda)=\mathrm{i}(\alpha^{\pm}(x)-\Phi_{0})+\mathrm{i}(\beta^{\pm}(x)-\Phi_{1})\lambda^{2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{4}\right)
as
\lambda\to 0
, where the real coefficients
\Phi_{0}
and
\Phi_{1}
are given by (
2.34
), and where
\beta^{\pm}(x)-\Phi_{1}\leq-c
, for
c>0
a constant depending on
J_{c}^{\pm}
. Also
h^{\pm}_{-}(\lambda;x)+\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda)=-(h^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda;x)-\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda))
.

H6:

The boundary values taken by
h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
on the branch cut
0<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A(x)
can be expressed in terms of the difference in boundary values taken by
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
on the same cut:

(4.16)

g^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda;x)-g^{\pm}_{-}(\lambda;x)=\pm 2h_{+}^{\pm}(\lambda;x)=\mp 2h_{-}^{\pm}(\lambda;x),\quad 0<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A(x).

Proof.

The proof
can be found in Appendix
B
.
∎

4.2.
Steepest-descent analysis for
x\in J^{+}

4.2.1.
Removal of the poles

For
x\in J^{+}
, we begin by interpolating the residues at the poles to replace the meromorphic function
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})
satisfying Riemann-Hilbert Problem
1
with a sectionally analytic function. Let
\Sigma_{0}=[0,\mathrm{i}A_{\text{max}}]
be oriented from
\mathrm{i}A_{\text{max}}
to
0
;
let
\Sigma_{\pm}
be oriented contours from
\lambda=0
to
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A_{\text{max}}
lying in
\pm\operatorname{Re}\{\lambda\}>0
away from its endpoints such that the parabolic lens region
\Lambda
defined by (
3.24
) is enclosed by
\Sigma_{+}\cup\Sigma_{-}
.
Denote by
\Omega_{\pm}
the region enclosed between
\Sigma_{0}
and
\Sigma_{\pm}
.
See Figure
9
.

\imag(\lambda)=0
\Sigma_{0}
\Sigma_{0}^{*}
\Omega_{+}
\Omega_{-}
\Omega_{+}^{*}
\Omega_{-}^{*}
\Sigma_{+}
\Sigma_{-}
\Sigma_{+}^{*}
\Sigma_{-}^{*}

Figure 9.

The regions
\Omega_{\pm}
and the jump contours

\Sigma_{0}
and
\Sigma_{\pm}
(with their orientations) used to define the matrix transformation
\widetilde{\bf{M}}\mapsto\bf{Q}
in (
4.18
).

For any fixed
K\in\mathbb{Z}
and
(x,t_{2},t_{3},\dots,t_{M})\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
, define the exponent function

(4.17)

2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t}):=\mathrm{i}(2K+1)\Phi(\lambda)+\Xi(\lambda)+2\mathrm{i}Q(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t}),

where
Q
is defined in (
2.53
),
which is analytic in
\Omega_{+}\cup\Omega_{-}
. Let
K_{+}:=0
and
K_{-}:=-1
. Define

(4.18)

{\bf Q}(\lambda;x):=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\bf M}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K_{\pm}}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K_{\pm}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Omega_{\pm},\\
{\bf Q}(\lambda^{*};x)^{-\dagger},&\lambda\in\Omega_{\pm}^{*},\\
\widetilde{\bf M}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0}),&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

The resulting Riemann-Hilbert problem satisfied by
\mathbf{Q}(\lambda;x)
is as follows.

4
Sectionally Analytic Problem.

Given
\epsilon>0
and
x\in J^{+}
, seek a
2\times 2
matrix function
\mathbf{Q}(\lambda)=\mathbf{Q}(\lambda;x)
with the following properties.

Analyticity:

\mathbf{Q}(\lambda)
is analytic for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma
and satisfies the Schwarz symmetry condition
\mathbf{Q}(\lambda^{*})=\mathbf{Q}(\lambda)^{-\dagger}
.

Jump conditions:

\mathbf{Q}(\lambda)
takes continuous boundary values on
\Sigma
from each maximal connected component of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma
. Given a point
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
on one of the oriented arcs of
\Sigma
, let the boundary value taken at
\lambda
by
\mathbf{Q}
from the left (respectively, right) be denoted
\mathbf{Q}_{+}(\lambda)
(respectively,
\mathbf{Q}_{-}(\lambda)
). Then

(4.19)

\mathbf{Q}_{+}(\lambda)=\mathbf{Q}_{-}(\lambda)\mathbf{V^{Q}}(\lambda),

where the jump matrix
\mathbf{V^{Q}}(\lambda)=\mathbf{V^{Q}}(\lambda;x)
is defined on the various arcs of
\Sigma\cap\mathbb{C}_{+}
by

(4.20)

\mathbf{V^{Q}}(\lambda;x):=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{\varphi^{+}(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in\Sigma_{0},

(4.21)

\mathbf{V^{Q}}(\lambda;x):=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{[\varphi^{+}(\lambda;x)\pm 2\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda)]/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pm},

where

(4.22)

\varphi^{+}(\lambda;x):=\Xi(\lambda)+2\mathrm{i}Q(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})+\overline{L}(\lambda).

Corresponding jump conditions on the arcs of
\Sigma
in the lower half-plane are induced by the Schwarz symmetry condition.

Normalization:

\mathbf{Q}(\lambda)\to\mathbb{I}
as
\lambda\to\infty
.

Here in writing down the jumps we have used (
3.4
), (
3.13
),
(
3.14
), and (
3.15
).

4.2.2.
Installing the
g
-function and lens deformation

We now introduce the
g
-function. Let
g^{+}(\lambda;x)
be defined as in
(
4.7
).
Make the change of variables

(4.23)

\mathbf{R}(\lambda;x):=\mathbf{Q}(\lambda;x)\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{e}^{-g^{+}(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}&0\\
0&\mathrm{e}^{g^{+}(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}\end{pmatrix},

Now define
B\equiv B(x)
to be the subset of
\Sigma_{0}
in which
g^{+}
has
its jump discontinuity (with orientation inherited from
\Sigma_{0}
). We call

\overline{B\cup B^{*}}
the
band
. The function
{\bf R}(\lambda;x)

satisfies the jumps

(4.24)

\mathbf{R}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{R}_{-}(\lambda;x)\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{e}^{-(g^{+}_{+}(\lambda;x)-g^{+}_{-}(\lambda;x))/\epsilon}&0\\
-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)&\mathrm{e}^{(g^{+}_{+}(\lambda;x)-g^{+}_{-}(\lambda;x))/\epsilon}\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in B(x),

and

(4.25)

\mathbf{R}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{R}_{-}(\lambda;x)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{-2h^{+}(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in\Sigma_{0}\setminus B(x),

where, as in (
4.15
),

(4.26)

h^{+}(\lambda;x):=g^{+}(\lambda;x)-\frac{1}{2}\varphi^{+}(\lambda;x),\qquad\lambda\in\Sigma_{0}\setminus B(x).

To compute the jumps we have used Property G4 of Proposition
14
.

We now prepare to open lenses. Properties H1–H3 from §
4.1
can
be used to characterize the analytic continuation of
h^{+}(\lambda;x)

from
\Sigma_{0}\setminus B(x)
to the domains
\Omega_{\pm}
.
In particular, a matrix factorization and the use of the identity (
4.16
) implies that
(
4.24
) can be written in the equivalent form:

(4.27)

\mathbf{R}_{+}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{L}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{R}_{-}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{L}_{-}(\lambda;x)^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)^{-1}\\
-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in B(x),

where
\mathbf{L}(\lambda;x)
is the matrix analytic for
\lambda\in\Omega_{+}\cup\Omega_{-}
defined by

(4.28)

\mathbf{L}(\lambda;x):=\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2h^{+}(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in\Omega_{+}\cup\Omega_{-},\quad x\in J_{+}.

Note that
\mathbf{L}(\lambda;x)
inherits from
h^{+}(\lambda;x)
a jump discontinuity across
B(x)
, which explains the subscripts in (
4.27
) indicating boundary values taken. Lastly, we note that the jump condition on
\Sigma_{\pm}
takes the form

(4.29)

\mathbf{R}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{R}_{-}(\lambda;x)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{2[\pm\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda)-h^{+}(\lambda;x)]/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pm}.

Define two lens domains
\Lambda_{+}
and
\Lambda_{-}
bounded by
B(x)
and the
two parabolic arcs defined in property H3 of
h^{+}
in
Proposition
15
in §
4.1
. We denote these
parabolic arcs by
\Sigma_{L\pm}
. Property H3 also
tells us that the matrix
{\bf L}(\lambda;x)
will decay to the identity as

\epsilon\downarrow 0
on
\Sigma_{L\pm}
. With this in mind, define

(4.30)

\mathbf{S}(\lambda;x):=\begin{cases}\mathbf{R}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{L}(\lambda;x),&\quad\lambda\in\Lambda_{+},\\
\mathbf{R}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{L}(\lambda;x)^{-1},&\quad\lambda\in\Lambda_{-},\\
\mathbf{S}(\lambda^{*};x)^{-\dagger},&\quad\lambda\in\Lambda_{+}^{*}\cup\Lambda_{-}^{*},\\
\mathbf{R}(\lambda;x),&\quad\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)=0
\Sigma_{L+}
\Sigma_{L-}
\Sigma_{L+}^{*}
\Sigma_{L-}^{*}
\mathrm{i}A(x)
-\mathrm{i}A(x)
\Lambda_{+}
\Lambda_{-}
\Lambda_{+}^{*}
\Lambda_{-}^{*}

Figure 10.

The regions
\Lambda_{\pm}
and the jump contours
\Sigma_{L\pm}
(along with their orientations) defining the transformation
\bf{R}\mapsto\bf{S}
given by (
4.30
).

The substitution (
4.30
) separates the factors in the jump
conditions (
4.27
) so that the jump matrix for

\mathbf{S}(\lambda;x)
on the band
B(x)
is the explicit off-diagonal factor on
the right-hand side of (
4.27
).
On the contours
\Sigma_{L\pm}
we have

\mathbf{S}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{S}_{-}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{L}(\lambda;x)
. From
Proposition
10
in §
3.2.2
, the jump matrix for

\mathbf{S}(\lambda;x)
on
B(x)
is uniformly an
\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)

perturbation of
-\mathrm{i}\sigma_{1}
as long as
\lambda
is bounded away from the
real axis.

Let
\Sigma^{\prime}=\Sigma\cup\Sigma_{L\pm}\cup\Sigma^{*}_{L\pm}
denote the contour formed by adjoining the new lens contours to
\Sigma
. The matrix
\mathbf{S}(\lambda;x)
is then seen to satisfy the following Riemann-Hilbert problem, which has been “stabilized” via the introduction of
g^{+}(\lambda;x)
.

5
Stabilized Problem.

Given
\epsilon>0
and
x\in J^{+}
, seek a
2\times 2
matrix function
\mathbf{S}(\lambda)=\mathbf{S}(\lambda;x)
with the following properties.

Analyticity:

\mathbf{S}(\lambda)
is analytic for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}
and satisfies the Schwarz symmetry condition
\mathbf{S}(\lambda^{*})=\mathbf{S}(\lambda)^{-\dagger}
.

Jump conditions:

\mathbf{S}(\lambda)
takes continuous boundary values on
\Sigma^{\prime}
from each maximal connected component of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}
. Given a point
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
on one of the oriented arcs of
\Sigma^{\prime}
, let the boundary value taken at
\lambda
by
\mathbf{S}
from the left (respectively, right) be denoted
\mathbf{S}_{+}(\lambda)
(respectively,
\mathbf{S}_{-}(\lambda)
). Then

(4.31)

\mathbf{S}_{+}(\lambda)=\mathbf{S}_{-}(\lambda)\mathbf{V^{S}}(\lambda),

where the jump matrix
\mathbf{V^{S}}(\lambda)=\mathbf{V^{S}}(\lambda;x)
is defined on the various arcs of
\Sigma^{\prime}\cap\mathbb{C}_{+}
by

(4.32)

\mathbf{V^{S}}(\lambda;x):=\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{-2h^{+}(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Sigma_{0}\setminus B(x),\\
\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)^{-1}\\
-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)&0\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in B(x),\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{2[\pm\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda)-h^{+}(\lambda;x)]/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pm},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2h^{+}(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Sigma_{L,\pm},\\
\end{cases}

where
h^{+}(\lambda;x)
is given by (
4.26
).
Corresponding jump conditions on the arcs of
\Sigma^{\prime}
in the lower half-plane are induced by the Schwarz symmetry
condition.

Normalization:

\mathbf{S}(\lambda)\to\mathbb{I}
as
\lambda\to\infty
.

4.3.
Parametrix construction

4.3.1.
Outer parametrix

We begin with the construction of an outer parametrix designed to approximately solve the jump condition for
\mathbf{S}(\lambda;x)
on the vertical band
B(x)\cup B(x)^{*}
.
It follows from Proposition
10
that
\mathbf{V^{S}}(\lambda;x)=-\mathrm{i}\sigma_{1}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)
for those
\lambda\in B(x)
bounded away from
\lambda=0
and
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A_{\text{max}}
. Define

(4.33)

\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}(\lambda;x):=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}\left(\frac{\lambda+\mathrm{i}A(x)}{\lambda-\mathrm{i}A(x)}\right)^{\sigma_{3}/4}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}^{-1},\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(B(x)\cup B(x)^{*}),

where the powers
\pm 1/4
refer to the principal branch, and

(4.34)

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\pi/4}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\pi/4}\\
-\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\pi/4}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\pi/4}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\det(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}})=1,\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}^{\dagger}

is a matrix of eigenvectors for
-\mathrm{i}\sigma_{1}
.

Then
\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}(\lambda;x)
is analytic in its domain of definition, bounded for
\lambda
away from
\pm\mathrm{i}A(x)
, with
\det(\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}(\lambda;x))=1
, and

(4.35)

\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}(\lambda;x)=\mathbb{I}+\frac{A(x)}{2\mathrm{i}\lambda}\sigma_{1}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{-2}\right),\qquad\lambda\to\infty.

Moreover, the boundary values
\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{+}(\lambda;x)
and
\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{-}(\lambda;x)
satisfy the jump relation

(4.36)

\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{-}(\lambda;x)(-\mathrm{i}\sigma_{1}),\qquad\lambda\in B(x)\cup B(x)^{*},

where the jump contour orientation is taken downwards from
\mathrm{i}A(x)
to
-\mathrm{i}A(x)
.

4.3.2.
Airy Parametrix at
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A(x)

We need local models in neighborhoods of the band endpoints
\pm\mathrm{i}A(x)
.
Provided that
|x-x_{0}|>0
, where
x_{0}
is the maximizer of
A
, the appropriate model is constructed from Airy functions. See
[
11
, Appendix B]
for a complete derivation of this model with a slightly different normalization.
The function
y=\Ai(u)
is the unique solution of the differential equation
y^{\prime\prime}(u)=uy(u)
such that

(4.37)

\Ai(u)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-2u^{3/2}/3}}{2u^{1/4}\sqrt{\pi}}\left[1-\frac{5}{48}u^{-3/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(u^{-3}\right)\right],\qquad u\to\infty,\quad|\arg(u)|<\pi.

It is an entire function, and it satisfies the identity

(4.38)

\Ai(u)+\omega\Ai(\omega u)+\omega^{2}\Ai(\omega^{2}u)=0,\qquad\omega:=\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}\pi/3}.

Its derivative,
\Ai^{\prime}(u)
, satisfies

(4.39)

\Ai^{\prime}(u)=-\frac{u^{1/4}\mathrm{e}^{-2u^{3/2}/3}}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\left[1+\frac{7}{48}u^{-3/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(u^{-3}\right)\right],\qquad u\to\infty,\quad|\arg(u)|<\pi.

Setting
u:=\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{2/3}z
, we define a matrix function
\mathbf{A}(z)
as follows:

(4.40)

\mathbf{A}(z):=\begin{cases}\sqrt{2\pi}\left(\tfrac{4}{3}\right)^{\frac{\sigma_{3}}{6}}\begin{pmatrix}-\Ai^{\prime}(u)&\omega\Ai^{\prime}(\omega^{2}u)\\
-\mathrm{i}\Ai(u)&\mathrm{i}\omega^{2}\Ai(\omega^{2}u)\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\pi\sigma_{3}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{2}{3}u^{3/2}\sigma_{3}},&\arg(z)\in(0,\tfrac{2\pi}{3}),\\
\sqrt{2\pi}\left(\tfrac{4}{3}\right)^{\frac{\sigma_{3}}{6}}\begin{pmatrix}\omega^{2}\Ai^{\prime}(\omega u)&\omega\Ai^{\prime}(\omega^{2}u)\\
\mathrm{i}\omega\Ai(\omega u)&\mathrm{i}\omega^{2}\Ai(\omega^{2}u)\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\pi\sigma_{3}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{2}{3}u^{3/2}\sigma_{3}},&\arg(z)\in(\tfrac{2\pi}{3},\pi),\\
\sqrt{2\pi}\left(\tfrac{4}{3}\right)^{\frac{\sigma_{3}}{6}}\begin{pmatrix}\omega\Ai^{\prime}(\omega^{2}u)&-\omega^{2}\Ai^{\prime}(\omega u)\\
\mathrm{i}\omega^{2}\Ai(\omega^{2}u)&-\mathrm{i}\omega\Ai(\omega u)\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\pi\sigma_{3}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{2}{3}u^{3/2}\sigma_{3}},&\arg(z)\in(-\pi,-\tfrac{2\pi}{3}),\\
\sqrt{2\pi}\left(\tfrac{4}{3}\right)^{\frac{\sigma_{3}}{6}}\begin{pmatrix}-\Ai^{\prime}(u)&-\omega^{2}\Ai^{\prime}(\omega u)\\
-\mathrm{i}\Ai(u)&-\mathrm{i}\omega\Ai(\omega u)\end{pmatrix}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4}\pi\sigma_{3}}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{2}{3}u^{3/2}\sigma_{3}},&\arg(z)\in(-\tfrac{2\pi}{3},0).\end{cases}

From (
4.37
) and (
4.39
) we get that

(4.41)

\mathbf{A}(z)\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}z^{-\sigma_{3}/4}=\mathbb{I}+\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{O}\!\left(z^{-3}\right)&\mathcal{O}\!\left(z^{-1}\right)\\
\mathcal{O}\!\left(z^{-2}\right)&\mathcal{O}\!\left(z^{-3}\right)\end{pmatrix},\quad z\to\infty

uniformly in all directions of the complex plane, where
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}
is defined by (
4.34
).
The matrix function
\mathbf{A}(z)
is analytic in
z
with the exception of the four rays
\arg(\pm z)=0
and
\arg(z)=\pm 2\pi/3
, along which
\mathbf{A}(z)
takes continuous boundary values from either side, but across which it experiences jump discontinuities. Taking
\arg(-z)=0
to be oriented away from the origin and all other rays to be oriented toward the origin, the use of (
4.38
) yields the following jump conditions relating the boundary values of
\mathbf{A}(z)
:

(4.42)

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{+}(z)=\mathbf{A}_{-}(z)\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-z^{3/2}}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\arg(z)=0,

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{+}(z)=\mathbf{A}_{-}(z)\,\mathrm{i}\sigma_{1},

\displaystyle\arg(-z)=0,

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{+}(z)=\mathbf{A}_{-}(z)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{z^{3/2}}&1\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\arg(z)=\pm\frac{2\pi}{3}.

These jump conditions along with (
4.41
) and a Liouville argument imply that
\det(\mathbf{A}(z))=1
. They capture the behavior of the exponential factors in the jump conditions satisfied by
\mathbf{S}(\lambda;x)
in a neighborhood of
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A(x)
.

To exactly match the jump conditions (
4.32
) in a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}
of
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A(x)
, we first introduce a local coordinate
W(\lambda)
. According to property H1 of Proposition
15
, the map
\lambda\mapsto W(\lambda;x):=(2h(\lambda;x))^{2/3}
is conformal on any sufficiently small neighborhood
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}
of
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A(x)
with conformal image a neighborhood of
W=0
such that
W(\Sigma_{0}\cap\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)};x)\subset\mathbb{R}
with
W(B(x)\cap\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)};x)=[-w,0]
for some
w>0
. Locally, we deform
\Sigma_{L,\pm}
such that
\arg(W(\Sigma_{L,\pm};x))=\pm 2\pi/3
, and then we define the local model in the form

(4.43)

\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{Airy}}(\lambda;x):=\mathbf{H}^{\epsilon}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{A}\left(\frac{W(\lambda;x)}{\epsilon^{2/3}}\right)\mathrm{i}\sigma_{2}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)^{\sigma_{3}/2},\qquad\lambda\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)},

where
\mathbf{H}^{\epsilon}(\lambda;x)
is a unit-determinant holomorphic function for
\lambda\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}
used to match the local model to the outer model for
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}
.

To define
\mathbf{H}^{\epsilon}(\lambda;x)
, observe that

(4.44)

\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}:=\left(\frac{W(\lambda;x)}{\epsilon^{2/3}}\right)^{\sigma_{3}/4}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}\mathrm{i}\sigma_{2}

is analytic with unit determinant for
\lambda\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}\setminus B(x)
, and satisfies the same jump condition as
\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}(\lambda;x)
for
\lambda\in B(x)\cap\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}
.
Let

(4.45)

\mathbf{H}^{\epsilon}(\lambda;x):=\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{C}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}(\lambda)^{-1},\qquad\lambda\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)},

then
\mathbf{H}^{\epsilon}(\lambda;x)
has no jump on
B(x)\cap\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}
and admits a holomorphic extension to
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}
.

16
.

The matrix
\breve{\mathbf{S}}_{\mathrm{Airy}}(\lambda;x)
is analytic in
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}
with continuous boundary values on
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}\cap\Sigma^{\prime}
which match the jump conditions of
\mathbf{S}(\lambda;x)
exactly. Finally,

(4.46)

\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{Airy}}(\lambda;x)\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}(\lambda;x)^{-1}=\mathbb{I}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right),

which holds uniformly for
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}
and
x\in J_{c}^{+}
.

4.3.3.
Parametrix at the origin

The outer model is not uniformly accurate near the origin, so a local model is needed. Let
\mathcal{U}_{0}
denote a disk of sufficiently small radius, independent of
\epsilon
, centered at
\lambda=0
.
Motivated by the local approximations of
Y_{\epsilon}
and
T_{\epsilon}
for
\lambda
near 0 in Propositions
7
and
11
, introduce a rescaled local coordinate on
\mathcal{U}_{0}
by first defining

(4.47)

Z=Z(\lambda):=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{\epsilon\pi}}\left(\Phi(0)-\Phi(\lambda)\right)^{1/2},\quad\lambda=\mathrm{i}s,\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}},

where the square root is positive. It then follows from Proposition
2.31
that this formula admits continuation to
\mathcal{U}_{0}
, defining
Z
as an odd analytic function of
\lambda
with the property that
Z(0)=0
and
Z^{\prime}(0)>0
. It follows that
\lambda\mapsto Z
is a conformal map on the disk
\mathcal{U}_{0}
of sufficiently small radius independent of
\epsilon>0
.

Next, noting that
h^{+}
is analytic in
\mathcal{U}_{0}\setminus(B(x)\cup B(x)^{*})
and that
h^{+}_{+}(\lambda;x)+h^{+}_{-}(\lambda;x)=0
for
\lambda\in B(x)\cup B(x)^{*}
, the function

(4.48)

\hat{h}^{+}(\lambda;x)=\sgn(\operatorname{Re}\{\lambda\})\cdot h^{+}(\lambda;x)

has no jump on the band, and thus extends to an analytic function in
\mathcal{U}_{0}
. Define for
\lambda\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
the functions

(4.49)

\displaystyle m^{+}(\lambda;x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{2}{\epsilon}\left[\hat{h}^{+}(\lambda;x)-\hat{h}^{+}(0;x)\right],

\displaystyle n^{+}(\lambda;x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{2}{\epsilon}\left[\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda)-\mathrm{i}\Phi(0)-\hat{h}^{+}(\lambda;x)+\hat{h}^{+}(0;x)\right].

According to properties H4 and H5 of Proposition
15
these functions are even, analytic functions of
\lambda
, vanishing at
\lambda=0
, such that the coefficient of
\lambda^{2}
in their Taylor expansion at
\lambda=0
is strictly positive imaginary. Since the coordinate map
Z(\lambda)
is an odd, univalent function of
\lambda
, we can view
m^{+}(\lambda;x)
and
n^{+}(\lambda;x)
as even functions of
Z
analytic at
Z=0
with positive imaginary coefficients of
Z^{2}
in their respective Taylor expansions:

(4.50)

\displaystyle m^{+}(\lambda;x)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\mu^{+}(x)Z(\lambda)^{2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon Z(\lambda)^{4}\right),

\displaystyle\mu^{+}(x)\geq c,

\displaystyle n^{+}(\lambda;x)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\nu^{+}(x)Z(\lambda)^{2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon Z(\lambda)^{4}\right),

\displaystyle\nu^{+}(x)\geq c,

where
c>0
is a constant depending on
J^{+}_{c}
.

Next, we define a function which is a local solution of the jump condition satisfied by the outer parametrix for
\lambda
near
0
. Let

(4.51)

\mathbf{C}_{0}(\lambda):=\begin{cases}\mathrm{i}\sigma_{1},&\operatorname{Re}\{\lambda\}<0\\
\hfill\mathbb{I},&\operatorname{Re}\{\lambda\}>0.\end{cases}

so that the function

(4.52)

\mathbf{H}_{0}(\lambda;x)=\dot{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{C}_{0}(\lambda)^{-1}

has unit determinant, is independent of
\epsilon
, and is analytic for
\lambda\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
.

Then if we locally define a function
\mathbf{W}(\lambda;x)
by

(4.53)

\mathbf{W}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{S}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{C}_{0}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{\hat{h}^{+}(0;x)\sigma_{3}/\epsilon},\quad\lambda\in\mathcal{U}_{0},

then
\mathbf{W}(\lambda;x)
is analytic in
\mathcal{U}_{0}\setminus(\Sigma^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0})
where it satisfies the jump relation

(4.54)

\begin{gathered}\mathbf{W}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{W}_{-}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V^{W}}(\lambda;x),\qquad\lambda\in\Sigma^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0},\\
\mathbf{V^{W}}(\lambda;x):=\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{n^{+}(\lambda;x)}&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Sigma^{+}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{m^{+}(\lambda,x)}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Sigma^{+}_{L}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0},\\
\begin{pmatrix}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)&0\\
0&T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)^{-1}\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in B(x)\cap\mathcal{U}_{0},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{-m^{+}(\lambda,x)}&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Sigma^{-}_{L}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{-n^{+}(\lambda;x)}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Sigma^{-}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0},\\
\mathbf{V^{W}}(\lambda^{*};x)^{\dagger},&\lambda\in\Sigma^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0}\cap\mathbb{C}^{-}.\end{cases}\end{gathered}

To construct a local model
\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{0}
inside
\mathcal{U}_{0}
, we make two approximations: first, we replace all instances of
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
and
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
in the jump condition
\mathbf{V^{S}}
defined by (
4.32
) with their local approximations
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z(\lambda))
and
\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z(\lambda))
defined by (
3.27
) and (
3.34
) respectively; second we rewrite the exponential phases in
\mathbf{V^{S}}
in terms of the functions
m^{+}(\lambda;x)
and
n^{+}(\lambda;x)
and then replace these by the leading-order terms in their Taylor expansions at
\lambda=0
given in (
4.50
).
For concreteness, we locally deform the contours
\Sigma^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0}
as necessary so that the images are straight line segments with

(4.55)

\arg(Z(B(x)\cap\mathcal{U}_{0}))=\tfrac{\pi}{2},\quad\arg(Z(\Sigma^{\pm}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0}))=\tfrac{\pi}{2}\mp\tfrac{\pi}{3},\quad\arg(Z(\Sigma_{L}^{\pm}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0}))=\tfrac{\pi}{2}\mp\tfrac{\pi}{6},

and preserve the symmetry
Z(\Sigma^{\prime})^{*}=Z(\Sigma^{\prime})
to define the images in
\mathbb{C}^{-}
.
Let
\Sigma^{\circledast}
denote the contour in the
Z
-plane consisting of six infinite rays with angles
\pm\arg(Z)\in\{\pi/6,\pi/3,\pi/2\}
taking all rays oriented outward from the origin.

We then define

(4.56)

\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{0}(\lambda;x):=\mathbf{H}_{0}(\lambda;x)\mathrm{e}^{\hat{h}^{+}(0;x)\sigma_{3}/\epsilon}\breve{\mathbf{W}}(Z(\lambda);\mu^{+}(x),\nu^{+}(x))\mathrm{e}^{-\hat{h}^{+}(0;x)\sigma_{3}/\epsilon}\mathbf{C}_{0}(\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\mathcal{U}_{0},

where
\breve{\mathbf{W}}(Z;\mu,\nu)
satisfies the following problem:

6
.

Given parameters
\mu>0
and
\nu>0
, seek a
2\times 2
matrix function
\breve{\mathbf{W}}(Z)=\breve{\mathbf{W}}(Z;\mu,\nu)
with the following properties.

Analyticity:

\breve{\mathbf{W}}(Z)
is analytic for
Z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma^{\circledast}
and satisfies the Schwarz symmetry condition
\breve{\mathbf{W}}(Z^{*})=\breve{\mathbf{W}}(Z)^{-\dagger}
.

Jump conditions:

\breve{\mathbf{W}}(Z)
takes continuous boundary values on
\Sigma^{\circledast}
from each maximal connected component of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma^{\circledast}
. Given a point
Z
on one of the oriented arcs of
\Sigma^{\circledast}
, let the boundary value taken at
Z
by
\breve{\mathbf{W}}
from the left (respectively, right) be denoted
\breve{\mathbf{W}}_{+}(Z)
(respectively,
\breve{\mathbf{W}}_{-}(Z)
). Then

(4.57)

\breve{\mathbf{W}}_{+}(Z)=\breve{\mathbf{W}}_{-}(Z)\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{W}}}(Z),

where the jump matrix
\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{W}}}(Z)=\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{W}}}(Z;\mu,\nu)
is defined on the various arcs of
\Sigma^{\circledast}
by

(4.58)

\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{W}}}(Z;\mu,\nu):=\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\nu Z^{2}}&1\end{pmatrix},&\arg(Z)=\frac{\pi}{6},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mu Z^{2}}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\arg(Z)=\frac{\pi}{3},\\
\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)^{-1}&0\\
0&\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)\end{pmatrix},&\arg(Z)=\frac{\pi}{2},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\mu Z^{2}}&1\end{pmatrix},&\arg(Z)=\frac{2\pi}{3},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\nu Z^{2}}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\arg(Z)=\frac{5\pi}{6},\\
\quad\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{W}}}(Z^{*};\mu,\nu)^{\dagger}&\arg(Z)\in-\frac{k\pi}{6},\ k=1,\dots,5.\end{cases}

Normalization:

\breve{\mathbf{W}}(Z)\to\mathbb{I}
as
Z\to\infty
.

4
.

Uniformly for
\lambda\in\Sigma^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0}
,

\mathbf{V^{W}}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{W}}}\left(Z(\lambda);\mu^{+}(x),\nu^{+}(x)\right)^{-1}=\mathbb{I}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right),

where on the arcs
B(x)\cup B(x)^{*}
we first replace
\mathbf{V^{W}}(\lambda;x)
by its inverse to compare with outward orientation of all arcs of
\Sigma^{\circledast}
.

Proof.

First consider
\lambda\in B(x)
; then the statement
\mathbf{V^{W}}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{W}}}(Z(\lambda);\mu^{+}(x),\nu^{+}(x))^{-1}=\mathbb{I}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)
follows for such
\lambda
immediately from Proposition
11
. The analysis on each of the remaining components of
\Sigma^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0}
in the upper half-plane is similar, so we give full details for one case, say
\lambda\in\Sigma^{+}
. Recalling (
4.55
),
\arg(Z(\Sigma^{+}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0}))=\pi/6
and so comparing (
4.54
) to (
4.58
) we have for any matrix norm
\|\cdot\|

(4.59)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{V^{W}}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{W}}}(Z(\lambda);\mu^{+}(x),\nu^{+}(x))^{-1}-\mathbb{I}\right\|\leq K\left|e(\lambda;x)\right|,

(4.60)

\displaystyle e(\lambda;x):=Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{n^{+}(\lambda;x)}-\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z(\lambda))\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\nu^{+}(x)Z(\lambda)^{2}},

for some constant
K>0
depending on the matrix norm. By Proposition
7
,

(4.61)

\displaystyle e(\lambda;x)=\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z(\lambda))\left[\left(1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)\right)\mathrm{e}^{n^{+}(\lambda;x)}-\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\nu^{+}(x)Z(\lambda)^{2}}\right],

where we note that
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z(\lambda))
and
\mathrm{e}^{n^{+}(\lambda;x)}
are uniformly bounded on
\Sigma^{+}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0}
, as follows from Proposition
8
and property H3 of Proposition
15
.
Now consider separately those
\lambda\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
for which
|\lambda|\leq\epsilon^{3/8}
and those for which
|\lambda|\geq\epsilon^{3/8}
.
For
|\lambda|\leq\epsilon^{3/8}
, if follows from (
4.50
) that the difference of exponentials in (
4.61
) is
\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)
.
Conversely, for
\lambda\in\Sigma^{+}
with
|\lambda|\geq\epsilon^{3/8}
, then both exponential factors are separately small beyond all orders in
\epsilon
as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
. We conclude that
e(\lambda)=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)
holds uniformly for
\lambda\in\Sigma^{+}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0}
. Similar arguments apply on the other components of
\Sigma^{\prime}\setminus B(x)
in the upper-half-plane where the estimate in Proposition
7
is replaced by Proposition
11
whenever the jump condition depends on
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
in place of
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
; similarly property H3 of Proposition
15
is replaced by property H2 whenever
m^{+}(\lambda;x)
appears in place of
n^{+}(\lambda;x)
. Once the result is established in the upper half-plane, the symmetries
\mathbf{V^{W}}(\lambda)=\mathbf{V^{W}}(\lambda^{*})^{\dagger}
and
\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{W}}}(\lambda)=\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{W}}}(\lambda^{*})^{\dagger}
extend the result to the components of
\Sigma^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0}
in the lower half-plane.
∎

5
.

\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{W}}}(Z;\mu,\nu)
is analytic in all of its arguments along each ray of
\Sigma^{\circledast}
, and the limiting values as
Z\to 0
along each ray satisfy the cyclic consistency condition, i.e., their counterclockwise-ordered product is the identity.

Proof.

The analyticity of the jump matrix
\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{W}}}(Z;\mu,\nu)
on each ray of
\Sigma^{\circledast}
in the upper half-plane is obvious from formulæ (
3.27
), (
3.34
), and from the fact that all the exponential factors have the form
\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}cZ^{2}}
for
c\in\{\mu,\nu\}
.
To prove the consistency of the jump conditions, we first observe that this condition holds automatically for
\mathbf{V^{W}}(\lambda;x)
(replaced on the re-oriented arcs
B(x)\cup B(x)^{*}
by its inverse) because this jump matrix arose from a sequence of explicit sectionally analytic substitutions continuous up to the boundary of each component of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}
, each with unit determinant, applied to the matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})
(cf. Riemann-Hilbert problem
1
) which by definition is analytic at
\lambda=0
. To this we add the fact that the Taylor approximations (
4.50
) are exact in the limit
Z\to 0
, and, according to Propositions
7
and
11
, the functions
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
and
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
agree in the limit
\lambda\to 0
along the arcs of
\Sigma^{\prime}
with the approximations
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)
and
\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)
in the corresponding limit
Z\to 0
along
\Sigma^{\circledast}=Z(\Sigma^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0})
. Therefore the limiting value of
\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{W}}}(Z;\mu,\nu)
as
Z\to 0
along a given ray of
\Sigma^{\circledast}
agrees exactly with that of
\mathbf{V^{W}}(\lambda;x)
as
\lambda\to 0
along the corresponding arc of
\Sigma^{\prime}
(the limits
\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\mathbf{V^{W}}(\lambda;x)
and
\lim_{Z\to 0}\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{W}}}(Z;\mu,\nu)
along any component of
\Sigma^{\prime}
and
\Sigma^{\circledast}
are independent of
x
and
(\mu,\nu)
respectively).
∎

The model
\breve{\mathbf{W}}(Z;\mu,\nu)
is independent of the dispersion parameter
\epsilon
and its jump matrix
\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{W}}}(Z;\mu,\nu)
decays exponentially to the identity as
Z\to\infty
along each each ray of
\Sigma^{\circledast}\setminus\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
. The jump along the imaginary axis also decays to identity, but only algebraically. Before proving an existence result for the solution of our model problem, it is useful to first remove the slowly decaying jump along the imaginary axis.
Using the asymptotic expansion of
\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)
from Proposition
12
write

(4.62)

\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(Z):=\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}(1-\sqrt{2})\zeta(-\frac{1}{2})Z^{-1}},

so that
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(Z)=1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(Z^{-2}\right)
as
Z\to\infty
with
-\mathrm{i}Z>0
.
Let
\mathcal{B}:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]
be a
C^{\infty}
bump function with the property that

(4.63)

\mathcal{B}(\xi)=1\text{\quad for }\xi\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{B}(\xi)=0\text{\quad for }\xi\geq 1

and define the function

(4.64)

\displaystyle D(Z):=\begin{cases}\begin{multlined}\exp\left(\vphantom{\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{2\xi}{\xi^{2}+Z^{2}}}\mathrm{i}(1-\sqrt{2})\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2})\sgn(\operatorname{Re}\{Z\})Z^{-1}+\right.\\
\left.\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{1/2}^{\infty}(1-\mathcal{B}(\xi))\log(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(\mathrm{i}\xi))\frac{2\xi}{\xi^{2}+Z^{2}}\mathrm{d}\xi\right),\end{multlined}&|Z|>1,\\
\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{1/2}^{\infty}(1-\mathcal{B}(\xi))\log(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(\mathrm{i}\xi))\frac{2\xi}{\xi^{2}+Z^{2}}\mathrm{d}\xi\right),&|Z|<1.\end{cases}

Let
\partial\mathbb{D}
denote the unit circle in the
Z
-plane, and let
\partial\mathbb{D}^{\pm}
denote the upper and lower unit semi-circles oriented from
Z=-1
to
Z=1
. Then

17
.

The function
D(Z)
is analytic, bounded, nonzero, and satisfies the symmetry
D(Z^{*})^{*}=D(Z)^{-1}
for
Z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\cup\partial\mathbb{D})
.
For
Z\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\cup\partial\mathbb{D}
it takes continuous boundary values which satisfy the jump relation

(4.65)

\displaystyle D_{+}(Z)=D_{-}(Z)v^{D}(Z)

(4.66)

\displaystyle v^{D}(Z)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z),&-\mathrm{i}Z\in(1,\infty),\\
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(Z)^{1-\mathcal{B}(|Z|)},&-\mathrm{i}Z\in(\tfrac{1}{2},1),\\
1,&-\mathrm{i}Z\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2}),\\
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(1-\sqrt{2})\zeta(-\frac{1}{2})\sgn(\operatorname{Re}\{Z\})Z^{-1}},&\phantom{-\mathrm{i}}Z\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{+},\\
v^{D}(Z^{*})^{*},&-\mathrm{i}Z<0\text{ or }Z\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{-}\end{cases}

and, uniformly for large
Z
,

D(Z)=1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(Z^{-1}\right),\quad Z\to\infty.

Proof.

Using the partial fraction expansion

2\xi(\xi^{2}+Z^{2})^{-1}=-\mathrm{i}(Z-\mathrm{i}\xi)^{-1}+\mathrm{i}(Z+\mathrm{i}\xi)^{-1}

and a change of variables one can rewrite the integral term in (
4.64
) in the form

(4.67)

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{1/2}^{\infty}(1-\mathcal{B}(\xi))\log(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(\mathrm{i}\xi))\frac{2\xi}{\xi^{2}+Z^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}\xi\\
=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{0}^{\mathrm{i}\infty}(1-\mathcal{B}(|\eta|))\log(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(\eta))\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{\eta-Z}\\
+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{0}^{-\mathrm{i}\infty}(1-\mathcal{B}(|\eta|))\log(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(\eta^{*})^{*})\frac{\mathrm{d}\eta}{\eta-Z},

where in the last term we used the fact that
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(Z)
is real-valued on the positive imaginary axis.
Analyticity of
D(Z)
for
Z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\cup\partial\mathbb{D})
and the jump conditions then follow immediately from the Cauchy-Plemelj formula. Boundedness on any compact set follows from the fact that the function
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(Z)
is analytic and nonzero on the positive imaginary axis and
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(Z)=1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(Z^{-2}\right)
(cf. Proposition
12
) which implies both the continuity and boundedness of the boundary values for
Z\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
. In particular
D(Z)
is actually analytic for
-\mathrm{i}Z\in(-1/2,1/2)
.
The symmetry property
D(Z^{*})^{*}=D(Z)^{-1}
follows from direct inspection.
Finally, the decay estimate
D(Z)=1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(Z^{-1}\right)
as
Z\to\infty
follows immediately from the fact that
\log(\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(\mathrm{i}\xi))\in L^{1}([0,\infty))
since it decays like
\xi^{-2}
as
\xi\to\infty
.
∎

Using
D(Z)
we transform the model problem. Let

(4.68)

\mathbf{X}(Z;\mu,\nu):=\breve{\mathbf{W}}(Z;\mu,\nu)D(Z)^{\sigma_{3}},

then using Proposition
17
it follows that
\mathbf{X}(Z;\mu,\nu)
solves the following problem.

7
.

Given
\mu,\nu>0
, seek a
2\times 2
matrix function
\mathbf{X}(Z)=\mathbf{X}(Z;\mu,\nu)
with the following properties.

Analyticity:

\mathbf{X}(Z)
is analytic for
Z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(\Sigma^{\circledast}\cup\partial{\mathbb{D}})
and satisfies the Schwarz symmetry condition
\mathbf{X}(Z^{*})=\mathbf{X}(Z)^{-\dagger}
.

Jump conditions:

\mathbf{X}(Z)
takes continuous boundary values on
\Sigma^{\circledast}\cup\partial{\mathbb{D}}
from each maximal connected component of
\mathbb{C}\setminus(\Sigma^{\circledast}\cup\partial\mathbb{D})
. For
Z\in\Sigma^{\circledast}\cup\partial\mathbb{D}
, denote the boundary value taken from the left (respectively, right) by
\mathbf{X}_{+}(Z)
(respectively,
\mathbf{X}_{-}(Z)
). Then

(4.69)

\mathbf{X}_{+}(Z)=\mathbf{X}_{-}(Z)\mathbf{V^{X}}(Z),

where the jump matrix
\mathbf{V^{X}}(Z)=\mathbf{V^{X}}(Z;\mu,\nu)
is defined on the various arcs of
\Sigma^{\circledast}\cup\partial\mathbb{D}
by

(4.70)

\mathbf{V^{X}}(Z;\mu,\nu):=\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)D(Z)^{2}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\nu Z^{2}}&1\end{pmatrix},&\arg(Z)=\frac{\pi}{6},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)^{-1}D(Z)^{-2}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mu Z^{2}}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\arg(Z)=\frac{\pi}{3},\\
\left(\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(Z)^{1-\mathcal{B}(|Z|)}}{\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)}\right)^{\sigma_{3}},&-\mathrm{i}Z\in(0,1),\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)^{-1}D(Z)^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\mu Z^{2}}&1\end{pmatrix},&\arg(Z)=\frac{2\pi}{3},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)D(Z)^{-2}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\nu Z^{2}}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\arg(Z)=\frac{5\pi}{6},\\
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(1-\sqrt{2})\zeta(-\frac{1}{2})\sgn(\operatorname{Re}\{Z\})Z^{-1}\sigma_{3}},&Z\in\partial\mathbb{D}^{+},\\
\quad\mathbf{V^{X}}(Z^{*};\mu,\nu)^{\dagger},&\arg(Z)\in-\frac{k\pi}{6},\ k=1,\dots,5.\end{cases}

Normalization:

\mathbf{X}(Z)\to\mathbb{I}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(Z^{-1}\right)
as
Z\to\infty
.

6
.

\mathbf{V^{X}}(Z;\mu,\nu)
is analytic in
\mu
and
\nu
along each arc of
\Sigma^{\circledast}\cup\partial\mathbb{D}
, and at each point of self-intersection in the contour
\Sigma^{\circledast}\cup\partial\mathbb{D}
, the limiting values of the jump along each component contour satisfy the cyclic consistency condition.

Proof.

Analyticity of
\mathbf{V^{X}}(Z;\mu,\nu)
in
\mu
and
\nu
is obvious from (
4.70
). Cyclic consistency of the jumps at self-intersection points of the jump contour follows from Lemma
5
and the observation that the function
D(Z)
defining the transformation (
4.68
) from
\dot{\mathbf{W}}\mapsto\mathbf{X}
has continuous boundary values as
Z\to\Sigma^{\circledast}\cup\partial\mathbb{D}
— including all points of self intersection — from each connected component of
\mathbb{C}\setminus(\Sigma^{\circledast}\cup\partial\mathbb{D})
which preserves the consistency of the jumps. ∎

7
.

There exists a unique solution
\mathbf{X}(\lambda;\mu,\nu)
of Riemann-Hilbert Problem
7
which is uniformly bounded for
(\mu,\nu)
in any compact subset of
(0,\infty)^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{S}
denote the open sector
\mathcal{S}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|\arg(z)|<\pi/3\}
; the mapping
(\mu,\nu)\mapsto\mathbf{V^{X}}(\diamond;\mu,\nu)-\mathbb{I}
is analytic from
\mathcal{S}^{2}\to L^{2}(\Sigma^{\circledast}\cup\partial\mathbb{D})\cap L^{\infty}(\Sigma^{\circledast}\cup\partial\mathbb{D})
. Cyclic consistency of the jump
\mathbf{V^{X}}(Z;\mu,\nu)
at points of self-intersection established in Lemma
6
together with the symmetry
\mathbf{V^{X}}(Z^{*};\mu,\nu)=\mathbf{V^{X}}(Z;\mu,\nu)^{\dagger}
are sufficient to apply Zhou’s vanishing lemma
[
30
]
to deduce that the only matrix
\mathbf{X}^{0}(Z)
analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus(\Sigma^{\circledast}\cup\partial\mathbb{D})
solving the homogenous version of the Riemann-Hilbert problem, namely
\mathbf{X}^{0}_{+}(Z)=\mathbf{X}^{0}_{-}(Z)\mathbf{V^{X}}(Z)
for
Z\in\Sigma^{\circledast}\cup\partial\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbf{X}^{0}(Z)\to\mathbf{0}
as
Z\to\infty
, is the zero matrix
\mathbf{X}^{0}(Z)\equiv\mathbf{0}
.
From analytic Fredholm theory it then follows that there exists a unique solution of Riemann-Hilbert Problem
7
whose boundary values
\mathbf{X}_{\pm}(Z;\mu,\nu)
satisfy the jump condition, and also the normalization condition at
Z=\infty
in the sense that
\mathbf{X}_{\pm}(\diamond)-\mathbb{I}\in L^{2}(\Sigma^{\circledast}\cup\partial\mathbb{D})
. Moreover these boundary values depend analytically on the parameters
(\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal{S}^{2}
.
The solution
\mathbf{X}(Z;\mu,\nu)
of Riemann-Hilbert problem
7
is given in terms of its boundary values by

(4.71)

\mathbf{X}(Z;\mu,\nu)=\mathbb{I}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Sigma^{\circledast}\cup\partial\mathbb{D}}\frac{\mathbf{X}_{-}(\eta;\mu,\nu)(\mathbf{V^{X}}(\eta;\mu,\nu)-\mathbb{I})}{\eta-Z}\mathrm{d}\eta.

Observing that
\mathbf{V^{X}}(\eta;\mu,\nu)-\mathbb{I}
decays exponentially to zero along each unbounded component of
\Sigma^{\circledast}
for each
(\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal{S}^{2}
, the factor
(\eta-Z)^{-1}
can be expanded geometrically for
Z\to\infty
yielding an asymptotic expansion for
\mathbf{X}(Z;\mu,\nu)
in powers of
Z^{-1}
. In particular,
\mathbf{X}(Z;\mu,\nu)=\mathbb{I}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(Z^{-1}\right)
as
Z\to\infty
. Cyclic consistency of the jump matrix
\mathbf{V^{X}}(Z)
at points of self-intersection and
C^{\infty}
smoothness on each arc of
\Sigma^{\circledast}\cup\partial\mathbb{D}
imply that
\mathbf{X}(Z)
has bounded and continuous boundary values
\mathbf{X}_{\pm}(Z;\mu,\nu)
. Finally, analyticity of the solution
\mathbf{X}(Z;\mu,\nu)
for
(\mu,\nu)\in\mathcal{S}^{2}
implies uniform boundedness of the solution for parameters
(\mu,\nu)
in any compact subset of
\mathcal{S}^{2}
, and hence by restriction to positive real values, in any compact subset of
(0,\infty)^{2}
.
∎

With
\mathbf{X}(Z;\mu,\nu)
uniquely determined, we then invert the explicit transformations (
4.56
), (
4.68
) connecting
\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{0}(\lambda;x)
to
\mathbf{X}(Z;\mu,\nu)
to write for
\lambda\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
,

(4.72)

\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{0}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{H}_{0}(\lambda;x)\mathrm{e}^{\hat{h}^{+}(0;x)\sigma_{3}/\epsilon}\mathbf{X}(Z(\lambda);\mu^{+}(x),\nu^{+}(x))D(Z(\lambda))^{-\sigma_{3}}\mathrm{e}^{-\hat{h}^{+}(0;x)\sigma_{3}/\epsilon}\mathbf{C}_{0}(\lambda),

where we recall the definitions in (
4.47
)–(
4.48
), (
4.51
)–(
4.52
), and (
4.64
).

8
.

For
x\in J^{+}_{c}
,

\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{0}(\lambda;x)
is uniformly bounded on
\mathcal{U}_{0}
with
\det(\breve{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x))=1
. Also,

\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{0}(\lambda;x)\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}(\lambda;x)^{-1}=\mathbb{I}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)
holds uniformly for
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{U}_{0}
. Finally,

\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{S}}}(\lambda;x)^{-1}=\mathbb{I}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)
holds uniformly for
\lambda\in\Sigma^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0}
, where
\mathbf{V^{S}}(\lambda;x)
and
\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{S}}}(\lambda;x)
denote the jump matrices for
\mathbf{S}(\lambda;x)
and
\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{0}(\lambda;x)
respectively.

Proof.

The fact that
\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{0}(\lambda;x)
is bounded with unit determinant follows from the corresponding properties of
\mathbf{X}(Z;\mu,\nu)
and the explicit formula (
4.72
). Then, using (
4.52
), we have

(4.73)

\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{0}(\lambda;x)\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}(\lambda;x)^{-1}=\\
\mathbf{H}_{0}(\lambda;x)\mathrm{e}^{\hat{h}^{+}(0;x)\sigma_{3}/\epsilon}\mathbf{X}(Z(\lambda);\mu^{+}(x),\nu^{+}(x))D(Z(\lambda))^{-\sigma_{3}}\mathrm{e}^{-\hat{h}^{+}(0;x)\sigma_{3}/\epsilon}\mathbf{H}_{0}(\lambda;x)^{-1}.

Combining the large-
Z
asymptotic behavior of
D(Z)
and
\mathbf{X}(Z;\mu^{+}(x),\nu^{+}(x))
described in Proposition
17
and Riemann-Hilbert Problem
7
with the local coordinate rescaling in (
4.47
), it follows that
\mathbf{X}(Z(\lambda);\mu^{+}(x),\nu^{+}(x))D(Z(\lambda))^{-\sigma_{3}}=\mathbb{I}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)
uniformly for
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{U}_{0}
. Observing that
\hat{h}^{+}(0)=h_{+}^{+}(0)\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
, as follows from property H4 in Proposition
15
, and that
\mathbf{H}_{0}(\lambda;x)
is analytic on the closure of
\mathcal{U}_{0}
and is independent of
\epsilon
, it follows that
\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{0}(\lambda;x)\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}(\lambda;x)^{-1}=\mathbb{I}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)
holds as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
uniformly for
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{U}_{0}
.

To estimate
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{S}}}(\lambda;x)^{-1}-\mathbb{I}
for
\lambda\in\Sigma^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0}
, we observe that

(4.74)

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{S}}}(\lambda;x)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{C}_{0}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{\hat{h}^{+}(0)\sigma_{3}/\epsilon}\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{W}}}(Z(\lambda);\mu^{+}(x),\nu^{+}(x))\mathrm{e}^{-\hat{h}^{+}(0)\sigma_{3}/\epsilon}\mathbf{C}_{0}(\lambda),

\displaystyle\mathbf{V^{S}}(\lambda;x)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{C}_{0}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{\hat{h}^{+}(0)\sigma_{3}/\epsilon}\mathbf{V^{W}}(\lambda;x)\mathrm{e}^{-\hat{h}^{+}(0)\sigma_{3}/\epsilon}\mathbf{C}_{0}(\lambda).

Since the conjugating factors are all bounded with bounded inverses in
\mathcal{U}_{0}
, the result follows from Lemma
4
.
∎

4.3.4.
Global Parametrix

For each
x\in J^{+}
, we can now define a parametrix for
\mathbf{S}(\lambda;x)
as follows:

(4.75)

\breve{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x):=\begin{cases}\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{0}(\lambda;x),&\lambda\in\mathcal{U}_{0},\\
\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\text{Airy}}(\lambda;x),&\lambda\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)},\\
\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\text{Airy}}(\lambda^{*};x)^{-\dagger},&\lambda\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}^{*},\\
\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}(\lambda;x),&\text{ elsewhere}.\end{cases}

4.4.
Accuracy of the global parametrix

To compare the unknown solution
\mathbf{S}(\lambda;x)
of Riemann-Hilbert Problem
5
, directly constructed from the solution
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,0)
of the soliton ensemble Riemann-Hilbert Problem
1
with our explicitly constructed parametrix
\dot{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)
we consider the error defined by the ratio

(4.76)

\mathbf{E}(\lambda;x):=\mathbf{S}(\lambda;x)\breve{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)^{-1}

in each region of the complex
\lambda
-plane in which both factors of the right-hand side are defined and analytic. Then
\mathbf{E}(\lambda;x)
is analytic for
\lambda\in\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
, where
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}=\Sigma^{\prime}\cup\partial\mathcal{U}_{0}\cup\partial\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}\cup\partial\mathcal{U}^{*}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}
. The following result characterizes the jump matrix of
\mathbf{E}(\lambda;x)
on
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
.

9
.

For
x\in J_{c}^{+}
, the estimate

(4.77)

\mathbf{E}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{E}_{-}(\lambda;x)\left[\mathbb{I}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)\right],\qquad\lambda\in\Sigma^{\prime\prime}

holds uniformly as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
.

Proof.

First consider
\lambda
on the boundaries of the neighborhoods
\mathcal{U}_{0}
,
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}
, and
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}^{*}
, which is in each case a simple closed curve. Across these curves,
\mathbf{S}(\lambda;x)
has no jump, so
\mathbf{E}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{E}_{-}(\lambda;x)\breve{\mathbf{S}}_{-}(\lambda;x)\breve{\mathbf{S}}_{+}(\lambda;x)^{-1}
. Taking the curves
\partial\mathcal{U}_{0}
,
\partial\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)},\ \partial\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}^{*}
to have clockwise orientation,
\breve{\mathbf{S}}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}(\lambda;x)
in each case, while
\breve{\mathbf{S}}_{-}(\lambda;x)=\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{0}(\lambda;x)
for
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{U}_{0}
,
\breve{\mathbf{S}}_{-}(\lambda;x)=\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{Airy}}(\lambda;x)
for
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}
, and
\breve{\mathbf{S}}_{-}(\lambda;x)=\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{Airy}}(\lambda^{*};x)^{-\dagger}
for
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}^{*}
. The bound

\mathbf{E}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{E}_{-}(\lambda;x)\left[\mathbb{I}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)\right]
then follows from Proposition
16
for
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}
and the same estimate holds on
\partial\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}^{*}
by Schwarz symmetry of the matrix factors in (
4.77
). Similarly, by Lemma
8
, we have
\mathbf{E}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{E}_{-}(\lambda;x)\left[\mathbb{I}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)\right]
for
\lambda\in\partial\mathcal{U}_{0}
.

The matrix
\mathbf{S}(\lambda;x)
has jump discontinuities on all of the other component contours of
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
. If we write
\mathbf{S}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{S}_{-}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)
and
\breve{\mathbf{S}}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\breve{\mathbf{S}}_{-}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{S}}}(\lambda;x)
for
\lambda
on any of these arcs, then

(4.78)

\mathbf{E}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{E}_{-}(\lambda;x)\breve{\mathbf{S}}_{-}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{S}}}(\lambda;x)^{-1}\breve{\mathbf{S}}_{-}(\lambda;x)^{-1}.

For
\lambda\in\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\cap\left(\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}\cup\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}^{*}\right)
, it follows immediately from Proposition
16
that
\mathbf{E}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{E}_{-}(\lambda;x)
. In the remainder of
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
either:
\breve{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)=\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}(\lambda;x)
, which is bounded with bounded inverse for
\lambda\not\in\left(\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}\cup\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}^{*}\right)
as follows immediately from (
4.33
); or, when
\lambda\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
,
\breve{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)=\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{0}(\lambda;x)
which is bounded with bounded inverse by Lemma
8
. Thus, it will be sufficient to estimate
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{S}}}(\lambda;x)^{-1}-\mathbb{I}
for
\lambda
lying in
\Sigma^{\prime}
outside
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}\cup\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}^{*}
. Moreover, it is sufficient to consider only
\lambda
with
\operatorname{Im}\{\lambda\}>0
because the corresponding estimates for
\lambda
in the lower half-plane follow from Schwarz symmetry.

To analyze
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{S}}}(\lambda;x)^{-1}-\mathbb{I}
on these arcs, first consider
\lambda\in\Sigma_{0}
outside the neighborhoods
\mathcal{U}_{0}
and
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}
. If
\lambda\in B(x)
, then
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)
is given by (
4.32
) while
\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{S}}}(\lambda;x)
is exactly the same with
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
replaced by 1. The fact that
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{S}}}(\lambda;x)^{-1}-\mathbb{I}=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)
holds uniformly for
\lambda\in B(x)
bounded away from
0
and
\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}}
follows from Proposition
10
.
If, instead,
\lambda\in\Sigma_{0}\setminus\ B(x)
outside of
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}
, then
\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{S}}}(\lambda;x)=\mathbb{I}
, and
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)
is given by (
4.32
). Then
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{S}}}(\lambda;x)^{-1}-\mathbb{I}=\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)-\mathbb{I}
is uniformly exponentially small as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
as follows form property H2 of Proposition
15
together with Propositions
10
and
13
to uniformly bound
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
(to apply Proposition
13
, note that the model function
\mathcal{T}_{1}(W)
defined by (
3.36
) is uniformly bounded for
W\leq 0
).

Next, consider
\lambda
in the lens contours
\Sigma_{\pm}
and
\Sigma_{\pm,L}
outside the neighborhoods
\mathcal{U}_{0}
and
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)}
. On each of these contours
\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{S}}}(\lambda;x)=\mathbb{I}
, while
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)
is given by (
4.32
). In each case we have that
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{S}}}(\lambda;x)^{-1}-\mathbb{I}=\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)-\mathbb{I}
is uniformly exponentially small as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
.
For
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pm}\setminus\mathcal{U}_{0}
this follows from property H3 of Proposition
15
together with Proposition
6
to control
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
for
\lambda
away from
\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}}
and Proposition
3.31
to handle the case when
\lambda
approaches this point along
\Sigma_{\pm}
(noting that the model function
\mathcal{Y}_{1}(W)
defined by (
3.30
) remains bounded for such
\lambda
).
For
\lambda\in\Sigma_{L,\pm}\setminus(\mathcal{U}_{0}\cup\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{i}A(x)})
this follows from property H2 of Proposition
15
along with Proposition
10
.

Finally, consider
\lambda\in\Sigma^{\prime\prime}\cap\mathcal{U}_{0}
. The uniform estimate
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\breve{\mathbf{S}}}(\lambda;x)^{-1}-\mathbb{I}=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)
now follows directly from Lemma
8
in this case.
∎

4.5.
Alteration of the nonlinear steepest descent calculation for
x<x_{0}

In the steepest descent analysis carried out above it was assumed that
x\in J^{+}=(x_{0},X_{+})
, where
x_{0}
is the unique location of the maximum of the initial amplitude of our semicircular initial data, i.e.,
A(x_{0})=A_{\mathrm{max}}
. The analysis for
x\in J^{-}=(X_{-},x_{0})
is very similar with minor modifications we will outline below. For
x\in J^{-}
, our starting point is the renormalized meromorphic Riemann-Hilbert Problem
2
with solution
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})
. The first step in our analysis is to interpolate the poles of
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})
to replace it with a sectionally analytic function. Let
K_{+}=-1
and
K_{-}=0
. Akin to (
4.18
), define

(4.79)

\mathbf{Q}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x):=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K_{\pm}}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-2f_{K_{\pm}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})/\epsilon}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Omega_{\pm},\\
\mathbf{Q}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda^{*};x)^{-\dagger},&\lambda\in\Omega_{\pm}^{*},\\
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0}),&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

where we remind the reader of the domains
\Omega^{\pm}
and contour
\Sigma
defined in Figure
9
.
The resulting function
\mathbf{Q}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)
is analytic for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma
and satisfies a problem like Riemann-Hilbert Problem
4
with the jump (
4.20
)-(
4.21
) replaced by

(4.80)

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{Q}^{\updownarrow}}(\lambda;x)=\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{-\varphi^{-}(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Sigma_{0},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{-\left[\varphi^{-}(\lambda;x)\mp 2\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda)\right]/\epsilon}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pm},\\
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{Q}^{\updownarrow}}(\lambda^{*};x)^{\dagger},&\lambda\in\Sigma\cap\mathbb{C}^{-},\end{cases}

where

(4.81)

\displaystyle\varphi^{-}(\lambda;x):=\Xi(\lambda)+2\mathrm{i}Q(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})-\overline{L}(\lambda).

The next step in the analysis is again to introduce a
g
-function. For
x\in J^{-}
, we use the function
g^{-}(\lambda;x)
(cf. (
4.7
)) to define

(4.82)

\mathbf{R}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x):=\mathbf{Q}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{e}^{-g^{-}(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}&0\\
0&\mathrm{e}^{g^{-}(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}\end{pmatrix}.

Just as was the case for
x\in J^{+}
, for
x\in J^{-}
the function
g^{-}(\lambda;x)
is analytic for
\lambda
away from the band
\overline{B\cup B^{*}}
(here
B\equiv B(x)
). Using (
4.11
) and (
4.16
) we have

(4.83)

\mathbf{R}_{+}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{R}_{-}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{e}^{2h^{-}_{+}(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}&-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\\
0&\mathrm{e}^{2h^{-}_{-}(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\lambda\in B(x),

where
h^{-}(\lambda;x)
, analytic for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\overline{B\cup B^{*}}
, is defined by (
4.15
).
This jump relation can be rewritten as

(4.84)

\mathbf{R}_{+}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{L}_{+}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{R}_{-}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{L}_{-}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\\
-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in B(x),

where
\mathbf{L}_{+}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)
is the matrix analytic for
\lambda\in\Omega_{+}\cup\Omega_{-}
defined by

(4.85)

\mathbf{L}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x):=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2h^{-}(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix}.

This factorization motivates a further deformation.
As before, let
\Lambda_{\pm}
be the two domains bounded by
B(x)
and a pair of parabolic arcs defined in property H3 of Proposition
15
; denote the parabolic arcs by
\Sigma_{L\pm}
(cf. Figure
10
).
Let

(4.86)

\mathbf{S}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x):=\begin{cases}\mathbf{R}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{L}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)^{\pm 1},&\lambda\in\Lambda_{\pm},\\
\mathbf{S}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda^{*};x)^{-\dagger},&\lambda\in\Lambda_{+}^{*}\cup\Lambda_{-}^{*},\\
\mathbf{R}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x),&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then
\mathbf{S}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)
is a function analytic in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}
satisfying Riemann-Hilbert Problem
5
, except that the jump
\mathbf{V^{S}}(\lambda;x)
is replaced by

(4.87)

\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}^{\updownarrow}}(\lambda;x):=\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{-2h^{-}(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Sigma_{0}\setminus B(x),\\
\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\\
-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in B(x),\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{-2\left[h^{-}(\lambda;x)\mp\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda)\right]/\epsilon}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Sigma_{\pm},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2h^{-}(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\Sigma_{L\pm}.\\
\end{cases}

The jump
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}^{\updownarrow}}(\lambda;x)
has a well-defined pointwise limit as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
away from the points
\lambda=0,\mathrm{i}A(x),-\mathrm{i}A(x)
.
The construction of a global parametrix
\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)
for
\mathbf{S}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)
follows along the same lines as was done for
x\in J^{+}
in Section
4.3
. One important point we emphasize is that inspecting (
4.32
) and (
4.87
) and recalling Proposition
10
,

(4.88)

\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}}(\lambda;x)=\lim_{\epsilon\downarrow 0}\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{S}^{\updownarrow}}(\lambda;x)=-\mathrm{i}\sigma_{1},\qquad\lambda\in B(x),

which shows that the outer model
\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}(\lambda;x)
valid for
x\in J^{+}
previously constructed in (
4.33
) works for
x\in J^{-}
as well. In particular, for
x\in J^{-}
the endpoints of
B(x)
, which ultimately determine the leading order asymptotic behavior of the semiclassical soliton ensemble
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{0})
, are still given by
\pm\mathrm{i}A(x)
.
We leave the remaining details to the interested reader.

Once the global parametrix
\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)
is constructed, the error

(4.89)

\mathbf{E}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{S}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x)^{-1}

can be considered, and, repeating the analysis in Section
4.4
, one proves the following lemma by mimicking the proof of Lemma
9
.

10
.

For
x\in J^{-}_{c}
, the estimate

(4.90)

\mathbf{E}^{\updownarrow}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{E}^{\updownarrow}_{-}(\lambda;x)\left[\mathbb{I}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)\right],\qquad\lambda\in\Sigma^{\prime\prime},

holds uniformly as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
.

4.6.
Application of small-norm theory

To deal with the cases
x\in J^{\pm}
simultaneously, for any matrix function
\mathbf{A}
defined for
x\in J^{+}
and
\mathbf{A}^{\updownarrow}
defined for
x\in J^{-}
, set

(4.91)

\mathbf{A}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x):=\begin{cases}\mathbf{A}(\lambda;x),&x\in J^{+},\\
\mathbf{A}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x),&x\in J^{-}.\end{cases}

It follows from (
4.76
) and (
4.89
) that
\mathbf{E}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x)=\mathbb{I}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{-1}\right)
as
\lambda\to\infty
and
\mathbf{E}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x)
is an analytic function for
\lambda\in C\setminus\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
.
Moreover, from Lemmas
9
and
10
, the jump matrix
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}^{\sharp}}(\lambda;x)
is uniformly an
\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)
perturbation of the identity matrix for
x
in compact subsets of
(X_{-},X_{+})\setminus\{x_{0}\}
as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
.
Finally we observe that the contour
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
is compact and independent of
\epsilon
.
Together, these facts classify
\mathbf{E}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x)
as the solution of a Riemann-Hilbert problem of
small-norm type
. The small-norm theory can be applied in the context of matrix-valued functions that are Hölder continuous up to the boundary of each connected component of
\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
. The theory establishes the existence of a unique function
\mathbf{E}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x)
satisfying the normalization and jump conditions in the Riemann-Hilbert problem and yields estimates for
\mathbf{E}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x)-\mathbb{I}
which are proportional to the product of the above
\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)
uniform estimate of
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}^{\sharp}}(\lambda;x)-\mathbb{I}
and the operator norm of a Cauchy (singular integral) projection operator for the contour
\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
. The end result is that a unique solution
\mathbf{E}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x)
of the error Riemann-Hilbert problem exists and the estimate
\mathbf{E}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x)=\mathbb{I}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{-1/2}\right)
holds as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
uniformly for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma^{\prime\prime}
and
x
in compact subsets of
(X_{-},X_{+})\setminus\{x_{0}\}
. Moreover, in the convergent Laurent expansion of
\mathbf{E}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x)
about
\lambda=\infty
,

(4.92)

\mathbf{E}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x)=\mathbb{I}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{E}^{\sharp[n]}(x)\lambda^{-n},\qquad|\lambda|>\sup_{\lambda^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\prime\prime}}|\lambda^{\prime}|,

all of the coefficients
\mathbf{E}^{\sharp[n]}(x)
,
n\geq 1
, satisfy
\mathbf{E}^{\sharp[n]}(x)=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)
as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
.

Now, from the definition of (
4.76
) and (
4.89
),
\mathbf{S}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{E}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x)\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x)
. For
|\lambda|
sufficiently large,
\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x)=\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}(\lambda;x)
and
\mathbf{S}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{M}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})\mathrm{e}^{-g^{s}(\lambda;x)\sigma_{3}}
, where
s=\sgn(x-x_{0})
. Therefore for all sufficiently large
\lambda
, the solution at
t=0
of the semiclassical soliton ensemble Riemann-Hilbert Problem
1
for
x\in J^{+}
and Riemann-Hilbert Problem
2
for
x\in J^{-}
is given by
\mathbf{M}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})=\mathbf{E}^{\sharp}(\lambda;x)\breve{\mathbf{S}}^{\mathrm{out}}(\lambda;x)\mathrm{e}^{g^{s}(\lambda;x)\sigma_{3}}
.
It then follows from the reconstruction formula (
2.58
), using Property G5 in Proposition
14
, (
4.35
), and (
4.92
), that

(4.93)

\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{0})=A(x)+2\mathrm{i}E^{\sharp[1]}_{12}(x)=A(x)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right),

where we recall that
A(x)=\psi_{0}(x)
is a Cauchy initial datum of semicircular Klaus-Shaw type.
This completes the proof of Theorem
2.1
in the case that either
X_{-}<x<x_{0}
or
x_{0}<x<X_{+}
.

5.
Proof of Theorem
2.1
for
x<X_{-}
and
x>X_{+}

Suppose that
x>X_{+}
.
Let
\mathscr{D}
be a bounded, simply connected domain in the upper
half-plane
\mathbb{C}_{+}
such that
\partial\mathscr{D}
is a
simple closed loop that starts and ends at the origin
and encloses all of the points
\lambda=\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{j}
,
j=0,\dots,N-1
for
all
N
. See Figure
11

\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)=0
\mathscr{D}
\mathscr{D}^{*}

Figure 11.
The regions
\mathscr{D}
and
\mathscr{D}^{*}
and oriented boundaries
\partial\mathscr{D}
and
\partial\mathscr{D}^{*}
.

Starting from Riemann-Hilbert Problem
1
with
solution
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
, we define

(5.1)

\mathbf{U}(\lambda;x):=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{0}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\mathscr{D},\\
\mathbf{U}(\lambda^{*};x)^{-\dagger},&\lambda\in\mathscr{D}^{*},\\
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0}),&\mathbb{C}\setminus\overline{\mathscr{D}\cup\mathscr{D}^{*}},\end{cases}

where we recall the definition of
f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
in
(
4.17
). The definition
\mathbf{U}(\lambda;x)
has the effect
of removing the poles from
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})
since,
for
K=0
,

(5.2)

c_{n}(x,\mathbf{0})=\mathrm{i}\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{\lambda=\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{2f_{0}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})/\epsilon}}{\widetilde{a}(\lambda)}.

The matrix
\mathbf{U}(\lambda;x)
satisfies the following Riemann-Hilbert problem.

8
Problem for
\mathbf{t}=\mathbf{0}
and
x>X_{+}
.

Given
\epsilon>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, seek a
2\times 2
matrix function

\mathbf{U}(\lambda)=\mathbf{U}(\lambda;x)
with the following properties.

Analyticity:

\mathbf{U}(\lambda)
is analytic for

\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(\partial\mathscr{D}\cup\partial\mathscr{D}^{*})

and satisfies the Schwarz symmetry condition

\mathbf{U}(\lambda^{*})=\mathbf{U}(\lambda)^{-\dagger}
.

Jump conditions:

\mathbf{U}(\lambda)
takes continuous
boundary values on
\partial\mathscr{D}
and
\partial\mathscr{D}^{*}
.
Orienting both loops
\partial\mathscr{D}
and
\partial\mathscr{D}^{*}

in the clockwise direction, the jumps are:

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{+}(\lambda;x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{-}(\lambda;x)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\mathrm{i}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{0}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D},

(5.4)

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{+}(\lambda;x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{-}(\lambda;x)\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathrm{i}\widetilde{a}(\lambda^{*})^{*-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{0}(\lambda^{*};x,\mathbf{0})^{*}/\epsilon}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D}^{*}.

Normalization:

\mathbf{U}(\lambda)\to\mathbb{I}
as
\lambda\to\infty
.

Using (
3.13
), we can rewrite the jump on
\partial\mathscr{D}
as

(5.5)

\mathbf{U}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{U}_{-}(\lambda;x)\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\mathrm{i}Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{2F(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D},

where

(5.6)

2F(\lambda;x):=2f_{0}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})+L(\lambda).

We now show that as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
the factor
\mathrm{e}^{2F(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}
is exponentially small uniformly for
\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D}
bounded away from the origin, provided that the domain
\mathscr{D}
consists of points sufficiently close to the imaginary interval
0<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A_{\mathrm{max}}
. From
(
3.14
) and (
4.17
),

(5.7)

2F(\lambda;x)=\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda)+\Xi(\lambda)+2\mathrm{i}x\lambda+\overline{L}(\lambda)\pm\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda),\quad 0<\imag\{\lambda\}<A_{\mathrm{max}},\quad\pm\re\{\lambda\}>0.

From Lemma
3
, letting
\lambda
approach a positive imaginary value
\lambda\to\mathrm{i}s
from the domain
\pm\mathrm{Re}\{\lambda\}>0
gives limiting values

(5.8)

2F(\mathrm{i}s;x)=2(X_{+}-x)s-2\int_{x_{+}(s)}^{X_{+}}\sqrt{s^{2}-A(x)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathrm{i}\Phi(\mathrm{i}s)\pm\mathrm{i}\Phi(\mathrm{i}s),\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}.

From (
2.27
) we see that
\Phi(\mathrm{i}s)
is purely
real for
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
. Therefore the real part of
2F(\lambda;x)
is continuous across the imaginary axis, and we deduce that unambiguously

(5.9)

\re\{2F(\mathrm{i}s;x)\}=2(X_{+}-x)s-2\int_{x_{+}(s)}^{X_{+}}\sqrt{s^{2}-A(x)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad 0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}.

Since
x>X_{+}
and the integral is manifestly positive, it follows that

\re\{2F(\mathrm{i}s;x)\}<0
for
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
, as required. Moreover, if
s>0
is bounded away from zero, the upper bound on
\re\{2F(\mathrm{i}s;x)\}
can be replaced with a negative constant. By continuity of
\re\{2F(\lambda;x)\}
, this establishes the desired uniform exponential decay of
\mathrm{e}^{2F(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}
as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
.

We now assume further that for some small parameters
\delta>0
and
\sigma>0
, the part of
\partial\mathscr{D}
with
|\lambda|>\sigma
lies within the domain
D_{\sigma}
of Proposition
6
. Then it follows that also
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
is uniformly bounded, implying that for
\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D}
with
|\lambda|>\sigma
, we have

\mathbf{U}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{U}_{-}(\lambda;x)(\mathbb{I}+\text{exponentially small})

holding uniformly for
\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D}
bounded away from the origin in the limit
\epsilon\downarrow 0
.
By Schwarz symmetry the same holds for
\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D}^{*}
bounded
away from the origin. All together, we are assuming that the loop
\partial\mathscr{D}
is both sufficiently close to the imaginary segment
0<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A_{\mathrm{max}}
and that there is a fixed minimum distance between this segment and the part of the loop outside a small disk centered at the origin.

To model
\mathbf{U}(\lambda;x)
, it then suffices to analyze the jump matrix
in (
5.5
) near the origin. From (
4.17
) and

\Xi_{1}=-(X_{+}+X_{-})
(see Proposition (
2
)), the
function
2f_{0}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})
is analytic at
\lambda=0
with Taylor
expansion

(5.10)

2f_{0}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})=\mathrm{i}\Phi_{0}+2\mathrm{i}(x-\overline{X})\lambda+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{2}\right),\quad\overline{X}:=\frac{1}{2}(X_{+}+X_{-}),\quad\lambda\to 0.

Similarly, from (
3.14
) and (
4.2
),
L(\lambda)

has the expansions

(5.11)

L(\lambda)=\pm\mathrm{i}\Phi_{0}-\mathrm{i}(X_{+}-X_{-})\lambda+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{2}\right),\quad\lambda\to 0,\quad\pm\re\{\lambda\}>0.

From (
5.6
), (
5.10
), and (
5.11
), it
follows that

(5.12)

2F(\lambda;x)=\mathrm{i}\Phi_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}\Phi_{0}+2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{2}\right),\quad\lambda\to 0,\quad\pm\re\{\lambda\}>0.

Combining (
2.24
) and (
2.27
)
gives
\Phi_{0}=N\epsilon\pi
. Therefore,

(5.13)

\mathrm{e}^{2F(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}=\mathrm{e}^{[2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{2}\right)]/\epsilon}

in a neighborhood of
\lambda=0
regardless of the sign of
\re\{\lambda\}
, which affects the error term but not its order estimate.
Comparing this with (
5.5
) motivates the definition of the
following global approximation for
\mathbf{U}
:

(5.14)

\breve{\mathbf{U}}(\lambda;x):=\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\mathscr{D},\\
\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda/\epsilon}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in\mathscr{D}^{*},\\
\mathbb{I},&\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\overline{\mathscr{D}\cup\mathscr{D}^{*}}.\end{cases}

Note that
\det(\breve{\mathbf{U}}(\lambda;x))=1
and that
\breve{\mathbf{U}}(\lambda;x)=\sigma_{2}\breve{\mathbf{U}}(\lambda^{*};x)^{*}\sigma_{2}=\breve{\mathbf{U}}(\lambda^{*};x)^{-\dagger}
. The error is defined as
\mathbf{E}(\lambda;x):=\mathbf{U}(\lambda;x)\breve{\mathbf{U}}(\lambda;x)^{-1}
. It is clearly analytic for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(\partial\mathscr{D}\cup\partial\mathscr{D}^{*})
. It tends to the identity as
\lambda\to\infty
because
\mathbf{U}(\lambda;x)
does so while
\breve{\mathbf{U}}(\lambda;x)^{-1}=\mathbb{I}
for
|\lambda|
sufficiently large. Imposing the symmetry
\mathbf{E}(\lambda;x)=\sigma_{2}\mathbf{E}(\lambda^{*};x)^{*}\sigma_{2}=\mathbf{E}(\lambda^{*};x)^{-\dagger}
,
\mathbf{E}(\lambda;x)
is characterized by its jump condition across
\partial\mathscr{D}
, which according to (
5.5
) and (
5.14
) reads

(5.15)

\mathbf{E}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{E}_{-}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda;x),\\
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda;x):=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\mathrm{i}[Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{2F(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}-\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda/\epsilon}]&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D}.

We can write the jump condition across
\partial\mathscr{D}^{*}
in the same form
\mathbf{E}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{E}_{-}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda;x)
, where
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda^{*};x)^{-\dagger}
holds for
\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D}^{*}
.
With the clockwise orientation of both loops, the jump contour
\Sigma:=\partial\mathscr{D}\cup\partial\mathscr{D}^{*}
is a complete oriented contour that divides the complex plane into complementary regions:
\mathscr{D}\cup\mathscr{D}^{*}
on the right and
\mathbb{C}\setminus\overline{\mathscr{D}\cup\mathscr{D}^{*}}
on the left.

Since
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})=\mathbf{U}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{E}(\lambda;x)
for
|\lambda|
sufficiently large, using the Plemelj formula we can express
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{0})
exactly in the form

(5.16)

\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{0})=2\mathrm{i}\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda E_{12}(\lambda;x)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Sigma}(E_{12,+}(\lambda;x)-E_{12,-}(\lambda;x))\,\mathrm{d}\lambda,

which follows from

(5.17)

\mathbf{E}(\lambda;x)=\mathbb{I}+\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma}[\mathbf{E}_{+}(\diamond;x)-\mathbf{E}_{-}(\diamond;x)](\lambda),\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma,

in which for an arbitrary oriented contour
\Sigma
, the Cauchy transform of a matrix-valued function
\mathbf{F}(\diamond)
defined on
\Sigma
is given by

(5.18)

\mathcal{C}^{\Sigma}[\mathbf{F}(\diamond)](\lambda):=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{\Sigma}\frac{\mathbf{F}(\xi)\,\mathrm{d}\xi}{\xi-\lambda},\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\Sigma.

Letting
\lambda
tend to
\Sigma
from the right side in (
5.17
), denoting the resulting boundary value by
\boldsymbol{\mu}(\lambda;x):=\mathbf{E}_{-}(\lambda;x)
, and using
\mathbf{E}_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{E}_{-}(\lambda;x)\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda;x)
