yields the singular integral equation

(5.19)

\boldsymbol{\mu}(\lambda;x)-\mathcal{C}_{-}^{\Sigma}[\boldsymbol{\mu}(\diamond;x)(\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\diamond;x)-\mathbb{I})](\lambda)=\mathbb{I},\quad\lambda\in\Sigma.

In terms of the solution
\boldsymbol{\mu}(\lambda;x)
of this integral equation, from (
5.16
) we obtain

(5.20)

\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{0})=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Sigma}(\boldsymbol{\mu}(\lambda;x)(\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda;x)-\mathbb{I}))_{12}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda.

We note that since
\Sigma
may be taken to be a Lipschitz curve independent of
\epsilon
,
\mathcal{C}_{-}^{\Sigma}
is a bounded linear operator on
L^{2}(\Sigma)
, with fixed operator norm
\|\mathcal{C}_{-}^{\Sigma}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)\circlearrowleft}<\infty
.

We claim that
\|\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}-\mathbb{I}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)
. This would be implied by the assertion that

(5.21)

\sup_{\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D}}\left|Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{2F(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}-\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda/\epsilon}\right|=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right).

Combining Propositions
6
,
7
, and
8
shows that
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
is uniformly bounded for
\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D}
.
Picking an exponent
p<1
, the Taylor formula (
5.12
) shows that if
x>X_{+}
, then there is some
C>0
so that both
\mathrm{e}^{2F(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}
and
\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda/\epsilon}
are uniformly
\mathcal{O}\!\left(\mathrm{e}^{-C\epsilon^{p-1}}\right)
on
\partial\mathscr{D}
with
|\lambda|>\epsilon^{p}
. Therefore,

(5.22)

\begin{split}\mathop{\sup_{\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D}}}_{|\lambda|>\epsilon^{p}}\left|Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{2F(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}-\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda/\epsilon}\right|&=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\mathrm{e}^{-C\epsilon^{p-1}}\right)\\
&=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right).\end{split}

It therefore remains to prove that if
x>X_{+}
, then for some
p<1
,

(5.23)

\mathop{\sup_{\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D}}}_{|\lambda|\leq\epsilon^{p}}\left|\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda/\epsilon}\right|\cdot\left|Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{[2F(\lambda;x)-2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda]/\epsilon}-1\right|=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right).

For this, we assume now that in a neighborhood of the origin,
\partial\mathscr{D}
lies within the sector of opening angle strictly less than
\pi
symmetric about the positive imaginary axis. Then, because
x>X_{+}
, there is some constant
C>0
such that
|\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda/\epsilon}|\leq\mathrm{e}^{-C|\lambda|/\epsilon}
holds for all
\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D}
. If
p>\tfrac{1}{2}
, then
\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D}
with
|\lambda|\leq\epsilon^{p}
implies, using (
5.12
), that

(5.24)

\begin{split}\mathrm{e}^{[2F(\lambda;x)-2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda]/\epsilon}&=1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\epsilon}\right)\\
&=1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{\lambda}{\epsilon^{1/2}}\right).\end{split}

Under the same conditions, using Propositions
7
and
8
gives

(5.25)

\begin{split}Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)&=\mathcal{Y}_{0}\left(\frac{\varphi(\lambda)}{\epsilon^{1/2}}\right)\left(1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\lambda\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right)\\
&=\left(1-2\mathrm{i}(\sqrt{2}-1)\zeta(\tfrac{1}{2})\frac{\varphi(\lambda)}{\epsilon^{1/2}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{\varphi(\lambda)^{2}}{\epsilon}\right)\right)\left(1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\lambda\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right)\\
&=\left(1-2\mathrm{i}(\sqrt{2}-1)\zeta(\tfrac{1}{2})\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(0)\lambda}{\epsilon^{1/2}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\epsilon}\right)\right)\left(1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\lambda\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right)\\
&=\left(1+k\frac{\lambda}{\epsilon^{1/2}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\epsilon}\right)\right)\left(1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\lambda\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right)\\
&=1+k\frac{\lambda}{\epsilon^{1/2}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\lambda\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\epsilon}\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{\lambda^{3}}{\epsilon^{1/2}}\right)\\
&=1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{\lambda}{\epsilon^{1/2}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right),\end{split}

where
k:=-2\mathrm{i}(\sqrt{2}-1)\zeta(\frac{1}{2})/v(0)
.
Therefore, if
\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D}
with
|\lambda|\leq\epsilon^{p}
and
p>\tfrac{1}{2}
,

(5.26)

\left|\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda/\epsilon}\right|\cdot\left|Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{[2F(\lambda;x)-2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda]/\epsilon}-1\right|=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\frac{\lambda}{\epsilon}\mathrm{e}^{-C|\lambda|/\epsilon}\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\mathrm{e}^{-C|\lambda|/\epsilon}\right).

Because
\mathrm{e}^{-Cy}
and
y\mathrm{e}^{-Cy}
are both uniformly bounded functions of
y\geq 0
, the result (
5.23
) follows assuming that
p>\frac{1}{2}
. Therefore choosing any
p\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
and combining with (
5.22
) yields (
5.21
) and therefore
\|\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}-\mathbb{I}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)
as desired.

Since
\|\mathcal{C}_{-}^{\Sigma}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)\circlearrowleft}
is finite and independent of
\epsilon
, it follows that the composition with multiplication on the right by
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}-\mathbb{I}
yields a bounded linear operator on
L^{2}(\Sigma)
with norm
\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{1/2}\right)
. Therefore, the singular integral equation (
5.19
) can be solved by Neumann series provided that
\epsilon
is sufficiently small. In particular, this implies that (after one explicit iteration)

(5.27)

\boldsymbol{\mu}-\mathbb{I}-\mathcal{C}_{-}^{\Sigma}[\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}-\mathbb{I}]=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\quad\text{in $L^{2}(\Sigma)$}.

Now, the first line of (
5.22
) and the estimate (
5.26
), along with the Schwarz symmetry
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda;x)=\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda^{*};x)^{-\dagger}
to obtain similar estimates for
\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D}^{*}
, imply that
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}-\mathbb{I}=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)
in
L^{2}(\Sigma)
. Therefore,
by Cauchy-Schwarz (
5.20
) implies that

(5.28)

\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{0})=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda;x)-\mathbb{I})_{12}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\\
{}-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Sigma}\left(\mathcal{C}_{-}^{\Sigma}[\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\diamond;x)-\mathbb{I}](\lambda)(\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda;x)-\mathbb{I})\right)_{12}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{2}\right).

Since
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}-\mathbb{I}
is an off-diagonal matrix at each point of
\Sigma
, so is its Cauchy transform
\mathcal{C}_{-}^{\Sigma}[\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\diamond;x)-\mathbb{I}](\lambda)
. Therefore their product is diagonal, so the integral on the second line vanishes and hence

(5.29)

\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{0})=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda;x)-\mathbb{I})_{12}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{2}\right).

Moreover,
\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda;x)
is lower-triangular for
\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D}
, so there is no contribution to the integral from
\partial\mathscr{D}
:

(5.30)

\begin{split}-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda;x)-\mathbb{I})_{12}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda&=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\partial\mathscr{D}^{*}}(\mathbf{V}^{\mathbf{E}}(\lambda;x)-\mathbb{I})_{12}\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\\
&=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\pi}\int_{\partial\mathscr{D}^{*}}\left(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda^{*})^{*}\mathrm{e}^{2F(\lambda^{*};x)^{*}/\epsilon}-\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda/\epsilon}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\\
&=\left[-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\pi}\int_{\partial\mathscr{D}}\left(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{2F(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}-\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda/\epsilon}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda\right]^{*},\end{split}

where in the last line we used Schwarz reflection.
Now, a more refined version of (
5.26
) is obtained from using instead the penultimate lines of (
5.24
) and (
5.25
). For
\lambda\in\partial\mathscr{D}
with
|\lambda|\leq\epsilon^{p}
and
p>\frac{1}{2}
,

(5.31)

Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{2F(\lambda;x)/\epsilon}-\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda/\epsilon}\\
\begin{aligned} &=\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda/\epsilon}\left(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)\mathrm{e}^{[2F(\lambda;x)-2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda]/\epsilon}-1\right)\\
{}&=\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}(x-X_{+})\lambda/\epsilon}\left(k\frac{\lambda}{\epsilon^{1/2}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{3/2}\frac{\lambda}{\epsilon}\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\left(\frac{\lambda}{\epsilon}\right)^{2}\right)\right.\\
&\qquad{}\left.+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{3/2}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\epsilon}\right)^{3}\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{2}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\epsilon}\right)^{4}\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{7/2}\left(\frac{\lambda}{\epsilon}\right)^{5}\right)\right).\end{aligned}

Taking
p\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
and making use of the first line of (
5.22
), the same left-hand side is exponentially small on the complement of
\partial\mathscr{D}
. We therefore see that at the cost of an exponentially small error, the integral in (
5.30
) of the explicit term on the right-hand side of (
5.31
)
can be extended to the closed curve
\partial\mathscr{D}
where it integrates to zero by Cauchy’s theorem. The remaining six terms on the right-hand side of (
5.31
) have unspecified analyticity properties, so to estimate the integral in (
5.30
), the best we can do is
L^{1}(\partial\mathscr{D})
estimates, which by scaling are
all
\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{2}\right)
as
y^{p}\mathrm{e}^{-Cy}
is integrable on
y>0
for
p=0,\dots,5
. We conclude from (
5.29
) that
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{0})=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{2}\right)
when
x>X_{+}
, and clearly the estimate is uniform for
x
in compact subsets of the indicated interval.

To obtain the corresponding estimate for
x<X_{-}
, we start instead from the matrix
\mathbf{M}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})
solving Riemann-Hilbert Problem
2
and proceed similarly, now constructing
F(\lambda;x)
from
f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{0})
for a nonzero
K\in\mathbb{Z}
.

This completes the proof of Theorem
2.1
.

6.
Dispersive regularization of Talanov focusing

In this section we prove Theorems
2.77
,
2.3
,
and
2.4
. Our starting point is
Riemann-Hilbert Problem
1
with solution

\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
.
Let
D
denote a half disk in the upper half-plane
\mathbb{C}_{+}
with center at the origin and radius
L>0
sufficiently large to contain the points
\lambda=\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{j}
,
j=0,\dots,N-1
for all
N
, and such that the boundary
\partial D
satisfies
\partial D\cap\mathbb{R}=[-L,L]
. See Figure
12
.

D
D^{*}
-L
L

Figure 12.
The regions
D
and
D^{*}
.

Fix
K\in\mathbb{Z}
, and let
\widetilde{a}(\lambda)
be defined by the Blaschke product (
3.12
).
Because they are polynomials by assumption,
\Phi(\lambda)
and
\Xi(\lambda)
are analytic for
\lambda\in D
, and
we define a new unknown related to
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
as follows:

(6.1)

\mathbf{N}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t}):=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix},&\lambda\in D,\\
\mathbf{N}(\lambda^{*};x,\mathbf{t})^{-\dagger},&\lambda\in D^{*},\\
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t}),&\mathbb{C}\setminus\overline{D\cup D^{*}},\end{cases}

where we recall the definition of
f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
in (
4.17
).
One then checks from the conditions of Riemann-Hilbert Problem
1
that, since
c_{n}(x,\mathbf{t})
can be written in the form

(6.2)

c_{n}(x,\mathbf{t})=\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{\lambda=\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})/\epsilon}}{\widetilde{a}(\lambda)},

the new unknown
\mathbf{N}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
has removable singularities at all poles of
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
and so is piecewise analytic in
D
,
D^{*}
, and the unbounded exterior domain, taking continuous boundary values on its jump contour consisting of the three arcs
\partial D\setminus[-L,L]
,
(-L,L)
, and
\partial D^{*}\setminus[-L,L]
. The definition also preserves the normalization condition:
\mathbf{N}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\to\mathbb{I}
as
\lambda\to\infty
. Assuming that the three arcs are oriented from
-L
toward
L
as shown in Figure
12
, and that boundary values from the left (right) are indicated with a subscript of “
+
” (“
-
”) the jump conditions satisfied by
\mathbf{N}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
are as follows:

(6.3)

\mathbf{N}_{+}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})=\mathbf{N}_{-}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in\partial D\setminus[-L,L],

(6.4)

\mathbf{N}_{+}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})=\mathbf{N}_{-}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda^{*})^{*-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda^{*};x,\mathbf{t})^{*}/\epsilon}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in\partial D^{*}\setminus[-L,L],

and for
\lambda\in(-L,L)
,

(6.5)

\mathbf{N}_{+}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\\
\begin{aligned} &=\mathbf{N}_{-}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{*-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})^{*}/\epsilon}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix}\\
&=\mathbf{N}_{-}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\begin{pmatrix}1+\mathrm{e}^{2[f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})+f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})^{*}]/\epsilon}&\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{*-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})^{*}/\epsilon}\\
-\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix},\end{aligned}

where on the last line we used the identity
\widetilde{a}(\lambda)\widetilde{a}(\lambda^{*})^{*}=1
.

10
.

A similar substitution can made in the equivalent Riemann-Hilbert problem for
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
based on the identity

(6.6)

c_{n}^{\updownarrow}(x,\mathbf{t})=-\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{\lambda=\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{n}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})/\epsilon}}{\widetilde{a}(\lambda)}.

One sets

(6.7)

\mathbf{N}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t}):=\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})/\epsilon}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in D,

and then defines
\mathbf{N}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t}):=\sigma_{2}\mathbf{N}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda^{*};x,\mathbf{t})^{*}\sigma_{2}=\mathbf{N}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})^{-\dagger}
for
\lambda\in D^{*}
to maintain Schwarz symmetry, and sets
\mathbf{N}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t}):=\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}^{\updownarrow}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
for
\lambda
outside the closure of
D\cup D^{*}
. This defines a matrix function of
\lambda
that is analytic except on the three arcs
\partial D\setminus[-L,L]
,
(-L,L)
, and
\partial D^{*}\setminus[-L,L]
, and that tends to the identity as
\lambda\to\infty
. The jump conditions across the three arcs read

(6.8)

\mathbf{N}^{\updownarrow}_{+}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})=\mathbf{N}^{\updownarrow}_{-}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\begin{pmatrix}1&-\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})/\epsilon}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in\partial D\setminus[-L,L],

(6.9)

\mathbf{N}^{\updownarrow}_{-}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})=\mathbf{N}^{\updownarrow}_{-}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda^{*})^{*-1}\mathrm{e}^{-2f_{K}(\lambda^{*};x,\mathbf{t})^{*}/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in\partial D^{*}\setminus[-L,L],

and, for
\lambda\in(-L,L)
,

(6.10)

\mathbf{N}^{\updownarrow}_{+}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\\
\begin{aligned} &=\mathbf{N}^{\updownarrow}_{-}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{*-1}\mathrm{e}^{-2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})^{*}/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})/\epsilon}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\\
&=\mathbf{N}^{\updownarrow}_{-}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\begin{pmatrix}1&\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})/\epsilon}\\
-\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{*-1}\mathrm{e}^{-2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})^{*}/\epsilon}&1+\mathrm{e}^{-2[f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})+f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})^{*}]/\epsilon}\end{pmatrix}.\end{aligned}

Note that according to Propositions
1
and
2
, the polynomials
\Phi(\lambda)
and
\Xi(\lambda)
have coefficients that are real and imaginary, respectively, and it therefore follows that

(6.11)

f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})+f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})^{*}=0,\quad\lambda\in(-L,L).

The jump matrix in the jump condition (
6.5
) naturally arises as a “UL” product; however it also admits a factorization of “LU” type. Indeed, using (
6.11
) and again taking into account that
\widetilde{a}(\lambda)\widetilde{a}(\lambda^{*})^{*}=1
it follows that for
\lambda\in(-L,L)
,

(6.12)

\begin{pmatrix}1+\mathrm{e}^{2[f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})+f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})^{*}]/\epsilon}&\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{*-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})^{*}/\epsilon}\\
-\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix}\\
{}=\begin{pmatrix}2^{1/2}&0\\
-2^{-1/2}\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})/\epsilon}&2^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}2^{1/2}&2^{-1/2}\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{*-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})^{*}/\epsilon}\\
0&2^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}.

To exploit this alternate factorization, note that
\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}
is analytic for
\lambda\in D^{*}
, as its poles lie in the domain
D
, and
f(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
and
f(\lambda^{*};x,\mathbf{t})^{*}
are polynomials in
\lambda
. Therefore, defining a matrix function
\mathbf{O}(\lambda;x,t)
by

(6.13)

\mathbf{O}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t}):=\mathbf{N}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\begin{pmatrix}2^{1/2}&2^{-1/2}\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda^{*})^{*-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda^{*};x,\mathbf{t})^{*}/\epsilon}\\
0&2^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}^{-1},\quad\lambda\in D,

(6.14)

\mathbf{O}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t}):=\mathbf{N}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\begin{pmatrix}2^{1/2}&0\\
-2^{-1/2}\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})/\epsilon}&2^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix},\quad\lambda\in D^{*},

and by
\mathbf{O}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t}):=\mathbf{N}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
in the exterior domain, we see easily that
\mathbf{O}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
is analytic where defined, and by comparing with (
6.5
) and (
6.12
) we see that
\mathbf{O}_{+}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})=\mathbf{O}_{-}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
for
\lambda\in(-L,L)
. Therefore an argument based on Morera’s theorem shows that
\mathbf{O}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
may be considered to be analytic in the interior of the closure of
D\cup D^{*}
, so its only jump occurs across the non-real arcs of
\partial D
and
\partial D^{*}
, which form a circle
C
containing all of the points
\lambda=\pm\mathrm{i}\widetilde{s}_{j}
,
j=0,\dots,N-1
in its interior. Taking the circle
C
to have clockwise orientation, a computation shows that the jump of
\mathbf{O}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
across
C
takes the same analytic form regardless of whether
\lambda
is in the lower or upper half-plane, specifically

(6.15)

\mathbf{O}_{+}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\\
\begin{aligned} &=\mathbf{O}_{-}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\begin{pmatrix}2^{1/2}&2^{-1/2}\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda^{*})^{*-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda^{*};x,\mathbf{t})^{*}/\epsilon}\\
0&2^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})/\epsilon}&1\end{pmatrix}\\
&=\mathbf{O}_{-}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\begin{pmatrix}2^{-1/2}&2^{-1/2}\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda^{*})^{*-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda^{*};x,\mathbf{t})^{*}/\epsilon}\\
2^{-1/2}\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}\widetilde{a}(\lambda)^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})/\epsilon}&2^{-1/2}\end{pmatrix}\end{aligned}

for
\lambda\in C
.

Now fix a positive scaling factor
\nu>0
to be determined, and consider the asymptotic behavior of the function
\widetilde{a}(\nu\epsilon^{-1}\Lambda)
as
\epsilon=\epsilon_{N}\to 0
with
|\Lambda|=1
fixed. We obtain

(6.16)

\begin{split}\widetilde{a}(\nu\epsilon^{-1}\Lambda)&=\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{1-\mathrm{i}\nu^{-1}\Lambda^{-1}\epsilon\widetilde{s}_{j}}{1+\mathrm{i}\nu^{-1}\Lambda^{-1}\epsilon\widetilde{s}_{j}}\\
&=\exp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\log\left(\frac{1-\mathrm{i}\nu^{-1}\Lambda^{-1}\epsilon\widetilde{s}_{j}}{1+\mathrm{i}\nu^{-1}\Lambda^{-1}\epsilon\widetilde{s}_{j}}\right)\right)\\
&=\exp\left(-\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\left[2\mathrm{i}\nu^{-1}\Lambda^{-1}\epsilon\widetilde{s}_{j}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3}\widetilde{s}_{j}^{3})\right]\right)\\
&=\exp\left(-2\mathrm{i}\nu^{-1}\Lambda^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\epsilon\widetilde{s}_{j}\right)(1+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})),\end{split}

where we used only the facts that
0<\widetilde{s}_{j}<A_{\mathrm{max}}
for all
j
and
\epsilon
, and that
N\epsilon=\mathcal{O}(1)
. Recalling (
2.63
)–(
2.64
), we substitute into (
6.16
) with the choice of
\nu
given in (
2.77
)
to obtain that

(6.17)

\widetilde{a}(\nu\epsilon^{-1}\Lambda)=\mathrm{e}^{-4\mathrm{i}\Lambda^{-1}}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{2}\right)\right),\quad\epsilon\to 0

holds uniformly for
|\Lambda|=1
.
Next, observe that

(6.18)

\begin{split}2f_{K}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})&=\mathrm{i}(2K+1)\Phi(\lambda)+\Xi(\lambda)+2\mathrm{i}\left(\lambda x+\sum_{n=2}^{M}\lambda^{n}t_{n}\right)\\
&=\mathrm{i}\left(\sum_{p=0}^{\mathcal{P}}(2K+1)\Phi_{p}\lambda^{2p}+\sum_{q=1}^{\mathcal{Q}}\Xi_{q}\lambda^{2q-1}+2x\lambda+\sum_{m=2}^{M}2t_{m}\lambda^{m}\right).\end{split}

Consequently, setting
\lambda=\nu\epsilon^{-1}\Lambda
and replacing
x
and
\mathbf{t}
according to the left-hand side of (
2.76
), we obtain

(6.19)

2f_{K}\left(\frac{\nu\Lambda}{\epsilon};x^{\circ}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\nu}X,\mathbf{t}^{\circ}+\left(\frac{\epsilon^{3}}{\nu^{2}}T_{2},\frac{\epsilon^{4}}{\nu^{3}}T_{3},\dots,\frac{\epsilon^{M+1}}{\nu^{M}}T_{M}\right)\right)\\
{}=\mathrm{i}(2K+1)\Phi_{0}+2\mathrm{i}\epsilon\left(X\Lambda+\sum_{m=2}^{M}T_{m}\Lambda^{m}\right).

We deal with the constant term
\mathrm{i}(2K+1)\Phi_{0}
in (
6.19
) along with the factors
\mathrm{i}(-1)^{K}
in the jump condition (
6.15
) by introducing one more transformation, a diagonal constant conjugation:

(6.20)

\mathbf{P}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t}):=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(2K+1)(\pi-2\Phi_{0}/\epsilon)\sigma_{3}/4}\mathbf{O}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(2K+1)(\pi-2\Phi_{0}/\epsilon)\sigma_{3}/4}.

Note that
\mathbf{P}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
is analytic for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus C
and
\mathbf{P}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\to\mathbb{I}
as
\lambda\to\infty
.
Next, we specify the radius
L
of the circle
C
as
L=\nu/\epsilon
. In terms of the variable
\Lambda
, this corresponds to
|\Lambda|=1
.
The jump condition across this circle satisfied by
\mathbf{P}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
then takes the form

(6.21)

\mathbf{P}_{+}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})=\mathbf{P}_{-}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})\left[\mathbf{R}_{-}(\Lambda)^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{+}(\Lambda)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right],\quad|\Lambda|=1,

where the matrix
\mathbf{R}_{-}(\Lambda)^{-1}\mathbf{R}_{+}(\Lambda)
is defined in (
2.72
), and the error estimate is uniform for
|\Lambda|=1
and for
(X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})
bounded in
\mathbb{R}^{M}
.
The conclusion of small-norm theory is then that

(6.22)

\mathbf{P}\left(\frac{\nu\Lambda}{\epsilon};x^{\circ}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\nu}X,\mathbf{t}^{\circ}+\left(\frac{\epsilon^{3}}{\nu^{2}}T_{2},\frac{\epsilon^{4}}{\nu^{3}}T_{3},\dots,\frac{\epsilon^{M+1}}{\nu^{M}}T_{M}\right)\right)=\mathbf{R}(\Lambda;X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)

as
\epsilon\to 0
, where
\mathbf{R}(\Lambda;X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})
is the solution of Riemann-Hilbert Problem
3
and the error is uniform for bounded
(X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})
. Analogous formulæ hold for each of the coefficients of the Laurent expansion of both sides in descending powers of
\Lambda
.
Since the semiclassical soliton ensemble
\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{t})
is encoded in
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
by (
2.58
), since
\mathbf{O}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
agrees with
\widetilde{\mathbf{M}}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
in a neighborhood of
\lambda=\infty
,
and since
O_{12}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})=(-1)^{K}\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\Phi_{0}/\epsilon}P_{12}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})
, we get that

(6.23)

\widetilde{\psi}(x,\mathbf{t})=(-1)^{K}\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\Phi_{0}/\epsilon}2\mathrm{i}\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda P_{12}(\lambda;x,\mathbf{t})=(-1)^{K}\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\Phi_{0}/\epsilon}\frac{\nu}{\epsilon}2\mathrm{i}\lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}\Lambda P_{12}\left(\frac{\nu\Lambda}{\epsilon};x,\mathbf{t}\right).

Therefore, using the Laurent expansion of (
6.22
) and combining with (
2.73
) yields
(
2.76
) and completes the proof of Theorem
2.77
.

The proof of Theorem
2.3
also follows the same reasoning, except that since the time variables
t_{2},t_{3},\dots,t_{M}
are in proportion by
t_{m}=a_{m}t
, taking
t=\epsilon^{M+1}T_{M}/(a_{M}\nu^{M})
forces the lower-indexed rescaled times
T_{m}
for
m=2,\dots,M-1
to be small of order
T_{m}=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{M-m}\right)
. Therefore we may replace
\mathbf{R}(\Lambda;X,T_{2},T_{3},\dots,T_{M})
by
\mathbf{R}(\Lambda;X,0,0,\dots,0,T_{M})
at the cost of an additional error term proportional to
\epsilon
. The only remaining part of the proof is to observe the conditions on the flow mixture coefficients
a_{2},a_{3},\dots,a_{M}
such that the line parametrized by
(a_{2}t,a_{3}t,\dots,a_{M}t)\in\mathbb{R}^{M-1}
meets all (in the case that
\Xi(\lambda)
is a linear function and the even coefficients are correctly chosen) or one (in the case that
\Xi(\lambda)
has nonlinear terms and all the coefficients are correctly chosen given
K\in\mathbb{Z}
) of the focal points.

Appendix A
Proofs of asymptotic properties of
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
and
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
.

Here we collect proofs of the results stated in Section
3.2
that describe the local asymptotic behavior of the functions
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
and
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
in different regions of the complex plane in the limit
\epsilon\downarrow 0
. For the convenience of the reader the statements of each result proved below is preceded by its (re)statement.

See
6

Proof of Proposition
6
.

For

\lambda
in the indicated domain, in particular we have
\imag\{\lambda\}>0
with
-\mathrm{i}\lambda\not\in[0,A_{\mathrm{max}}]
, in which case

(A.1)

\epsilon\log(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda))=\int_{0}^{A_{\mathrm{max}}}\log\left(\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}s}{\lambda+\mathrm{i}s}\right)\rho(s)\,\mathrm{d}s-\epsilon\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\log\left(\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}s_{n}}{\lambda+\mathrm{i}s_{n}}\right).

Making a substitution
s\mapsto r
by using (
3.17
), this becomes:

(A.2)

\epsilon\log(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda))=\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}\log\left(\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}s(r)}{\lambda+\mathrm{i}s(r)}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r-\epsilon\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\log\left(\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}s(r_{n})}{\lambda+\mathrm{i}s(r_{n})}\right),\quad r_{n}=(n+\tfrac{1}{2})\epsilon.

Note that for convenience, in (
A.1
)–(
A.2
) we are labeling the sample points
s_{n}
and
r_{n}
in reverse order, increasing with
n
. The main thrust of the proof is to express the integrand in (
A.2
) as a sum of a function with an integrable second derivative, to which a standard theorem concerning Riemann sum approximation applies, and a more singular term that needs special treatment. The latter term is more singular because unlike in
[
2
]
where the analogue of
s(r)
vanishes linearly at
r=0
, here
s(r)
vanishes like
r^{1/2}
by (
3.18
).

To this end, we begin with the convergent series

(A.3)

\log\left(\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}s(r)}{\lambda+\mathrm{i}s(r)}\right)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\kappa_{k}\left(\frac{s(r)}{\lambda}\right)^{2k+1},\quad 0<s(r)<|\lambda|,\quad\kappa_{0}=-2\mathrm{i}.

Of course, although this series on the right-hand side is only convergent for
s(r)<|\lambda|
, the sum of the series appearing on the left-hand side is analytic
for
0<r<\Phi(0)/\pi
. We claim that the function
F(r;\lambda)
defined by

(A.4)

F(r;\lambda):=\log\left(\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}s(r)}{\lambda+\mathrm{i}s(r)}\right)-\kappa_{0}v_{0}\frac{\sqrt{r}}{\lambda}

has a second derivative with respect to
r
that is absolutely integrable on
(0,\Phi(0)/\pi)
whenever
\imag\{\lambda\}>0
and
-\mathrm{i}\lambda\not\in[0,A_{\mathrm{max}}]
. Indeed, comparing with (
A.3
), we can write
F(r;\lambda)
in the form

(A.5)

F(r;\lambda)=r^{3/2}G(r;\lambda),

where
G(r;\lambda)
is analytic at
r=0
with
G(0;\lambda)\neq 0
, and
G(\diamond;\lambda)
has an analytic continuation to
0\leq r<\Phi(0)/\pi
. Moreover, the condition
A^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})<0
implies that the analyticity of
G(r;\lambda)

extends to the endpoint
r=\Phi(0)/\pi
. The integrability of
F_{rr}(r;\lambda)
on
(0,\Phi(0)/\pi)
then follows from that of
r^{-1/2}
at
r=0
. In more detail,
we compute explicitly

(A.6)

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}F}{\mathrm{d}r^{2}}(r;\lambda)=\frac{4\mathrm{i}z(r)z^{\prime}(r)^{2}}{(1+z(r)^{2})^{2}}+\frac{\kappa_{0}v_{0}}{4\lambda r^{3/2}}-\frac{2\mathrm{i}z^{\prime\prime}(r)}{1+z(r)^{2}},\quad z(r):=\frac{s(r)}{\lambda}.

Since from (
3.18
) we have

(A.7)

\displaystyle s(r)=r^{1/2}v(r),\quad s^{\prime}(r)=\tfrac{1}{2}r^{-1/2}v(r)+r^{1/2}v^{\prime}(r),

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime}(r)=-\tfrac{1}{4}r^{-3/2}v(r)+r^{-1/2}v^{\prime}(r)+r^{1/2}v^{\prime\prime}(r),

using the fact that
v
is analytic on
[0,\Phi(0)/\pi]
with
v(0)>0
, we get

(A.8)

\begin{split}\frac{\kappa_{0}v_{0}}{4\lambda r^{3/2}}-\frac{2\mathrm{i}z^{\prime\prime}(r)}{1+z(r)^{2}}&=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\lambda r^{1/2}}\frac{r^{-1}(v(r)-v_{0})-4v^{\prime}(r)-4rv^{\prime\prime}(r)}{1+z(r)^{2}}-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\lambda^{3}r^{1/2}}\frac{v_{0}v(r)^{2}}{1+z(r)^{2}}\\
&=\mathcal{O}\!\left(r^{-1/2}\right).\end{split}

Similarly,

(A.9)

\begin{split}\frac{4\mathrm{i}z(r)z^{\prime}(r)^{2}}{(1+z(r)^{2})^{2}}&=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\lambda^{3}r^{1/2}}\frac{v(r)^{3}+4rv(r)^{2}v^{\prime}(r)+4r^{2}v(r)v^{\prime}(r)^{2}}{(1+z(r)^{2})^{2}}\\
&=\mathcal{O}\!\left(r^{-1/2}\right).\end{split}

The estimates in (
A.8
)–(
A.9
) hold uniformly as
\lambda
varies in
D_{\sigma}
.
It follows that

(A.10)

\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}|F_{rr}(r;\lambda)|\,\mathrm{d}r=\mathcal{O}\!\left(|\lambda|^{-1}\right),\quad\lambda\in D_{\sigma}.

Rewriting (
A.2
) using (
A.4
)

(A.11)

\epsilon\log(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda))=\frac{\kappa_{0}v_{0}}{\lambda}\left[\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}r^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}r-\epsilon\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}r_{n}^{1/2}\right]+\left[\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}F(r;\lambda)\,\mathrm{d}r-\epsilon\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}F(r_{n};\lambda)\right]

and applying the following basic inequality
2
2

2

The inequality (
A.12
) also holds if the function
f
is defined on
(0,\infty)
with the limits of the integrals adjusted accordingly and with the upper limit of the summation taken to be
k=\infty
.
from the theory of “midpoint rule” Riemann sums:

(A.12)

\left|\int_{0}^{1}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}f\left(\frac{k-\tfrac{1}{2}}{N}\right)\right|\leq\frac{1}{2N^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}|f^{\prime\prime}(x)|\,\mathrm{d}x,

now allows the second term on the right-hand side of (
A.11
) to be easily estimated from (
A.10
).
Here we use the fact that the sample points
r_{n}
are equally spaced with spacing
\epsilon
and centered as midpoints of
N
equal-length subintervals of
(0,\Phi(0)/\pi)
; thus
the second term
on the right-hand side of (
A.11
) is
\mathcal{O}\!\left(N^{-2}\right)=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{2}\right)
as a consequence of (
A.10
).
It therefore only remains to approximate the first term on the right-hand side of (
A.11
), for which we calculate directly:

(A.13)

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}r^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}r-\epsilon\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}r_{n}^{1/2}&=\int_{0}^{N\epsilon}r^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}r-\epsilon\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}r_{n}^{1/2}\\
&=\epsilon^{3/2}\left(\tfrac{2}{3}N^{3/2}-\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}(n+\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}\right).\end{split}

Now, for a function
f(x)
smooth on
[0,N-1]
, the first-order Euler-Maclaurin summation formula reads

(A.14)

\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}f(n)=\int_{0}^{N-1}f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{f(N-1)+f(0)}{2}+\int_{0}^{N-1}f^{\prime}(x)(x-\lfloor x\rfloor-\tfrac{1}{2})\,\mathrm{d}x.

Applying this to the function
f(x):=(x+\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}
which is smooth for
x>0
gives

(A.15)

\begin{split}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}(n+\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}&=\int_{0}^{N-1}(x+\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{(N-\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}-(\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{N-1}\frac{x-\lfloor x\rfloor-\tfrac{1}{2}}{(x+\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\tfrac{2}{3}N^{3/2}+\frac{1}{6\sqrt{2}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(N^{-1/2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{N-1}\frac{x-\lfloor x\rfloor-\tfrac{1}{2}}{(x+\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}}\,\mathrm{d}x,\quad N\to\infty.\end{split}

The last integral has a limit as
N\to\infty
because for any positive integer
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,

(A.16)

\begin{split}\int_{m-1}^{m}\frac{x-\lfloor x\rfloor-\tfrac{1}{2}}{(x+\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}}\mathrm{d}x&=\int_{m-1}^{m}\frac{x+\tfrac{1}{2}-m}{(x+\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=\tfrac{2}{3}\left((m+\tfrac{1}{2})^{3/2}-(m-\tfrac{1}{2})^{3/2}\right)-2m\left((m+\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}-(m-\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}\right)\\
&=\mathcal{O}\!\left(m^{-3/2}\right),\quad m\to\infty.\end{split}

To identify the limit, we use this result to write

(A.17)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{N-1}\frac{x-\lfloor x\rfloor-\tfrac{1}{2}}{(x+\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}}\,\mathrm{d}x&=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1/2}^{0}\frac{x-\lfloor x\rfloor-\tfrac{1}{2}}{(x+\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}}\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1/2}^{\infty}\frac{x-\lfloor x\rfloor-\tfrac{1}{2}}{(x+\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}}\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{O}\!\left(N^{-1/2}\right)\\
&=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1/2}^{0}(x+\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1/2}^{\infty}\frac{x-\lfloor x\rfloor-\tfrac{1}{2}}{(x+\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}}\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{O}\!\left(N^{-1/2}\right)\\
&=-\frac{1}{6\sqrt{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1/2}^{\infty}\frac{x-\lfloor x\rfloor-\tfrac{1}{2}}{(x+\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}}\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{O}\!\left(N^{-1/2}\right),\quad N\to\infty.\end{split}

According to
[
22
, 25.11.26]
, the remaining integral is a Hurwitz zeta function:

(A.18)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1/2}^{\infty}\frac{x-\lfloor x\rfloor-\tfrac{1}{2}}{(x+\tfrac{1}{2})^{1/2}}\,\mathrm{d}x=\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2}),

which, by
[
22
, 25.11.11]
can be written explicitly in terms of a Riemann zeta value:

(A.19)

\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{1}{2})=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}-1\right)\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2}).

Using (
A.15
) with (
A.17
)–(
A.19
) in (
A.13
) gives

(A.20)

\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}r^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}r-\epsilon\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}r_{n}^{1/2}=\epsilon^{3/2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2})+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{2}\right),\quad\epsilon\to 0,

because
N
is inversely proportional to
\epsilon
. Note that
\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2})\approx-0.207886
.

Then, the first term on the right-hand side of (
A.11
) is
\kappa_{0}v_{0}(1-1/\sqrt{2})\zeta(-\frac{1}{2})\epsilon^{3/2}/\lambda+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{2}\right)
where the error estimate holds in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
uniformly for
\lambda\in D_{\sigma}
.
Now we divide by
\epsilon
in (
A.11
) and exponentiate to obtain

Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=1+\frac{\kappa_{0}v_{0}}{\lambda}\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2})\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right).

The result then follows by recalling that
\kappa_{0}=-2\mathrm{i}
.
∎

See
7

Proof.

Let

\xi
be related to
\lambda
by the relations
\lambda=\mathrm{i}s(\xi)
or
\xi=(\Phi(0)-\Phi(\lambda))/\pi
. Thus,
\xi
is an even analytic function of
\lambda
near
\lambda=0
with
\xi=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{2}\right)
as
\lambda\to 0
. We may therefore define
\xi^{1/2}
as an odd conformal mapping on a neighborhood of the origin with the property that it maps small positive imaginary values of
\lambda
to small positive values of
\xi^{1/2}
, and the conformal map defined in (
3.26
) is related by a rotation:
\varphi_{0}(\lambda)=\mathrm{i}\xi^{1/2}
.
Consider the function

(A.21)

G^{\pm}(r;\lambda):=\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}s(\xi)\pm\mathrm{i}s(r)}{\xi^{1/2}\pm r^{1/2}}\right),\quad 0<r<\Phi(0)/\pi.

If
|\lambda|
is sufficiently small,
G^{\pm}(r;\lambda)
will be an analytic function of
r
in the indicated open interval; indeed, since from (
3.18
)
s(r)=r^{1/2}v(r)
with
v
analytic at
r=0
and
v(0)>0
,
\mathrm{e}^{G^{\pm}(r;\lambda)}

is uniformly bounded and bounded away from zero, and the same is true of
\mathrm{e}^{G^{-}(r;\lambda)}
even at
r=\xi
should
\xi
be positive real. Note that

(A.22)

G^{\pm}(0;\lambda)=\log\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}s(\xi)}{\xi^{1/2}}\right)=\log(\mathrm{i}v(\xi)),

which is well-defined with imaginary part close to
\pi/2
for
\lambda
small. Then

(A.23)

\begin{split}G^{\pm}(r;\lambda)-G^{\pm}(0;\lambda)&=\log\left(1+\left[\frac{\xi^{1/2}v(\xi)\pm r^{1/2}v(r)}{v(\xi)(\xi^{1/2}\pm r^{1/2})}-1\right]\right)\\
&=\log\left(1\mp\frac{r^{1/2}}{v(\xi)}\frac{v(\xi)-v(r)}{\xi^{1/2}\pm r^{1/2}}\right)\\
&=\log\left(1\mp\frac{\xi^{1/2}}{v(\xi)}r^{1/2}\frac{v(\xi)-v(r)}{\xi-r}+\frac{1}{v(\xi)}r\frac{v(\xi)-v(r)}{\xi-r}\right).\end{split}

This shows that
G^{\pm}(r;\lambda)-G^{\pm}(0;\lambda)
has a convergent power series expansion about
r=0
in integer powers of
r^{1/2}
. If we isolate the leading term proportional to
r^{1/2}
, the remainder will have a second derivative that is absolutely integrable at
r=0
. Therefore, we write

(A.24)

G^{-}(r;\lambda)-G^{+}(r;\lambda)=\frac{2\xi^{1/2}}{v(\xi)}\frac{v(\xi)-v(0)}{\xi}r^{1/2}+R(r;\lambda)

and

(A.25)

\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}|R_{rr}(r;\lambda)|\,\mathrm{d}r=\mathcal{O}\!\left(1\right),\quad\lambda\to 0.

It follows by Riemann sum approximation (cf. (
A.12
) and (
A.20
)) that

(A.26)

\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}[G^{-}(r;\lambda)-G^{+}(r;\lambda)]\,\mathrm{d}r-\epsilon\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}[G^{-}(r_{n};\lambda)-G^{+}(r_{n};\lambda)]\\
\begin{aligned} {}&=\frac{2\xi^{1/2}}{v(\xi)}\frac{v(\xi)-v(0)}{\xi}\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2})\epsilon^{3/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{2}\right)\\
{}&=\mathcal{E}_{0}(\lambda)\epsilon^{3/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{2}\right)\\
\end{aligned}

holds uniformly for
|\lambda|
sufficiently small.
Now we use these observations to rewrite
\log(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda))
as follows (cf. (
A.2
)):

(A.27)

\log(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda))=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}\log\left(\frac{\xi^{1/2}-r^{1/2}}{\xi^{1/2}+r^{1/2}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r-\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\log\left(\frac{\xi^{1/2}-r_{n}^{1/2}}{\xi^{1/2}+r_{n}^{1/2}}\right)\\
{}+\mathcal{E}_{0}(\lambda)\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right),

which holds as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
uniformly for sufficiently small
|\lambda|
.
Note that

(A.28)

\log\left(\frac{\xi^{1/2}-r^{1/2}}{\xi^{1/2}+r^{1/2}}\right)=\log\left(\frac{r^{1/2}-\xi^{1/2}}{r^{1/2}+\xi^{1/2}}\right)+\begin{cases}-\pi\mathrm{i},&0<\arg(\lambda)<\pi/2\;\text{so}\;-\pi<\arg(\xi)<0,\\
\pi\mathrm{i},&\pi/2<\arg(\lambda)<\pi\;\text{so}\;0<\arg(\xi)<\pi.\end{cases}

Therefore, since by (
2.24
) we have
\Phi(0)=N\pi\epsilon
, (
A.27
) can be rewritten in both cases of
0<\arg(\lambda)<\pi/2
and
\pi/2<\arg(\lambda)<\pi
the same way:

(A.29)

\log(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda))=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}\log\left(\frac{r^{1/2}-\xi^{1/2}}{r^{1/2}+\xi^{1/2}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r-\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\log\left(\frac{r_{n}^{1/2}-\xi^{1/2}}{r_{n}^{1/2}+\xi^{1/2}}\right)\\
{}+\mathcal{E}_{0}(\lambda)\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right).

Observing that adding
2\xi^{1/2}r^{-1/2}
to the integrand results in absolute convergence both at
r=0
and
r=\infty
,

(A.30)

\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}\log\left(\frac{r^{1/2}-\xi^{1/2}}{r^{1/2}+\xi^{1/2}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r\\
\begin{aligned} &=-\frac{2\xi^{1/2}}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r^{1/2}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[\log\left(\frac{r^{1/2}-\xi^{1/2}}{r^{1/2}+\xi^{1/2}}\right)+\frac{2\xi^{1/2}}{r^{1/2}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}r\\
&\quad\quad\quad{}-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\Phi(0)/\pi}^{\infty}\left[\log\left(\frac{r^{1/2}-\xi^{1/2}}{r^{1/2}+\xi^{1/2}}\right)+\frac{2\xi^{1/2}}{r^{1/2}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}r\\
&=-\frac{4\Phi(0)^{1/2}\xi^{1/2}}{\pi^{1/2}\epsilon}+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[\log\left(\frac{\tau^{1/2}+\mathrm{i}Z}{\tau^{1/2}-\mathrm{i}Z}\right)-\frac{2\mathrm{i}Z}{\tau^{1/2}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}\tau\\
&\quad\quad\quad{}-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\Phi(0)/\pi}^{\infty}\left[\log\left(\frac{r^{1/2}-\xi^{1/2}}{r^{1/2}+\xi^{1/2}}\right)+\frac{2\xi^{1/2}}{r^{1/2}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}r,\end{aligned}

where in the integral over
r\in(0,\infty)
we have rescaled by
r=\epsilon\tau
and we set
Z:=\mathrm{i}\xi^{1/2}/\epsilon^{1/2}=\varphi_{0}(\lambda)/\epsilon^{1/2}
. Similarly, recalling
r_{n}:=\epsilon(n+\tfrac{1}{2})
,

(A.31)

\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\log\left(\frac{r_{n}^{1/2}-\xi^{1/2}}{r_{n}^{1/2}+\xi^{1/2}}\right)=-\frac{2\xi^{1/2}}{\epsilon^{1/2}}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left[\log\left(\frac{\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}+\mathrm{i}Z}{\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}-\mathrm{i}Z}\right)-\frac{2\mathrm{i}Z}{\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}}\right]\\
{}-\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\left[\log\left(\frac{r_{n}^{1/2}-\xi^{1/2}}{r_{n}^{1/2}+\xi^{1/2}}\right)+\frac{2\xi^{1/2}}{r_{n}^{1/2}}\right],

so using

(A.32)

\begin{split}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}}&=\int_{0}^{N}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\sqrt{x}}+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}}-\int_{n}^{n+1}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\sqrt{x}}\right]\\
&=2\sqrt{N}+\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}}-2\sqrt{n+1}+2\sqrt{n}\right]\\
&=2\sqrt{N}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}}-2\sqrt{n+1}+2\sqrt{n}\right]+\mathcal{O}\!\left(N^{-3/2}\right)\end{split}

with
\Phi(0)=N\pi\epsilon
and
Z=\mathrm{i}\xi^{1/2}/\epsilon^{1/2}
we have that

(A.33)

\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\log\left(\frac{r_{n}^{1/2}-\xi^{1/2}}{r_{n}^{1/2}+\xi^{1/2}}\right)=-\frac{4\Phi(0)^{1/2}\xi^{1/2}}{\pi^{1/2}\epsilon}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left[\log\left(\frac{\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}+\mathrm{i}Z}{\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}-\mathrm{i}Z}\right)-4\mathrm{i}Z(\sqrt{n+1}-\sqrt{n})\right]\\
{}-\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\left[\log\left(\frac{r_{n}^{1/2}-\xi^{1/2}}{r_{n}^{1/2}+\xi^{1/2}}\right)+\frac{2\xi^{1/2}}{r_{n}^{1/2}}\right]+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)

holds in the limit
\epsilon\downarrow 0
uniformly for bounded
\xi
. Therefore, defining
k(Z):=k_{1}(Z)+k_{2}(Z)
, where

(A.34)

\begin{split}k_{1}(Z)&:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[\log\left(\frac{\tau^{1/2}+\mathrm{i}Z}{\tau^{1/2}-\mathrm{i}Z}\right)-\frac{2\mathrm{i}Z}{\tau^{1/2}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}\tau,\\
k_{2}(Z)&:=-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left[\log\left(\frac{\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}+\mathrm{i}Z}{\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}-\mathrm{i}Z}\right)-4\mathrm{i}Z(\sqrt{n+1}-\sqrt{n})\right],\end{split}

we have

(A.35)

\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}\log\left(\frac{r^{1/2}-\xi^{1/2}}{r^{1/2}+\xi^{1/2}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r-\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\log\left(\frac{r_{n}^{1/2}-\xi^{1/2}}{r_{n}^{1/2}+\xi^{1/2}}\right)=k(Z)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\\
{}-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\Phi(0)/\pi}^{\infty}\left[\log\left(\frac{r^{1/2}-\xi^{1/2}}{r^{1/2}+\xi^{1/2}}\right)+\frac{2\xi^{1/2}}{r^{1/2}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}r+\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\left[\log\left(\frac{r_{n}^{1/2}-\xi^{1/2}}{r_{n}^{1/2}+\xi^{1/2}}\right)+\frac{2\xi^{1/2}}{r_{n}^{1/2}}\right].

Assuming only that
\xi
is sufficiently small independent of
\epsilon
, the terms on the second line are
\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)
by Riemann sum approximation (cf. (
A.12
)) because the integrand has a second derivative that is absolutely integrable on
(\Phi(0)/\pi,\infty)
. Combining this result with (
A.29
) and exponentiating yields

(A.36)

Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=\mathrm{e}^{k(Z)}\left(1+\mathcal{E}_{0}(\lambda)\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right),\quad\epsilon\downarrow 0,\quad Z:=\mathrm{i}\frac{\xi^{1/2}}{\epsilon^{1/2}}=\frac{\varphi_{0}(\lambda)}{\epsilon^{1/2}},

holding only under the assumption that
|\lambda|
(and hence also
|\xi|
) is sufficiently small.
Note that
\mathcal{E}_{0}(\lambda)
is analytic in
\lambda
at
\lambda=0
and vanishes to first order at the origin (in fact, it is an odd function of
\lambda
), because
v
is analytic and nonvanishing.

Finally, note that
k_{1}(0)=0
and, differentiating under the integral sign,

(A.37)

k_{1}^{\prime}(Z)=-2\mathrm{i}Z^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{(\tau+Z^{2})\sqrt{\tau}}=-2\mathrm{i}Z^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\mathrm{d}w}{w^{2}+Z^{2}}=-2\pi\mathrm{i}Z\,\mathrm{sgn}(\re\{Z\}).

Therefore
k_{1}(Z)=-\pi\mathrm{i}Z^{2}\,\mathrm{sgn}(\re\{Z\})
.
The proof is complete upon identifying
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)
with
\mathrm{e}^{k(Z)}
and observing that
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)
take the common value of
1
in the coincident limits
\lambda\to 0
and
Z\to 0
respectively.
∎
