Proof.

Using
the representation
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)=\mathrm{e}^{k_{1}(Z)}\mathrm{e}^{k_{2}(Z)}=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\pi Z^{2}\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{Re}\{Z\})}\mathrm{e}^{k_{2}(Z)}
, to obtain the claimed behavior as
Z\to 0
, it only remains to expand the function
k_{2}(Z)=-k_{2}(-Z)
analytic at
Z=0
in a Taylor series. In particular,
by differentiation under the sum in (
A.34
),

(A.38)

k_{2}^{\prime}(0)=-2\mathrm{i}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}}-2(\sqrt{n+1}-\sqrt{n})\right]=-2\mathrm{i}\lim_{N\to\infty}\left[\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n+\frac{1}{2}}}-2\sqrt{N}\right],

and then a computation along the lines of (
A.14
)–(
A.19
) gives
k_{2}^{\prime}(0)=-2\mathrm{i}(\sqrt{2}-1)\zeta(\tfrac{1}{2})
.

To prove the large-
Z
asymptotic behavior, let
\gamma>0
be fixed, and suppose that
\epsilon^{1/2}|Z|\leq\gamma
. Then Proposition
7
implies that

(A.39)

\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)=Y_{\epsilon}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(\epsilon^{1/2}Z))\left(1-\mathcal{E}_{0}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(\epsilon^{1/2}Z))\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right),\quad\epsilon\to 0,

where
\varphi_{0}^{-1}
denotes the inverse conformal mapping to
\varphi_{0}
. If we also assume that
\frac{1}{2}\gamma\leq\epsilon^{1/2}|Z|
and that
Z
satisfies the indicated sectorial condition, then Proposition
6
applies to
Y_{\epsilon}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(\epsilon^{1/2}Z))
and we obtain

(A.40)

\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)=\left(1-\frac{2\mathrm{i}v_{0}}{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(\epsilon^{1/2}Z)}\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2})\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right)\\
{}\cdot\left(1-\mathcal{E}_{0}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(\epsilon^{1/2}Z))\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right),\quad\epsilon\to 0.

By the inequality
\epsilon^{1/2}|Z|\leq\gamma
we can clearly write the right-hand side in the form
1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(Z^{-1}\right)
, and together with the inequality
\frac{1}{2}\gamma\leq\epsilon^{1/2}|Z|
we obtain that
\epsilon\to 0
if and only if
|Z|\to\infty
.
∎

See
9

Proof.

Starting
from (
A.2
), we note the only difficulty in applying the sort of Riemann-sum arguments employed in the proof of Proposition
6

arises when
\mathrm{i}s(r)
is close to
\lambda\approx\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}}
, or equivalently, for
r
in a neighborhood of

\Phi(0)/\pi
. We therefore define a cutoff value
r_{c}\approx\Phi(0)/(2\pi)
by

(A.41)

r_{c}:=N_{c}\epsilon,\quad\text{where}\quad N_{c}:=\left[\frac{\Phi(0)}{2\pi\epsilon}\right]=\left[\frac{N}{2}\right]\quad([\cdot]=\text{integer part}),

and use it to rewrite (
A.2
) in the form

(A.42)

\epsilon\log(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda))=\Delta_{\text{down}}(\lambda)+\Delta_{\text{up}}(\lambda),

where

(A.43)

\Delta_{\text{down}}(\lambda):=\int_{0}^{r_{c}}\log\left(\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}s(r)}{\lambda+\mathrm{i}s(r)}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r-\epsilon\sum_{n=0}^{N_{c}-1}\log\left(\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}s(r_{n})}{\lambda+\mathrm{i}s(r_{n})}\right).

Recalling the definition (
A.4
) of
F(r;\lambda)
,
we can rewrite this as

(A.44)

\Delta_{\text{down}}(\lambda)=\frac{\kappa_{0}v_{0}}{\lambda}\left[\int_{0}^{r_{c}}r^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}r-\epsilon\sum_{n=0}^{N_{c}-1}r_{n}^{1/2}\right]+\left[\int_{0}^{r_{c}}F(r;\lambda)\,\mathrm{d}r-\epsilon\sum_{n=0}^{N_{c}-1}F(r_{n};\lambda)\right].

Exactly as in
(
A.11
)–(
A.20
), we obtain after recalling
\kappa_{0}=-2\mathrm{i}
(see (
A.3
)) and the definition (
3.31
),

(A.45)

\Delta_{\text{down}}(\lambda)=\mathcal{E}_{1}(\lambda)\epsilon^{3/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{2}\right).

From here we have

(A.46)

\log(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda))=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{r_{c}}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}\log\left(\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}s(r)}{\lambda+\mathrm{i}s(r)}\right)\,\mathrm{d}r-\sum_{n=N_{c}}^{N-1}\log\left(\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}s(r_{n})}{\lambda+\mathrm{i}s(r_{n})}\right)\\
{}+\mathcal{E}_{1}(\lambda)\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right),

where the terms on the first line of the right-hand side are exactly
\Delta_{\text{up}}(\lambda)/\epsilon
.
Next, we note

(A.47)

\log\left(\frac{\lambda-\mathrm{i}s(r)}{\lambda+\mathrm{i}s(r)}\right)=\log(-\mathrm{i}(\lambda-\mathrm{i}s(r)))-\log(-\mathrm{i}(\lambda+\mathrm{i}s(r))),

where all branches are principal. From (
A.12
), we
see

(A.48)

-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{r_{c}}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}\log(-\mathrm{i}(\lambda+\mathrm{i}s(r)))\,\mathrm{d}r+\sum_{n=N_{c}}^{N-1}\log(-\mathrm{i}(\lambda+\mathrm{i}s(r_{n})))=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)

holds uniformly for
\lambda
in a neighborhood of
\mathrm{i}A_{\max}
. Combining the last three
equations gives

(A.49)

\log(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda))=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{r_{c}}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}\log\left(-\mathrm{i}(\lambda-\mathrm{i}s(r))\right)\,\mathrm{d}r-\sum_{n=N_{c}}^{N-1}\log\left(-\mathrm{i}(\lambda-\mathrm{i}s(r_{n}))\right)\\
{}+\mathcal{E}_{1}(\lambda)\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right).

As in the proof of Proposition
7
, let
\xi
be related to
\lambda
by the equivalent relations
\lambda=\mathrm{i}s(\xi)
or
\xi=(\Phi(0)-\Phi(\lambda))/\pi
, implying that the conformal map
\lambda\mapsto\varphi_{1}(\lambda)
can be represented as
\varphi_{1}(\lambda)=\xi-\Phi(0)/\pi
. Therefore,

(A.50)

\log(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda))=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{r_{c}}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}\log\left(s(\xi)-s(r)\right)\,\mathrm{d}r-\sum_{n=N_{c}}^{N-1}\log\left(s(\xi)-s(r_{n})\right)\\
{}+\mathcal{E}_{1}(\lambda)\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right).

Analogous to the functions
G^{\pm}(r;\xi)
defined in (
A.21
), we
introduce

(A.51)

H(r;\xi):=\log(\xi-r)-\log(s(\xi)-s(r)).

As
H(r;\xi)
is analytic in
r
for
\xi
near
\Phi(0)/\pi
due to the assumption that
A^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})<0
,
applying (
A.12
) again gives

(A.52)

\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{r_{c}}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}H(r;\xi)\,\mathrm{d}r-\sum_{n=N_{c}}^{N-1}H(r_{n};\xi)=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right).

This allows us to replace
s(\cdot)
with the identity with no additional error:

(A.53)

\log(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda))=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{r_{c}}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}\log\left(\xi-r\right)\,\mathrm{d}r-\sum_{n=N_{c}}^{N-1}\log\left(\xi-r_{n}\right)+\mathcal{E}_{1}(\lambda)\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right).

Elementary calculations give

(A.54)

\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{r_{c}}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}\log\left(\xi-r\right)\,\mathrm{d}r\\
=N_{c}-N+(W+N-N_{c})\log(W+N-N_{c})-W\log(W)+(N-N_{c})\log(\epsilon),

where
W=(\xi-\Phi(0)/\pi)/\epsilon=\varphi_{1}(\lambda)/\epsilon
, and

(A.55)

-\sum_{n=N_{c}}^{N-1}\log\left(\xi-r_{n}\right)=-\sum_{m=0}^{N-N_{c}-1}\log\left(W+\frac{1}{2}+m\right)-(N-N_{c})\log(\epsilon).

Therefore

(A.56)

\log(Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda))=N_{c}-N+(W+N-N_{c})\log(W+N-N_{c})\\
{}-W\log(W)-\sum_{m=0}^{N-N_{c}-1}\log\left(W+\frac{1}{2}+m\right)+\mathcal{E}_{1}(\lambda)\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right),

and so

(A.57)

\begin{split}Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)&=\mathrm{e}^{N_{c}-N}(W+N-N_{c})^{W-N-N_{c}}W^{-W}\prod_{m=0}^{N-N_{c}-1}\frac{1}{W+m+\frac{1}{2}}\left(1+\mathcal{E}_{1}(\lambda)\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right)\\
&=\mathrm{e}^{N_{c}-N}(W+N-N_{c})^{W-N-N_{c}}W^{-W}\prod_{m=0}^{N-N_{c}-1}\frac{\Gamma(W+m+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(W+m+\frac{3}{2})}\left(1+\mathcal{E}_{1}(\lambda)\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right)\\
&=\mathrm{e}^{N_{c}-N}(W+N-N_{c})^{W-N-N_{c}}W^{-W}\frac{\Gamma(W+\frac{1}{2})}{\Gamma(W+N-N_{c}+\frac{1}{2})}\left(1+\mathcal{E}_{1}(\lambda)\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right).\end{split}

Stirling’s approximation of the gamma function
[
22
, Eq. 5.11.3]
gives

(A.58)

\frac{1}{\Gamma(W+N-N_{c}+\frac{1}{2})}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left(W+N-N_{c}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{-(W+N-N_{c})}\exp\left(W+N-N_{c}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\\
\times\left(1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{1}{W+N-N_{c}+\frac{1}{2}}\right)\right),

provided that
W+N-N_{c}+\tfrac{1}{2}
lies outside a thin sector centered on the negative imaginary axis. Recalling the definition (
A.41
) of
N_{c}
as well as the identity
\Phi(0)=N\pi\epsilon
, we see that the condition that
|\lambda-\mathrm{i}A_{\mathrm{max}}|
is sufficiently small guarantees that
|\epsilon W|\leq\delta
holds for some
\delta>0
small, which in turn implies both the desired sectorial condition as well as the estimate

(W+N-N_{c}+\frac{1}{2})^{-1}=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)
. Therefore,

(A.59)

Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\left(\frac{W+N-N_{c}}{W+N-N_{c}+\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{W-N-N_{c}}W^{-W}\Gamma(W+\tfrac{1}{2})\mathrm{e}^{W+\frac{1}{2}}\\
{}\cdot\left(1+\mathcal{E}_{1}(\lambda)\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right).

Finally, using

(A.60)

\left(\frac{n}{n+\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{n}=\left(1+\frac{1/2}{n}\right)^{-n}=\mathrm{e}^{-1/2}\left(1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(n^{-1}\right)\right)

with
n=W+N-N_{c}
gives (
3.29
) and completes the proof.
∎

See
10

Proof.

We begin
by suitably modifying the analysis of
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
as in the proof of Proposition
6
. We write
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=Y_{\epsilon}^{+}(\lambda)/Y_{\epsilon}^{-}(\lambda)
, where

(A.61)

Y_{\epsilon}^{\pm}(\lambda):=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}\log(-\mathrm{i}(\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}s(r)))\,\mathrm{d}r-\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\log(-\mathrm{i}(\lambda\mp\mathrm{i}s(r_{n})))\right).

We apply Riemann-sum arguments to
\epsilon\log(Y_{\epsilon}^{-}(\lambda))
by defining

(A.62)

F^{-}(r;\lambda):=\log(-\mathrm{i}(\lambda+\mathrm{i}s(r)))-\frac{\mathrm{i}v_{0}r^{1/2}}{\lambda}.

By direct calculation using (
3.18
) with
v
analytic on
[0,\Phi(0)/\pi]
,

(A.63)

\begin{split}-4\lambda^{-1}(\mathrm{i}s(r)+\lambda)^{2}F_{rr}^{-}(r;\lambda)&=\mathrm{i}r^{-3/2}(v(r)-v_{0})-2\lambda^{-1}v(r)r^{-1}(v(r)-v_{0})-4\mathrm{i}r^{-1/2}v^{\prime}(r)\\
&\quad{}-4\mathrm{i}r^{1/2}v^{\prime\prime}(r)+4\lambda^{-1}r(v(r)v^{\prime\prime}(r)-v^{\prime}(r)^{2})+\mathrm{i}\lambda^{-2}r^{-1/2}v_{0}v(r)^{2}\\
&=\mathcal{O}\!\left(r^{-1/2}\right)\end{split}

holds uniformly for
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\sigma}
(which in particular bounds
\lambda
away from zero) and
0<r<\Phi(0)/\pi
. Since
\mathrm{i}s(r)+\lambda
is bounded away from zero for
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\sigma}
and
0<r<\Phi(0)/\pi
, therefore
also
F_{rr}^{-}(r;\lambda)=\mathcal{O}\!\left(r^{-1/2}\right)
, and so

(A.64)

\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}|F_{rr}^{-}(r;\lambda)|\,\mathrm{d}r=\mathcal{O}\!\left(1\right)

holds uniformly for
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\sigma}
.
Therefore, applying (
A.12
) gives

(A.65)

\epsilon\log(Y_{\epsilon}^{-}(\lambda))=\frac{\mathrm{i}v_{0}}{\lambda}\left[\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}r^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}r-\epsilon\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}r_{n}^{1/2}\right]+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{2}\right)

as
\epsilon\downarrow 0

uniformly for
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\sigma}
.
Then using (
A.20
), dividing by
\epsilon
and exponentiating, we get

(A.66)

Y_{\epsilon}^{-}(\lambda)=1+\frac{\mathrm{i}v_{0}}{\lambda}\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2})\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right).

To analyze
Y_{\epsilon}^{+}(\lambda)
, we introduce the function

(A.67)

F^{+}(r;\lambda):=\log(-\mathrm{i}(\lambda-\mathrm{i}s(r)))-\log(\Phi(0)-\Phi(\lambda)-\pi r)+\frac{\mathrm{i}v_{0}r^{1/2}}{\lambda}.

Here the purpose of the second term is to compensate for the singularity of the first term that will occur should it be the case that
-\mathrm{i}\lambda\in(0,A_{\mathrm{max}})
. By direct calculation, we have

(A.68)

F^{+}_{rr}(r;\lambda)=\frac{s(r)s^{\prime\prime}(r)-s(\xi)s^{\prime\prime}(r)-s^{\prime}(r)^{2}}{(s(r)-s(\xi))^{2}}+\frac{1}{(r-\xi)^{2}}-\frac{v_{0}}{4s(\xi)r^{3/2}},

where for
\lambda\in\Lambda
we determine a unique value
\xi
by the relations
\lambda=\mathrm{i}s(\xi)
or
\xi=(\Phi(0)-\Phi(\lambda))/\pi
. It is straightforward to check by Taylor expansion of
s(r)
about
r=\xi
that this function has a removable singularity at
r=\xi
. It is also easy to check that for each
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\sigma}
,
r^{1/2}F_{rr}^{+}(r;\lambda)
is an analytic function of
r
for
0<r\leq\Phi(0)/\pi
that has a finite
limit as
r\downarrow 0
.
Moreover
r^{1/2}F_{rr}^{+}(r;\lambda)
is uniformly bounded for
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\sigma}
and
0<r<\Phi(0)/\pi
, and consequently

(A.69)

\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}|F_{rr}^{+}(r;\lambda)|\,\mathrm{d}r=\mathcal{O}\!\left(1\right)\quad\text{uniformly for $\lambda\in\Lambda_{\sigma}$}.

Applying (
A.12
) then gives

(A.70)

\epsilon\log(Y_{\epsilon}^{+}(\lambda))=\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}\log(\Phi(0)-\Phi(\lambda)-\pi r)\,\mathrm{d}r-\epsilon\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\log(\Phi(0)-\Phi(\lambda)-\pi r_{n})\\
-\frac{\mathrm{i}v_{0}}{\lambda}\left[\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}r^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}r-\epsilon\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}r_{n}^{1/2}\right]+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{2}\right).

Using (
A.20
) we may write this in the form

(A.71)

\epsilon\log(Y_{\epsilon}^{+}(\lambda))=\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}\log(\Phi(0)-\Phi(\lambda)-\pi r)\,\mathrm{d}r-\epsilon\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\log(\Phi(0)-\Phi(\lambda)-\pi r_{n})\\
-\frac{\mathrm{i}v_{0}}{\lambda}\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2})\epsilon^{3/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{2}\right).

Therefore,

(A.72)

Y_{\epsilon}^{+}(\lambda)=\frac{\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}\log(\Phi(0)-\Phi(\lambda)-\pi r)\,\mathrm{d}r\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{n=0}^{N-1}(\Phi(0)-\Phi(\lambda)-\pi r_{n})}\left(1-\frac{\mathrm{i}v_{0}}{\lambda}\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2})\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right)

holds in the limit
\epsilon\downarrow 0
uniformly for
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\sigma}
.

Combining (
A.66
) and (
A.72
) with
Y_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=Y_{\epsilon}^{+}(\lambda)/Y_{\epsilon}^{-}(\lambda)
and (
3.16
) shows that

(A.73)

T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)=\widetilde{T}_{\epsilon}(\Phi(\lambda))\left(1-\frac{2\mathrm{i}v_{0}}{\lambda}\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2})\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right),\quad\epsilon\downarrow 0

holds uniformly for
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\sigma}
, where, using also
r_{n}=\epsilon(n+\tfrac{1}{2})
,

(A.74)

\widetilde{T}_{\epsilon}(\Phi):=2\cos(\Phi/\epsilon)\mathrm{e}^{\pm\mathrm{i}\Phi/\epsilon}\frac{\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\Phi(0)/\pi}\log(\Phi(0)-\Phi-\pi r)\,\mathrm{d}r\right)}{\displaystyle\prod_{n=0}^{N-1}(\Phi(0)-\Phi-\pi\epsilon(n+\tfrac{1}{2}))},\quad\pm\imag\{\Phi\}>0.

Evaluating the integral explicitly, using the identities
\Gamma(z+1)=z\Gamma(z)
and
\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{2}-w)\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{2}+w)\cos(\pi w)=\pi
(cf.
[
22
, Eq. 5.5.3]
) with
N=\Phi(0)/(\pi\epsilon)
, and also taking into account that
\log(-\Phi)=\log(\Phi)\mp\mathrm{i}\pi
for
\pm\imag\{\Phi\}>0
gives the explicit formula

(A.75)

\widetilde{T}_{\epsilon}(\Phi)=\frac{\displaystyle 2\pi\left(\frac{\Phi}{\pi\epsilon}\right)^{\Phi/(\pi\epsilon)}\mathrm{e}^{-\Phi/(\pi\epsilon)}\left(\frac{\Phi(0)-\Phi}{\pi\epsilon}\right)^{(\Phi(0)-\Phi)/(\pi\epsilon)}\mathrm{e}^{-(\Phi(0)-\Phi)/(\pi\epsilon)}}{\displaystyle\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{2}+\Phi/(\pi\epsilon))\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{2}+(\Phi(0)-\Phi)/(\pi\epsilon))}.

It then follows from Stirling’s formula (cf.
[
22
, Eq. 5.11.3]
) that for
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\sigma}
,

(A.76)

\begin{split}\widetilde{T}_{\epsilon}(\Phi(\lambda))&=1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\Phi(\lambda)^{-1}\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon(\Phi(0)-\Phi(\lambda))^{-1}\right)\\
&=1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right),\end{split}

where to get the second line we use the fact that

\Phi(\lambda)
and
\Phi(0)-\Phi(\lambda)
are both bounded away from zero for
\lambda\in\Lambda_{\sigma}
.
Combining this result with (
A.73
) then proves the proposition.
∎

See
11

Proof.

Noting
that
\re\{\varphi_{0}(\lambda)\}
has the same sign as
\re\{\lambda\}
,
combining (
3.16
) with Proposition
7
and using
\Phi(0)=N\pi\epsilon
for
N\in\mathbb{Z}

yields (
3.33
) with

(A.77)

\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)=2\mathcal{Y}_{0}(Z)\cos(\pi Z^{2})\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\pi Z^{2}\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{Re}\{Z\})}=2\cos(\pi Z^{2})\prod_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}-\mathrm{i}Z}{\sqrt{n+\tfrac{1}{2}}+\mathrm{i}Z}\mathrm{e}^{4\mathrm{i}Z(\sqrt{n+1}-\sqrt{n})}.

Then, to obtain (
3.34
) one simply uses the infinite product expansion of
\cos(\pi Z^{2})
, cf.
[
22
, Eq. 4.22.2]
. It is straightforward to check that
T_{\epsilon}(\lambda)
and
\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)
take the common value of
2
in the coincident limits
\lambda\to 0
and
Z\to 0
respectively.
∎

See
12

Proof.

We
adapt the proof of Proposition
8
. To consider
Z
small, we get from the first equality in (
A.77
) that
\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)=2\mathrm{e}^{k_{2}(Z)}\cos(\pi Z^{2})
where
k_{2}(Z)
is the odd function analytic at the origin defined by (
A.34
). In the proof of Proposition
8
it was shown that
k_{2}^{\prime}(0)=-2\mathrm{i}(\sqrt{2}-1)\zeta(\tfrac{1}{2})
. This proves the claimed behavior of
\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)
near
Z=0
.

For the behavior as
Z\to\infty
, let
\gamma>0
be fixed and assume that
\epsilon^{1/2}|Z|\leq\gamma
. Then by Proposition
11

(A.78)

\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)=T_{\epsilon}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(\epsilon^{1/2}Z))\left(1-\mathcal{E}_{0}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(\epsilon^{1/2}Z))\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right),\quad\epsilon\to 0,

where
\varphi_{0}^{-1}
is the inverse of the conformal map
\varphi_{0}
. Assuming also that
\tfrac{1}{2}\gamma\leq\epsilon^{1/2}|Z|
and applying Proposition
10
to
T_{\epsilon}(\varphi^{-1}(\epsilon^{1/2}Z))
gives

(A.79)

\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)=\left(1-\frac{2\mathrm{i}v_{0}}{\varphi_{0}^{-1}(\epsilon^{1/2}Z)}\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\zeta(-\tfrac{1}{2})\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right)\\
{}\cdot\left(1-\mathcal{E}_{0}(\varphi_{0}^{-1}(\epsilon^{1/2}Z))\epsilon^{1/2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon\right)\right),\quad\epsilon\to 0.

Then using the inequalities
\tfrac{1}{2}\gamma\leq\epsilon^{1/2}|Z|\leq\gamma
as in the proof of Proposition
8
gives
\mathcal{T}_{0}(Z)=1+\mathcal{O}\!\left(Z^{-1}\right)
as
Z\to\infty
in the indicated sector.
∎

See
13

Proof.

Combining
(
3.16
) with Proposition
3.31
, taking into account that
\pm\re\{\lambda\}>0
corresponds to
\mp\imag\{\varphi_{1}(\lambda)\}>0
yields (
3.35
) with

(A.80)

\mathcal{T}_{1}(W)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\cos(\pi W)\Gamma(W+\tfrac{1}{2})e^{W}W^{-W}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\pi W\mathrm{sgn}(\imag\{W\})}.

But in terms of principal branches,
W^{-W}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\pi W\mathrm{sgn}(\imag\{W\})}=(-W)^{-W}
, so using
\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{2}-W)\Gamma(\tfrac{1}{2}+W)\cos(\pi W)=\pi
(cf.
[
22
, Eq. 5.5.3]
) the formula (
3.36
) follows.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs of the properties of
g(\lambda;x)
and
h(\lambda;x)

See
14

Proof.

To prove
G1, the analyticity of
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
in the indicated domain is obvious from the definition (
4.7
); the same formula shows that
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
is uniformly bounded at least for
\lambda
bounded away from the branch cut
-A(x)\leq-\mathrm{i}\lambda\leq A(x)
. On the other hand, using (
4.10
) shows that
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
is continuous up to its branch cut, so G1 is established.

To prove G2, note that oddness of
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
is obvious from the first line of (
4.7
) given that
R(\lambda;x)
is an odd function of
\lambda
. The Schwarz symmetry property G3 also follows immediately from (
4.7
) given that
R(\lambda^{*};x)=R(\lambda;x)^{*}
.

Property G4 is a direct consequence of the formula (
4.10
), upon taking into account (
4.8
) and
the fact that
R(\lambda;x)
changes sign across the branch cut. Combining G1 and G2 proves that
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)=O(\lambda^{-1})
as
\lambda\to\infty
, i.e., property G5.

To prove property G6, we take
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
in the form (
4.10
) to allow
\lambda
near
\mathrm{i}A(x)
and identify
G_{1}^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
with
\phi^{\pm}(-\mathrm{i}\lambda;x)/(4\mathrm{i}\lambda)
analytic at
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A(x)
. Since the loop integral over
L
in (
4.10
) is analytic near
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A(x)
and since
R(\lambda;x)
vanishes to order
1/2
at
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A(x)
, it remains to show that the integral vanishes for
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A(x)
under the condition
\pm(x-x_{0})>0
(and
x\in(X_{-},X_{+})
; otherwise
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)=0
and the result holds trivially). That is, we need to show that
\pm(x-x_{0})>0
and
x\in(X_{-},X_{+})
implies
M_{0}^{\pm}(x)=0
, where

(B.1)

M_{0}^{\pm}(x):=\oint_{L}\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{R(\mathrm{i}s;x)(s^{2}-A(x)^{2})}=-\oint_{L}\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{R(\mathrm{i}s;x)^{3}}.

Recall
L
is positively oriented and surrounds the branch cut of
R
.
Noting the identity

(B.2)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\frac{1}{R(\mathrm{i}s;x)}=\frac{s}{R(\mathrm{i}s;x)^{3}},

we integrate by parts to obtain

(B.3)

M_{0}^{\pm}(x)=\oint_{L}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\left(\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)}{s}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{R(\mathrm{i}s;x)}.

The integrand is now integrable at
s=\pm A(x)
, so the loop
L
can be contracted to the interval
[-A(x),A(x)]
. Using also that
\phi^{\pm}(s;x)
is an even function of
s
yields

(B.4)

M_{0}^{\pm}(x)=2\int_{-A(x)}^{A(x)}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\left(\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)}{s}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}}=4\int_{0}^{A(x)}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\left(\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)}{s}\right)\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}},

so from (
4.8
) we get

(B.5)

M_{0}^{\pm}(x)=8\int_{0}^{A(x)}\frac{2x-\mathrm{i}\Xi^{\prime}(\mathrm{i}s)\mp\mathrm{i}\overline{L}^{\prime}(\mathrm{i}s)}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Then, using (
4.4
),

(B.6)

\begin{split}M_{0}^{+}(x)&=16\int_{0}^{A(x)}\left[x-X_{+}+\int_{x_{+}(s)}^{X_{+}}\frac{s\,\mathrm{d}y}{\sqrt{s^{2}-A(y)^{2}}}\right]\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}}\\
&=16\int_{0}^{A(x)}\left[-\int_{x}^{X_{+}}\,\mathrm{d}y+\int_{x_{+}(s)}^{X_{+}}\frac{s\,\mathrm{d}y}{\sqrt{s^{2}-A(y)^{2}}}\right]\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}}.\end{split}

If
x_{0}<x<X_{+}
, then exchanging the order of integration yields

(B.7)

M_{0}^{+}(x)=16\int_{x}^{X_{+}}\left[\int_{A(y)}^{A(x)}\frac{s\,\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{s^{2}-A(y)^{2}}\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}}-\int_{0}^{A(x)}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}y.

Both of the inner integrals can be computed exactly and they are both equal to
\pi/2
, hence if
x_{0}<x<X_{+}
we deduce that
M_{0}^{+}(x)=0
.
Similarly,

(B.8)

\begin{split}M_{0}^{-}(x)&=16\int_{0}^{A(x)}\left[x-X_{-}-\int_{X_{-}}^{x_{-}(s)}\frac{s\,\mathrm{d}y}{\sqrt{s^{2}-A(y)^{2}}}\right]\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}}\\
&=16\int_{0}^{A(x)}\left[\int_{X_{-}}^{x}\,\mathrm{d}y-\int_{X_{-}}^{x_{-}(s)}\frac{s\,\mathrm{d}y}{\sqrt{s^{2}-A(y)^{2}}}\right]\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}}.\end{split}

Under the assumption that
X_{-}<x<x_{0}
, exchanging the integration order gives

(B.9)

M_{0}^{-}(x)=16\int_{X_{-}}^{x}\left[\int_{0}^{A(x)}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}}-\int_{A(y)}^{A(x)}\frac{s\,\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{s^{2}-A(y)^{2}}\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}}\right]\,\mathrm{d}y

and again the inner integrals cancel, yielding
M_{0}^{-}(x)=0
for
X_{-}<x<x_{0}
.

To prove G7, we may start with (
4.9
) and simply observe that as
x
tends to either support endpoint from within
(X_{-},X_{+})
,
R(\lambda;x)\to\lambda
uniformly for
\lambda
bounded away from the origin. Hence for each given
\lambda\neq 0
we fix an integration contour
L
surrounding the interval
[-A(x),A(x)]
with
\pm\mathrm{i}\lambda
on the exterior, and observe that the integrand in (
4.9
) converges uniformly on
L
to a function analytic on the interior of
L
. Hence the integral converges to zero by Cauchy’s theorem and the prefactor
R(\lambda;x)/(8\pi\mathrm{i})
remains bounded in the limit, which proves that
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)\to 0
.

To establish property G8, we differentiate the formula (
4.11
) with respect to
x
, noting that
\Xi(\mathrm{i}s)\pm\overline{L}(\mathrm{i}s)
is independent of
x
. Thus
g^{\pm}_{x}(\lambda;x)
is a function of
\lambda
analytic in the same domain as
g(\lambda;x)
itself, that is bounded, that satisfies a natural analogue of the Schwarz symmetry property G3, and that satisfies the differentiated boundary condition

(B.10)

g_{x+}^{\pm}(\mathrm{i}s;x)+g_{x-}^{\pm}(\mathrm{i}s;x)=-2s,\quad 0<s<A(x).

Therefore,
g_{x}^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
necessarily has the form

(B.11)

\begin{split}g_{x}^{\pm}(\lambda;x)&=\frac{R(\lambda;x)}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\left[\int_{0}^{A(x)}\frac{2s\,\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}(s+\mathrm{i}\lambda)}+\int_{0}^{A(x)}\frac{2s\,\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}(s-\mathrm{i}\lambda)}\right]\\
&=\frac{R(\lambda;x)}{\pi\mathrm{i}}\int_{-A(x)}^{A(x)}\frac{s^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}(s^{2}+\lambda^{2})}\\
&=\frac{R(\lambda;x)}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{L}\frac{s^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s}{R(\mathrm{i}s;x)(s^{2}+\lambda^{2})},\quad\text{$\mathrm{i}\lambda$ and $-\mathrm{i}\lambda$ exterior to $L$.}\end{split}

Evaluating this latter integral by residues at
s=\pm\mathrm{i}\lambda
and
s=\infty
shows that
g_{x}^{\pm}(\lambda;x)=\mathrm{i}(\lambda-R(\lambda;x))
, giving the claimed result.

Finally, to prove property G9, note that combining properties G7 and G8 gives
(
4.13
).
Since for a semicircular Klaus-Shaw potential with support
[X_{-},X_{+}]
the formulæ(
3.1
)–(
3.2
) together with the definition of
R(\lambda;x)
yield

(B.12)

L(\lambda)=-\mathrm{i}\int_{X_{-}}^{X_{+}}(\lambda-R(\lambda;y))\,\mathrm{d}y,

we obtain the identity (
4.14
).
∎

See
15

Proof.

To prove
property H1,
note that from the second line of (
4.15
),
h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
can be written in the form

(B.13)

h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)=\frac{R(\lambda;x)}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}H^{\pm}(\lambda;x),

where

H^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
is analytic in
\lambda
for
-A_{\mathrm{max}}<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A_{\mathrm{max}}
. Observe that for fixed
x\neq x_{0}
,
H^{\pm}(\mathrm{i}A(x);x)=0
when
\pm(x-x_{0})>0
(by property G6 of Proposition
14
). We now show that

(B.14)

\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial H^{\pm}}{\partial\lambda}(\lambda;x)>0,\quad\pm(x-x_{0})>0,\quad 0<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A_{\mathrm{max}},

that is,
H^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
is real and strictly increasing upwards along the imaginary
\lambda
-axis provided
\pm(x-x_{0})>0
. In particular, the root of
H^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
at
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A(x)
is a simple zero. To prove this, we first obtain a simple formula for
H^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
by integrating with respect to
x
the identity

(B.15)

h^{\pm}_{x}(\lambda;x)=\pm\left(g^{\pm}_{x}(\lambda;x)-\mathrm{i}\lambda\right)=\mp\mathrm{i}R(\lambda;x),

which follows from the first line of (
4.15
) and property G8 of Proposition
14
.
Since we have
H^{\pm}(\lambda;x_{\pm}(-\mathrm{i}\lambda))=0
(as an equivalent way of writing
H^{\pm}(\mathrm{i}A(x);x)=0
for
\pm(x-x_{0})>0
), and also
R(\lambda;x_{\pm}(-\mathrm{i}\lambda))=0
for
0<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A_{\mathrm{max}}
, it follows that
h^{\pm}(\lambda;x_{\pm}(-\mathrm{i}\lambda))=0
for
\pm(x-x_{0})>0
. Therefore, for
x\in[X_{-},X_{+}]
,

(B.16)

h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)=\mp\mathrm{i}\int_{x_{\pm}(-\mathrm{i}\lambda)}^{x}R(\lambda;y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad\pm(x-x_{0})>0,\quad A(x)<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A_{\mathrm{max}},

from which it follows that

(B.17)

H^{\pm}(\lambda;x)=\pm\frac{2\pi}{R(\lambda;x)}\int_{x_{\pm}(-\mathrm{i}\lambda)}^{x}R(\lambda;y)\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad\pm(x-x_{0})>0,\quad A(x)<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A_{\mathrm{max}}.

In these formulæ, the lower limit of integration is a real value between
x_{0}
and
x
under the indicated assumption that
A(x)<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A_{\mathrm{max}}
.
However, for semicircular Klaus-Shaw potentials
A
, the turning points (inverse function branches)
x_{\pm}(s)
are analytic on the interval
0<s<A_{\mathrm{max}}
, and hence the formula (
B.16
) for
h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
can be analytically continued to a domain of the form
\delta<\imag\{\lambda\}<A_{\mathrm{max}}-\delta
and
|\re\{\lambda\}|<\delta
omitting the vertical branch cut connecting
\pm\mathrm{i}A(x)
; it only becomes necessary to replace the real integration with a complex contour connecting
x_{\pm}(-\mathrm{i}\lambda)
with the real value
x
. In the case of the formula (
B.17
), the two boundary values taken on the cut necessarily agree as it has already been shown that
H^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
is analytic at
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A(x)
for
\pm(x-x_{0})>0
, making
H^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
an analytic function of
\lambda
in the domain
\delta<\imag\{\lambda\}<A_{\mathrm{max}}-\delta
and
|\re\{\lambda\}|<\delta
.
Differentiation with respect to
\lambda
using Leibniz’ rule yields

(B.18)

\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial H^{\pm}}{\partial\lambda}(\lambda;x)=\mp\frac{2\pi\mathrm{i}\lambda}{R(\lambda;x)^{3}}\int_{x_{\pm}(-\mathrm{i}\lambda)}^{x}\frac{A(y)^{2}-A(x)^{2}}{R(\lambda;y)}\,\mathrm{d}y,\quad\pm(x-x_{0})>0.

The strict inequality (
B.14
) now follows from (
B.18
). For example, consider the case
x>x_{0}
. If also
A(x)<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A_{\mathrm{max}}
, then
x_{0}<x_{+}(-\mathrm{i}\lambda)<y<x
for the integral in (
B.18
), so
A(y)>A(x)>0
since
A^{\prime}(x)<0
for
x>x_{0}
and also,
\lambda
,
R(\lambda;x)
, and
R(\lambda;y)
are all positive imaginary, confirming (
B.14
). On the other hand, if
0<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A(x)
, then instead
x_{0}<x<y<x_{+}(-\mathrm{i}\lambda)
for the integral in (
B.18
), so
A(x)>A(y)>0
and taking the boundary value from the right half-plane (arbitrarily, since
H^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
has no jump discontinuity on the imaginary axis) we see that
R(\lambda;x)
and
R(\lambda;y)
are positive real while
\lambda
remains positive imaginary, confirming (
B.14
) again. Finally, taking the limit
-\mathrm{i}\lambda\downarrow A(x)
gives

(B.19)

\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial H^{+}}{\partial\lambda}(\mathrm{i}A(x);x)=-\frac{2}{3A^{\prime}(x)}>0,\quad x>x_{0}

confirming (
B.14
) in the (most important for our purposes) boundary case. The argument for
x<x_{0}
is similar. Since
H^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
is analytic in a suitable neighborhood
D(x)
of
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A(x)
at which point it has a simple zero, property H1 is confirmed.

Property H2 also follows from the representation (
B.13
), the fact that
H^{\pm}(\mathrm{i}A(x);x)=0
, and the inequality (
B.14
). These show immediately that
h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
is positive and strictly increasing in the positive imaginary direction along the imaginary axis above
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A(x)
. To obtain the corresponding inequalities on
\re\{h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)\}
, one notes that the boundary values taken by
h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
on the imaginary branch cut below
\lambda=\mathrm{i}A(x)
are themselves purely imaginary and monotone, from which the desired inequalities are consequences of the Cauchy-Riemann equations. Uniformity for
x\in J_{c}^{\pm}
holds by continuity of
h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
as
J_{c}^{\pm}
is a compact subset of
J^{\pm}
.

To prove property H3, note first that the formulæ(
4.13
) following from properties G7 and G8 of Proposition
14
allow us to characterize the difference of boundary values taken by
g^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
when
0<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A(x)
, assuming that
\pm(x-x_{0})>0
. Indeed, if we use the subscript
+
(resp.,
-
) to denote the boundary value taken from the right (resp., left) half-plane, we can derive the following formula:

(B.20)

\begin{split}g^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda;x)-g^{\pm}_{-}(\lambda;x)&=2\mathrm{i}\int_{x}^{x_{\pm}(-\mathrm{i}\lambda)}R_{+}(\lambda;y)\,\mathrm{d}y\\
&=2\mathrm{i}\int_{x}^{x_{\pm}(-\mathrm{i}\lambda)}\sqrt{\lambda^{2}+A(y)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}y,\\
&\qquad\qquad\pm(x-x_{0})>0,\quad 0<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A(x).\end{split}

Comparing with (
2.27
), we see that

(B.21)

2\Phi(\lambda)\mp\left(-\mathrm{i}[g^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda;x)-g^{\pm}_{-}(\lambda;x)]\right)=\pm 2\int_{x_{\mp}(-i\lambda)}^{x}\sqrt{\lambda^{2}+A(y)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}y>0,\\
\pm(x-x_{0})>0,\quad 0<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A(x).

Applying Leibniz’ rule to differentiate this formula gives

(B.22)

\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\left[2\Phi(\lambda)\mp\left(-\mathrm{i}[g^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda;x)-g^{\pm}_{-}(\lambda;x)]\right)\right]=\pm 2\mathrm{i}\lambda\int_{x_{\mp}(-\mathrm{i}\lambda)}^{x}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\sqrt{\lambda^{2}+A(y)^{2}}}<0,\\
\pm(x-x_{0})>0,\quad 0<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A(x),

indicating that the positive quantity in square brackets is strictly decreasing as
-\mathrm{i}\lambda
increases from
0
to
A(x)
. The derivative is strictly negative even in the limit
-\mathrm{i}\lambda\uparrow A(x)
.

Now note that (
4.11
) and the first line of (
4.15
) immediately imply property H6.
In turn, this implies the boundary values taken by
h^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
on the cut are purely imaginary, and (
B.22
) can be equivalently written in two ways:

(B.23)

\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\left[2\Phi(\lambda)-(-2\mathrm{i}h_{+}^{\pm}(\lambda;x))\right]<0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda}\left[2\Phi(\lambda)+(-2\mathrm{i}h_{-}^{\pm}(\lambda;x))\right]<0,\\
\pm(x-x_{0})>0,\quad 0<-\mathrm{i}\lambda<A(x),

with the inequalities being strict even in the limit
-\mathrm{i}\lambda\uparrow A(x)
.
Property H3 then follows from H2 and a Cauchy-Riemann argument applied to (
B.23
). Again, uniformity of the estimates for
x\in J_{c}^{\pm}
follows from continuity.

To prove property H4, firstly note that the analyticity of the boundary values and the fact that they sum to zero both follow immediately from (
B.13
) because
R(\lambda;x)
changes sign across the branch cut. Now by the second line of (
4.15
) it is obvious that
H^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
is an even analytic function of
\lambda
and hence its power series at
\lambda=0
consists of only even powers of
\lambda
. While
R(\lambda;x)
is an odd function of
\lambda
, its boundary value
R_{+}(\lambda;x)
taken from the right half-plane can be written in terms of the principal branch square root as
R_{+}(\lambda;x)=(A(x)^{2}+\lambda^{2})^{1/2}
, and hence is an even analytic function of
\lambda
as well. It remains to calculate the first two terms of the Taylor expansion about
\lambda=0
of
h^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda;x)
. Clearly

(B.24)

R_{+}(\lambda;x)=A(x)+\lambda^{2}/(2A(x))+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{4}\right),\quad\lambda\to 0.

For
H^{\pm}(\lambda;x)
we use the second line of (
4.15
) to get

(B.25)

\begin{split}H^{\pm}(\lambda;x)&=\pm\frac{1}{4}\oint_{L}\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{R(\mathrm{i}s;x)(s^{2}+\lambda^{2})}\\
&=\pm\frac{1}{4}\oint_{L}\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{s^{2}R(\mathrm{i}s;x)}\mp\frac{\lambda^{2}}{4}\oint_{L}\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{s^{4}R(\mathrm{i}s;x)}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{4}\right),\quad\lambda\to 0.\end{split}

Therefore, using (
B.13
),

(B.26)

\begin{split}h_{+}^{\pm}(\lambda;x)&=\pm\frac{A(x)}{8\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{L}\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{s^{2}R(\mathrm{i}s;x)}\\
&\quad\quad{}\pm\frac{\lambda^{2}}{8\pi\mathrm{i}}\left[\frac{1}{2A(x)}\oint_{L}\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{s^{2}R(\mathrm{i}s;x)}-A(x)\oint_{L}\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{s^{4}R(\mathrm{i}s;x)}\right]+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{4}\right)\\
&=\pm\frac{A(x)}{8\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{L}\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{s^{2}R(\mathrm{i}s;x)}\\
&\quad\quad{}\mp\frac{\lambda^{2}}{16\pi\mathrm{i}A(x)}\left[\oint_{L}\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{s^{2}R(\mathrm{i}s;x)}+2\oint_{L}\frac{R(\mathrm{i}s;x)\phi^{\pm}(s;x)}{s^{4}}\,\mathrm{d}s\right]+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{4}\right),\quad\lambda\to 0.\end{split}

Now we recall the definition (
4.8
), into which we may substitute from the right-hand side of (
4.5
). Therefore,

(B.27)

\begin{split}\oint_{L}\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{s^{2}R(\mathrm{i}s;x)}&=4(x-X_{\pm})\oint_{L}\frac{\mathrm{d}s}{R(\mathrm{i}s;x)}-4\oint_{L}\frac{s^{2}}{R(\mathrm{i}s;x)}\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{1-z}\,x^{\prime}(s^{2}z)\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&=8\pi(x-X_{\pm})-8\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{1-z}\int_{-A(x)}^{A(x)}\frac{s^{2}x^{\prime}(s^{2}z)}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}z,\end{split}

a purely real expression in which
x^{\prime}(y)
denotes the derivative of the branch of the inverse function
y=A(x)^{2}
for which
\pm(x-x_{0})>0
. We observe that
\pm x^{\prime}(y)<0
holds strictly for
0\leq y<A_{\mathrm{max}}^{2}
. Similarly,

(B.28)

\oint_{L}\frac{R(\mathrm{i}s;x)\phi^{\pm}(s;x)}{s^{4}}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
\begin{aligned} &=4(x-X_{\pm})\oint\frac{R(\mathrm{i}s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{s^{2}}-4\oint_{L}R(\mathrm{i}s;x)\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{1-z}\,x^{\prime}(s^{2}z)\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&=-8\pi(x-X_{\pm})-8\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{1-z}\int_{-A(x)}^{A(x)}\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}\,x^{\prime}(s^{2}z)\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}z.\end{aligned}

Therefore,

(B.29)

\oint_{L}\frac{\phi^{\pm}(s;x)\,\mathrm{d}s}{s^{2}R(\mathrm{i}s;x)}+2\oint_{L}\frac{R(\mathrm{i}s;x)\phi^{\pm}(s;x)}{s^{4}}\,\mathrm{d}s=\\
-8\pi(x-X_{\pm})-8\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{1-z}\int_{-A(x)}^{A(x)}\left[\frac{s^{2}}{\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}}+2\sqrt{A(x)^{2}-s^{2}}\right]x^{\prime}(s^{2}z)\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}z,

an expression in which both terms are real and nonzero and have exactly the same sign, namely that of
x-x_{0}
. Therefore property H4 holds pointwise for
x\in J_{c}^{\pm}
, and the uniformity of the inequality
\beta^{\pm}(x)>0
follows by continuity.

Finally, all of the statements in property H5 follow from H4, with the exception of the inequality
\beta^{\pm}(x)-\Phi_{1}<0
. To prove this, first note that the opposite inequality
\beta^{\pm}(x)-\Phi_{1}>0
would be in contradiction with (
B.23
) taken in a neighborhood of
\lambda=0
on the positive imaginary axis. Therefore
\beta^{\pm}(x)-\Phi_{1}\leq 0
and it remains to rule out the possibility of zeros. For this purpose, it is sufficient to show that
\beta^{\pm}(x)-\Phi_{1}
is monotone for
\pm(x-x_{0})>0
. Using (
B.15
)
and (
B.24
) we get

(B.30)

\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[h^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda;x)-\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda)\right]=\frac{\partial h^{\pm}_{+}}{\partial x}(\lambda;x)=\mp\mathrm{i}R_{+}(\lambda;x)=\mp\mathrm{i}A(x)\mp\frac{\mathrm{i}\lambda^{2}}{2A(x)}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{4}\right),\quad\lambda\to 0.

Furthermore,

(B.31)

\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[h^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda;x)-\mathrm{i}\Phi(\lambda)\right]=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[\mathrm{i}(\alpha^{\pm}(x)-\Phi_{0})+\mathrm{i}(\beta^{\pm}(x)-\Phi_{1})\lambda^{2}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\lambda^{4}\right)\right],

from which we deduce that
(\beta^{\pm}(x)-\Phi_{1})_{x}=\mp 1/(2A(x))\neq 0
, and the proof is complete. The pointwise strict inequality
\beta^{\pm}(x)-\Phi_{1}<0
is uniform for
x
in the compact set
J_{c}^{\pm}
by continuity.
∎
